**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous mathematical framework to find the measurement that extracts the maximum classical information from a quantum state ensemble. It introduces the 'posterior algebra' and proves that optimal measurements correspond to this algebra being commutative. The work successfully proves Shor's conjecture for binary ensembles and provides a highly robust, certified receiver design method.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep structural understanding of measurement optimization in quantum information, linking it to algebraic properties (commutativity) which is a powerful theoretical tool applicable across quantum physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is determining the optimal quantum measurement (POVM) that maximizes the mutual information extracted from a given ensemble of quantum states.

**Main result.** The authors prove Shor's finite-dimensional binary orthogonal-measurement conjecture and establish that the optimal measurement structure is governed by the commutativity of a newly defined 'posterior algebra'.

**Method.** They introduce the posterior algebra and an affine information bound, showing that the maximum accessible information equals this bound if and only if the posterior algebra is abelian.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on a finite ensemble $E = \{(p_i, ho_i)\}_{i=1}^m$ of quantum states, and the measurement is described by a POVM $\{M_y\}_y$. The framework is general but has specific proofs for binary ensembles.

**Key observables.** Accessible Information $I(E, M)$, the affine information bound $U_{	ext{aff}}$, and the information regret $\epsilon_M = U_{	ext{aff}} - I(E, M)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ensemble must be 'faithful' (full rank $	au$), and the analysis is particularly detailed for binary ensembles.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework relies on the ensemble being faithful; the proof for Shor's conjecture is restricted to the binary case.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the performance of the proposed posterior-spectral receiver against other methods across 408 mixed-state instances, showing superior success rates and performance.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the information maximization problem to introducing the posterior algebra, proving the equivalence between information optimality and algebra commutativity, and finally developing a robust, certified posterior-spectral receiver.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Which quantum measurement extracts the most classical information from an ensemble? We introduce the posterior algebra, a new canonical operator algebra selected by mutual information. For faithful ensembles, an affine information bound is exact precisely when this algebra is commutative; its joint spectral measurement is then optimal, and every optimal finite POVM refines it. Binary ensembles have one generator; compactness covers singular states, giving a proof of Shor's finite-dimensional binary orthogonal-measurement conjecture. The framework also gives rigidity bounds and a certified posterior-spectral receiver, validated on 408 mixed-state instances.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a systematic, whole-system numerical framework to optimize the complex cryogenic wiring required for scaling superconducting quantum processors. By modeling heat loads and noise propagation across multiple temperature stages, the authors demonstrate that performance gains are achieved by moving beyond simple heuristics. The resulting tool provides a general methodology for maximizing computational resources in future quantum computing platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous, quantitative analysis of noise and thermal management in superconducting circuits directly impacts the feasibility and scaling limits of quantum processors, which is a core concern for quantum information scientists.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary bottleneck for scaling superconducting quantum processors is the heat and noise burden imposed by the necessary control wiring at millikelvin temperatures.

**Main result.** Systematic optimization of wiring configurations can substantially outperform conventional 'best practice' designs, revealing that the optimal solution is highly dependent on the specific system context and operating regime.

**Method.** The authors developed a whole-system numerical modeling approach to systematically optimize coaxial wiring design, incorporating heat load and noise propagation calculations.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a superconducting quantum processor connected via coaxial wiring through multiple cryogenic stages (e.g., 4K, MXC). The model calculates heat loads from conduction and signal dissipation, and noise propagation using established quantum noise formulas.

**Key observables.** Device temperature, noise burden (power spectral density), and achievable qubit capacity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Total attenuation levels, cable geometry, and the operating temperature of various cryogenic stages.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model uses phenomenological cooling-power curves for the dilution refrigerator and adopts an empirical Ohmic model for DC losses.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate temperature responses and noise contributions across different cryogenic stages as a function of total attenuation, identifying an 'equal balance' point for minimum total noise.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the bottleneck problem, details the holistic numerical modeling approach (including equations for noise and heat load), presents systematic optimization results showing context-dependency, and concludes by providing a web-based tool for general application.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Cryogenic capacity is a key bottleneck in the scaling of superconducting quantum processors, critically impacted by control wiring. Currently, wiring architectures are often designed following a small set of "best-practice" heuristics, little changed since they emerged for early, small-scale systems, and not rigorously evaluated for wider contexts. Here, we adopt a whole-system numerical modelling approach and present a systematic optimisation for the coaxial wiring design of a superconducting quantum processor. We identify optimised configurations that substantively outperform conventional designs in device temperature, noise burden, qubit capacity and other practical metrics. Importantly, our analysis delivers varied outcomes for different system contexts and operating regimes, illustrating the benefits of a systematic approach when there is no universal solution. Our results evaluate and sometimes challenge conventional wisdom in relation to design factors such as the utility of 0dB attenuators. We also use a wide exploration of cable configurations---directly enabled by our holistic and flexible numerical modelling approach---to develop more rigorously supported general principles for future cryogenic system design, based on a new conceptual framework to describe attenuator cascades that draws on analogies to low-noise amplifier chains. Our approach, design outcomes, and generalised analyses should also be readily applicable to other cryogenic quantum computing platforms. Finally, we support ready adoption of this approach through a web-based graphical tool which streamlines the analysis of the proposed framework under different contexts. Our results illustrate how our approach can help maximise the available computational resources of quantum processors at all system scales.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an antiblockade charging protocol for quantum batteries by engineering a bright-dark interaction within a three-level system. This novel protocol circumvents the energy-limiting blockade effect, leading to significantly enhanced energy storage capacity. The scheme is shown to be robust and capable of achieving superradiant power scaling in the bad-cavity limit.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses energy storage limitations in quantum devices by leveraging engineered quantum interference (bright-dark coupling) to create an antiblockade channel, which is highly relevant to cavity QED and open quantum system dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is overcoming the energy accumulation limitation in Quantum Batteries (QBs) caused by the inherent blockade effect.

**Main result.** The proposed antiblockade charging protocol, enabled by engineering a bright-dark interaction, significantly outperforms traditional blockade designs, achieving at least a 20-fold enhancement in energy storage.

**Method.** The analysis uses non-Hermitian Hamiltonians and steady-state solution derivations to model the charging dynamics in the presence of engineered coupling.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a V-type three-level QB cell ensemble coupled to a bosonic charger, analyzed in the bright and dark state basis.

**Key observables.** Steady-state energy storage ($E_s$), mean charging power ($P(t)$), and the second-order correlation function ($g^{(2)}(	au)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key regimes include the resonant scenario ($\omega_c = \omega_0$), the blockade ($\Delta=0$), and the antiblockade ($\Delta 
eq 0$) channel.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is simplified by restricting to the resonant scenario and retaining only lowest-order excitations for energy storage calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate energy diagrams comparing blockade ($\Delta=0$) vs. antiblockade ($\Delta 
eq 0$) channels, and compare $g^{(2)}(	au)$ and $E_s$ for both regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the blockade problem, proposes the antiblockade charging protocol via bright-dark coupling, derives the enhanced energy storage using non-Hermitian methods, and demonstrates superradiant power scaling in the bad-cavity limit.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Blockade, a phenomenon in which one particle hinders the transport of another, constitutes a core research topic in mesoscopic and microscopic physics. Benefiting from the unique single-particle characteristics, it underpins versatile applications spanning from quantum computation to quantum metrology. Nevertheless, in the context of quantum energy?storage devices, i.e., quantum batteries (QBs), blockade triggered by the intrinsic level splitting is in turn detrimental to energy accumulation. Here we propose an antiblockade charging protocol enabled by deliberately engineering a bright-dark interaction. Rooted in a manifold of equally-spaced dressed-dark states, this elaborate configuration opens up a new charging channel facilitating an antiblockade energy flow and outperforms the Rabi-split blockade design with at least a 20-fold enhancement in energy storage. Additionally, we further illustrate the achievement of superextensive (quadratic) charging characteristic by operating the system in the bad-cavity limit. In view of its inherent generality, our scheme offers a universal and feasible pathway toward high-performance quantum energy architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores controlling the Purcell effect in a planar photon-magnon system by treating the coupling and dissipation using non-Hermitian quantum theory. By systematically tuning magnon damping and magnetization, the authors show they can engineer the system into a regime where cavity photon decay is selectively enhanced by magnon-mediated losses. This establishes a versatile, chip-compatible method for controlling hybrid quantum dissipation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics and open quantum systems as it provides a concrete, tunable platform (magnonics) to study non-Hermitian physics, level attraction, and engineered dissipation effects, which are central topics in modern quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the realization and control of the Purcell effect in hybrid quantum systems where photon and magnon dynamics are coupled and subject to dissipation.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that engineered magnon damping and saturation magnetization can be used to drive the system into a Purcell regime, enabling precise control over cavity-photon dissipation.

**Method.** The authors developed a quantum theoretical framework using non-Hermitian coupled-mode theory and the input-output formalism, validated by full-wave electromagnetic simulations.

**Model / system.** The system models a planar hybrid quantum platform consisting of an Inverted Octagon-Ring Resonator (IORR) photon cavity coupled to a Yttrium Iron Garnet (YIG) magnon film. The dynamics are described by an open-system Hamiltonian incorporating coherent and dissipative photon-magnon coupling.

**Key observables.** Purcell factor ($F_P$), effective cavity decay rate ($eta$), hybrid-mode spectra, and time-domain decay profiles.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnon damping parameter ($\alpha$), Saturation Magnetization ($M_s$), and the ratio of coherent to dissipative coupling ($J$ vs. $\Gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the system can be modeled effectively using non-Hermitian quantum mechanics and that the explored parameter space is experimentally accessible.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the schematic setup, the quantum coupled-mode model, and simulation results showing the dependence of decay rates and Purcell factors on damping and coupling strength.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical framework (non-Hermitian theory) to simulating the system response by varying key parameters ($\alpha$ and $M_s$), culminating in the demonstration of controlled Purcell enhancement via dissipation engineering.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Purcell effect has emerged as a powerful mechanism for controlling spontaneous emission and dissipation in cavity and nanophotonic systems; however, its realization in dissipative non-Hermitian hybrid platforms remains largely unexplored. In this work, we investigate the Purcell effect in a dissipatively coupled photon-magnon hybrid quantum system consisting of a yttrium iron garnet (YIG) thin film integrated with an inverted octa-ring resonator (IORR) in a planar geometry. To describe the underlying dissipative hybrid dynamics, we develop a quantum theoretical framework based on non-Hermitian coupled-mode theory combined with the input-output formalism. We also analyze the temporal decay dynamics of the photon-magnon system. Full-wave electromagnetic simulations demonstrate that the system can be engineered to operate in the level-attraction regime through dissipative photon-magnon coupling. By systematically tuning the magnon damping, we uncover the emergence of a Purcell regime in which cavity-photon dissipation is selectively enhanced through magnon-mediated loss channels. We further show that the saturation magnetization (Ms) provides an additional degree of control over the onset, tunability, and robustness of the Purcell enhancement. The combined modulation of magnon damping and Ms strongly influences the effective photon-magnon coupling, hybrid-mode evolution, and dissipation landscape, enabling precise control of hybrid quantum states. These findings establish a versatile strategy for engineering magnetization- and dissipation-controlled photon-magnon interactions in planar, chip-compatible architectures, opening new avenues for non-Hermitian cavity magnonics, tunable microwave dissipation engineering, and hybrid quantum information technologies.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a theoretical framework for engineering dissipative quantum reservoirs using neutral atom arrays to enable intrinsic temporal memory for Quantum Reservoir Computing. By demonstrating that controlled dissipation is crucial for achieving necessary properties like the echo state property, the authors show that complex non-linear tasks can be solved efficiently with minimal physical resources.

**Why it may be interesting.** It bridges the gap between open quantum systems theory (dissipation, steady states) and quantum machine learning, providing concrete, physically realizable mechanisms (controlled dissipation) for achieving temporal memory in neutral atom arrays.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of implementing Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC) on NISQ hardware by achieving intrinsic temporal memory without relying on external classical buffering.

**Main result.** Controlled dissipation is shown to be strictly necessary to induce the required echo state property, fading memory, and separability in neutral atom arrays, enabling complex non-linear task solving with few atoms.

**Method.** The study simulates and empirically identifies specific dynamical regimes by analyzing the system's evolution under various noise channels using the Lindblad master equation framework.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is an analog neutral-atom array trapped in optical tweezers, whose dynamics are governed by a Hamiltonian including Rydberg interactions and dissipation modeled by the Lindblad master equation.

**Key observables.** Echo State Property (ESP), fading memory, separability, computational capacity, and the residual memory provided by the phase damping channel.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The amount of dissipation ($\gamma$), the proximity to the edge of quantum chaos, and the number of atoms ($N$, shown to work for $N=5$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on engineering dissipative quantum reservoirs and utilizes the Pure Output (PO) formalism to classify the dynamical behavior of the quantum channels.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a framework by first defining the problem (QRC memory), then analyzing the necessary dissipative dynamics using the Lindblad equation and PO formalism, and finally demonstrating performance on complex time-series tasks.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC) exploits the rich dynamics of quantum many-body systems to process time-dependent information with high-dimensional state spaces. While neutral atom arrays offer a scalable platform for this paradigm, realizing intrinsic temporal memory without relying on external classical buffering requires precise control over the relaxation dynamics of the system. We address the necessity of specific non-unitary dynamics for enforcing fading memory while overcoming the lack of separability. This study simulates and empirically identifies the specific dynamical regimes required to maximize the computational capacity of the reservoir, addressing the amount of dissipation, the effectiveness of lying at the edge of quantum chaos, and that of time-multiplexing compared to the scaling of the physical size of the system. Here we show that controlled dissipation is strictly necessary to induce echo state property, fading memory, and separability, in neutral atom arrays. Computational performance increases at the edge of quantum chaos. The counter-intuitive residual memory provided by the unital phase damping channel is justified by introducing a model based on pure outputs. The consequent implementation enables the solution of complex non-linear tasks, such as the Mackey-Glass time series, with as few as $N=5$ atoms. We establish a rigorous framework for engineering dissipative quantum reservoirs required for intrinsic temporal processing on near-term quantum devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores superfluidity in a spin-orbit-coupled Fermi gas by introducing controlled dissipation. Using non-Hermitian mean-field theory, the authors demonstrate that moderate loss can unexpectedly enhance the pairing gap and allow continuous tuning across the BCS-BEC crossover. This establishes dissipation as a versatile control parameter for quantum gas physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it extends standard BCS/BEC theory into the non-Hermitian domain, showing how engineered dissipation can actively enhance quantum correlations, a key topic in open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how spin-selective dissipation (loss) affects superfluid pairing in a two-dimensional Fermi gas coupled with Rashba spin-orbit coupling.

**Main result.** Moderate dissipation significantly enhances the pairing gap in a non-monotonic fashion, providing a new control knob to tune the system across the BCS-BEC crossover and modify the spectral structure.

**Method.** The authors employed a non-Hermitian mean-field BCS-BEC crossover theory, solving the gap and number equations self-consistently.

**Model / system.** The system is a 2D Rashba spin-orbit-coupled Fermi gas subjected to spin-dependent one-body loss ($\propto i\Gamma\sigma_z$). The theory uses the Bogoliubov-de Gennes formalism.

**Key observables.** Pairing gap ($\Delta_0$), chemical potential ($\mu$), quasiparticle spectrum (exceptional rings), and singlet/triplet pairing correlations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dissipation strength ($\Gamma$), SOC strength ($\alpha$), and interaction strength ($E_b$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the non-Hermitian mean-field approximation and assumes a symmetric solution for the order parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the non-monotonic dependence of $\Delta_0$ and $\mu$ on $\Gamma$ and $\alpha$, the reconstruction of the Density of States (DOS) in the complex plane, and the phase diagram showing 1ER vs. 3ER superfluid regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by setting up the non-Hermitian Hamiltonian, solving the self-consistent gap and number equations, analyzing the resulting quasiparticle spectrum (exceptional rings), and finally discussing the implications for pairing enhancement and crossover physics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate superfluid pairing in a two-dimensional Rashba spin-orbit-coupled Fermi gas subject to spin-selective one-body loss. Within the non-Hermitian mean-field framework, we self- consistently solve the gap and number equations and find that moderate dissipation can significantly enhance the pairing gap, resulting in a pronounced nonmonotonic dependence on the dissipation strength. Dissipation also provides an additional control parameter for driving the system across the BCS-BEC crossover. We further analyze the quasi-particle spectrum and identify two distinct superfluid regimes characterized by one and three exceptional rings, separated by an exceptional spectral transition. Interestingly, dissipation can enhance both pairing channels while simultaneously inducing a momentum-dependent phase twist in the triplet component. These results demonstrate that spin-selective dissipation provides a versatile non-Hermitian control knob for manipulating superfluid pairing, spectral structure, and crossover physics in spin-orbit-coupled quantum gases.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a theoretical framework to show that the effects of having different interaction parameters on different layers of a multiplex network can be mathematically mapped onto the effects of partial structural overlap between those layers. By analyzing various opinion models, the authors demonstrate a powerful interchangeability principle connecting dynamical heterogeneity to structural disorder.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on social dynamics, the mathematical techniques—analyzing phase transitions, mapping between parameter spaces, and using mean-field rate equations—are highly relevant to understanding phase transitions in complex quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the interchangeability between two duplex opinion dynamics systems: one with distinct dynamics parameters on each layer and full overlap, and another with identical parameters but only partial node overlap.

**Main result.** A general framework is established showing that dynamical heterogeneity (different parameters) can be formally mapped onto structural randomization (partial overlap $r$) via a derived functional relationship $a_2(r) = f(g(r))$.

**Method.** The analysis uses rate equations derived from the opinion dynamics on multiplex networks, employing the Landau approach to identify critical lines between ordered and disordered phases.

**Model / system.** The system is a binary opinion model on a two-layer full-graph multiplex (duplex) network, analyzed using models like the q-voter, q-Ising, and Sznajd models.

**Key observables.** Total magnetization ($m$), critical lines separating ordered/disordered phases, and the relative distance $D(r)$ quantifying solution correspondence.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dynamics parameters ($a_1, a_2$), overlap fraction ($r$), and model-specific parameters like lobby size ($q$) and independence probability ($p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on a mean-field approach, assuming the thermodynamic limit ($N 	o \infty$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic comparisons of the two system types, phase diagrams showing the effect of heterogeneity on transitions, and plots quantifying the relative distance $D(r)$ between solutions from the two settings.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the two system settings, develops the general framework for phase transition analysis using rate equations, derives the key mapping equation, and then applies this framework to several specific, well-known opinion dynamics models to validate the interchangeability.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We examine the interchangeability between two duplex systems with opinion dynamics: one characterized by different dynamics parameters $a_1$ and $a_2$ on each layer and full node overlap, and the second with the same parameter $a_1$ on both layers but only partial node overlap $r$. We introduce a general framework for identifying the critical line between the ordered and disordered phases, thereby translating one system into another. The description is supported by several examples of opinion dynamics models and a rigorous measure of the distance between the stationary solutions of the rate equations in both settings. Our results show that even large values of $r$ are sufficient to obtain very good correspondence between the two settings.

</details>

**Summary.** This work demonstrates that in high-dimensional stochastic systems, the geometry of the state space—specifically the 'interdimensional funnel'—can enhance topological protection. By increasing dimensionality, the system's steady-state probability becomes increasingly localized onto a coherent one-dimensional cycle, a phenomenon unique to stochastic systems and not found in standard quantum models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The findings provide a novel mechanism where high dimensionality, instead of causing decoherence, structures the dynamics into highly coherent, low-dimensional topological cycles, which has implications for understanding complex biological or active matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how high dimensionality can enhance topologically protected oscillations in stochastic systems, contrary to the expectation that high dimensions complicate dynamics.

**Main result.** Increasing the dimensionality ($D$) enhances an 'interdimensional funnel,' leading to improved one-dimensional localization and enhanced oscillatory coherence in the steady state.

**Method.** The analysis uses stochastic simulation (Gillespie method) alongside rigorous spectral analysis of the transition matrix to characterize localization and coherence.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a continuous-time Markov process on a configuration space defined by a hypercubic lattice of dimension $D$, incorporating both external and internal transitions.

**Key observables.** Localization measures ($	ext{Ploc}$, $	ext{floc}$), oscillation coherence (Period Precision $N$, Spectral Coherence $R$), and the steady-state probability distribution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimensionality ($D$), the ratio of transition rates ($\gamma_{ex}/\gamma_{in}$), and the system size ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes a specific structure for the stochastic dynamics (hypercubic lattice, ring-like internal states) and analyzes the system in the topological regime ($\gamma_{ex}/\gamma_{in} > 1$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate stochastic trajectories showing the funneling effect from the bulk to a 1D cycle, and compare steady-state probabilities across varying dimensions ($D$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical model and the counter-intuitive result (high D enhances coherence) to detailed mathematical analysis using spectral theory to prove the isolation of the steady-state band and characterize the mode structure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Biochemical systems such as protein complexes often occupy configuration spaces whose dimensionality grows with the number of molecular components. Although usually viewed as an obstacle to coherent dynamics, here we show that high dimensionality can enhance topologically protected oscillations. In the topological regime, a stochastic system with a $D$-dimensional configuration space develops a steady-state current confined to a one-dimensional edge cycle. The intervening boundary faces between the $D$-dimensional bulk and the one-dimensional cycle form an interdimensional funnel, sequentially pushing the system towards lower dimensional faces, down to the one-dimensional cycle. As a consequence, increasing $D$ enhances the funnel, leading to improved one-dimensional localization and oscillatory coherence. A quantized biorthogonal Zak phase identifies the transition to the topological regime, which additionally exhibits a highly structured non-Hermitian skin effect. In contrast with previous realizations of topological states in stochastic systems, the phenomena we uncover have no counterpart in quantum condensed matter or active matter systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a novel Generalized Langevin Equation (GLE) framework by employing a non-stationary projection distribution, allowing friction parameters to depend on the observable's position. Applying this to protein folding, the authors find that position-dependent friction effects are significant, especially for larger proteins like villin, suggesting that standard, stationary models are insufficient for accurate dynamic description.

**Why it may be interesting.** The methodology of deriving dynamics from constrained, non-stationary ensembles is highly relevant to open quantum systems and non-equilibrium statistical mechanics, providing a framework to incorporate system-specific constraints into generalized equations of motion.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the role of position-dependent friction effects in describing the complex dynamics of protein folding.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that observable-dependent friction effects are non-negligible, particularly for proteins like villin, requiring the use of non-stationary Generalized Langevin Equations (GLEs).

**Method.** The authors derive and apply a novel class of GLEs using a non-stationary projection distribution, constraining the initial ensemble to a hypersurface in phase space.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is protein folding, using reduced models like poly-alanine and villin, whose dynamics are described by a Hamiltonian system and analyzed via the GLE.

**Key observables.** Constrained Mean Squared Displacements (CMSD), total friction ($\gamma$), and the memory friction kernel ($\Gamma$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The initial constraint value ($A_0$), which dictates the position-dependent nature of the friction parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The standard GLE derivation assumes a stationary canonical distribution, which the authors overcome by using a non-stationary projection scheme.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare CMSD predictions from Molecular Dynamics (MD) versus the non-stationary GLE for Ala9 and villin, showing significant deviations for villin, and plot total friction vs. position $A_0$.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the GLE formalism, details the derivation using a non-stationary projection, applies this to protein folding trajectories (Ala9 and villin), and compares the resulting dynamics (CMSD and friction) against standard MD simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Generalized Langevin Equation (GLE) is an integro-differential equation of motion for a general observable of a many-body system and is rigorously derived using the projection operator formalism. In the standard derivation, a stationary canonical phase space distribution is used for the projection. Here, we derive a novel class of GLEs using a non-stationary projection distribution that constrains the initial ensemble to a hypersurface in phase space on which the observable has a fixed value $A_0$. As a result, all GLE parameters, and in particular the memory friction kernel, depend on $A_0$ and can be extracted from short simulations that do not sample the entire phase space. Applying this non-stationary GLE to protein folding trajectories, we find for villin and an $α$-helical poly-alanine segment that the total friction is higher in the folded state. These results demonstrate that observable-dependent friction effects are non-negligible and can be accounted for using non-stationary GLEs derived by constrained projection schemes.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the coarsening of charge-density waves in a 2D quasiclassical Holstein model after a thermal quench. It reveals that while the order parameter is nonconserved, microscopic charge conservation forces the domain growth to follow an anomalous $t^{1/4}$ law at low temperatures. This behavior smoothly crosses over to the expected $t^{1/2}$ law at higher temperatures, demonstrating the profound influence of microscopic constraints on macroscopic dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The interplay between emergent order parameters (CDW) and underlying microscopic conservation laws provides a rich example of non-equilibrium dynamics in strongly correlated systems, relevant to understanding phase separation in cuprates or manganites.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how charge-density-wave (CDW) coarsening dynamics proceed in two dimensions following a thermal quench into the ordered phase, particularly focusing on the constraints imposed by microscopic charge conservation.

**Main result.** The dynamics exhibit a crossover between two regimes: a low-temperature, charge-conserving limit leading to $L(t) \sim t^{1/4}$, and a high-temperature, curvature-driven limit restoring $L(t) \sim t^{1/2}$.

**Method.** The research employs quasiclassical approximations and Langevin simulations on the Holstein model to track the evolution of domain-wall size $L(t)$.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by the two-dimensional quasiclassical Holstein model, which couples lattice vibrations ($Q_i$) to electronic charge density ($n_i$). The dynamics are governed by a Langevin equation for the lattice coordinates.

**Key observables.** Domain growth length scale $L(t)$, and the apparent power-law exponents characterizing the coarsening kinetics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), the crossover length scale $L_	imes(T)$, and the ratio of cooperative to local contributions.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is the quasiclassical limit, treating lattice motion as slow compared to fast electronic dynamics, and assuming rapid electronic relaxation to quasi-equilibrium.

**Figures summary.** The analysis relies on fitting measured growth rates and performing dimensionless rate collapses to confirm the crossover picture between different power-law behaviors.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the quasiclassical Hamiltonian and equations of motion, simulates the thermal quench protocol, analyzes the resulting correlation functions to define $L(t)$, and finally discusses the crossover kinetics linking the $t^{1/4}$ and $t^{1/2}$ regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate charge-density-wave (CDW) coarsening in a two-dimensional quasiclassical Holstein model following thermal quenches into the ordered phase. Although the staggered CDW order parameter is nonconserved, domain-wall motion remains constrained by conservation of the microscopic electronic charge. The simulations exhibit apparent growth exponents between $1/4$ and the Allen--Cahn value $1/2$. These apparent exponents are primarily accounted for by the crossover kinetics $\dot{L}=[D(T)/L][1+L_\times^2(T)/L^2]$, where $L_\times(T)$ is a temperature-dependent crossover length. At low temperature, nearly binary occupations require compatible wall segments to rearrange cooperatively, producing the $L(t)\sim t^{1/4}$ limit. Thermal broadening creates partially occupied interfacial sites that permit local curvature-driven motion and restore $L(t)\sim t^{1/2}$ at late times. Fits to the measured growth rate, a dimensionless rate collapse, and a long-time large-system simulation consistently support this crossover picture. These results show that a microscopic conservation law can generate long-lived anomalous coarsening even when the emergent order parameter is nonconserved.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes using a waveguide-QED system to create a quantum reservoir with programmable memory. By coupling a nonlinear resonator to a waveguide, the system achieves coherent, delayed feedback that acts as a controllable memory source. This establishes a novel physical mechanism for engineering memory required for advanced quantum machine learning tasks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges quantum optics (waveguide QED, cavity QED) with quantum information processing (reservoir computing), providing a concrete physical platform for realizing complex quantum computation tasks.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is engineering task-relevant memory profiles in Quantum Reservoir Computing (QRC) because intrinsic dynamics do not naturally provide the necessary memory control.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that coherent delayed feedback, realized via a waveguide-QED setup, provides a hardware-level mechanism to engineer task-relevant memory in QRC.

**Method.** The study models the reservoir using a driven Kerr-nonlinear resonator coupled nonlocally to a 1D waveguide, deriving the memory kernel from the waveguide propagation delay.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a waveguide-QED quantum reservoir, involving a Kerr-nonlinear resonator coupled to a 1D waveguide. The dynamics are governed by a Hamiltonian incorporating Kerr nonlinearity and coupling terms, leading to a non-Markovian feedback channel.

**Key observables.** Delay-resolved memory benchmarks, including Short-Term-Memory (STM) capacity ($C_{	ext{STM}}^d$) and delayed-product capacity ($C_{	ext{prod}}^d$), and the prediction error (NRMSE) for time-series forecasting.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling geometry dictates the finite propagation delay ($	au_{\mu
u}$), while the Kerr nonlinearity ($U$) controls the conversion of returned fields into nonlinear features.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that the unmeasured propagating field acts as a coherent memory carrier, avoiding intermediate measurement or classical reinjection.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the waveguide-QED setup, showing how the resonator response depends on the programmed delay. Capacity plots compare STM and product reconstruction capabilities for varying delays, and NRMSE plots compare performance against different reservoir types (TDQRC vs TDLRC) for forecasting tasks.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the physical setup and the concept of engineering memory via delayed feedback. It then uses capacity benchmarks (STM, product) to separate the roles of waveguide propagation (timing) and Kerr nonlinearity (feature transformation). Finally, it applies this framework to forecasting chaotic time series (Mackey-Glass) to compare model performance.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Reservoir computing requires not only long memory, but also memory distributed over the timescales relevant to a given task. In most quantum reservoir computing architectures, however, the memory profile emerges from intrinsic dynamics and cannot be directly engineered. Here we introduce a waveguide-QED quantum reservoir in which coherent memory return is controlled by the coupling geometry. The reservoir consists of a driven Kerr-nonlinear resonator coupled to a one-dimensional waveguide at spatially separated points. An unmeasured propagating field carries information about past inputs and coherently returns it to the resonator after a finite propagation time, forming a non-Markovian feedback channel without intermediate measurement or classical reinjection. Delay-resolved memory benchmarks reveal two complementary functions: waveguide propagation determines when past information returns, whereas Kerr dynamics convert the returned field into nonlinear temporal features accessible through a linear readout. NARMA-$n$ prediction shows that the optimal feedback delay shifts systematically with task order and approximately tracks the explicit input-product lag. These results establish coherent delayed feedback as a mechanism for engineering task-relevant memory in quantum reservoir computing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an advanced, fully calibrated simulation framework for spin qubits in Si/SiGe devices, designed to overcome discrepancies between theory and experiment. By integrating complex physics—including charge screening effects and magnetic hysteresis via the Jiles-Atherton model—the authors successfully predict key experimental signatures like resonant frequencies and power chevrons. This robust tool significantly advances the predictive capability for scalable quantum computing architectures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous coupling of classical charge effects (electrostatics) and magnetic dynamics (micromagnetics) into a quantum Hamiltonian provides a highly realistic framework for modeling solid-state qubits, which is crucial for understanding decoherence and control.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is bridging the gap between theoretical quantum device simulations and actual experimental measurements for silicon-based spin qubits.

**Main result.** The authors developed a comprehensive, experimentally calibrated simulation pipeline that successfully reproduces key experimental observables, such as resonant frequencies and microwave power chevrons, across a multi-dot qubit array.

**Method.** The work combines advanced 3D classical electrostatics (Poisson solver) with micromagnetism (Jiles-Atherton model) and quantum dynamics (time-dependent RWA Hamiltonian) to model the coupled system.

**Model / system.** The system is a Si/SiGe heterostructure containing a 6-dot quantum dot array, coupled to integrated cobalt micromagnets. The simulation incorporates refined band alignment, a semi-empirical charge screening model, and the Jiles-Atherton model for magnetic hysteresis.

**Key observables.** Microwave power chevrons, qubit resonant frequencies ($f = g\mu_B / h B$), and gate lever-arm variation ($dE_C/dV_{	ext{gate}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Microwave frequency ($\omega_0$), external magnetic field ($B_{	ext{ext}}$), and material parameters calibrated against experimental data.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes the use of the Rotating-Wave Approximation (RWA) for dynamics and required significant empirical calibration for charge screening and magnetic hysteresis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the FEM model components, band alignment shifts, the calibration of Jiles-Atherton parameters, and direct comparisons between simulated and measured power chevrons for the six qubits.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically addresses simulation failures by first refining the electrostatic model (charge screening) and then incorporating the magnetic hysteresis via the Jiles-Atherton model, finally coupling these results into a time-dependent RWA Hamiltonian to predict dynamic observables.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Silicon-based spin qubits in Si/SiGe heterostructures are a leading platform for scalable quantum computing, yet bridging the gap between theoretical computer-aided design(CAD) models and experimental reality remains a significant challenge. Standard simulations often fail to capture critical physical phenomena, such as interface dipoles, parasitic charge accumulation, and the magnetic hysteresis of on-chip micromagnets. In this work, we present a comprehensive, experimentally calibrated 3D simulation pipeline for a 6-dot Si/SiGe device. We refine the semiconductor band alignment and introduce a semi-empirical classical charge screening model to accurately capture the formation of parasitic wells and their suppression of gate lever-arms. Furthermore, we apply the Jiles-Atherton model to account for the hysteresis and pre-magnetization of integrated cobalt micromagnets, successfully reproducing the experimental resonant frequencies across all six qubits. By coupling these calibrated electrostatic and magnetic profiles into a time-dependent rotating wave approximation (RWA) Hamiltonian, we reproduce experimental observables, including microwave power chevrons. This framework provides a robust foundation for predicting device behavior, evaluating microwave line losses, and optimizing future scalable spin qubit architectures prior to fabrication.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a rigorous theory to assess how errors from inexact numerical solvers accumulate in recursive state estimation processes. It introduces a framework that certifies the repairability of these solvers, allowing for the practical integration of machine learning and quantum-derived gains into classical control loops. This provides a vital tool for analyzing hybrid quantum-classical computation in physical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the application is classical state estimation, the rigorous mathematical framework for error bounding, certification, and hybrid quantum-classical integration is highly relevant for controlling open quantum systems where measurement and feedback are inherently noisy and approximate.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes the repairability of inexact numerical solvers used within recursive state estimation loops, addressing how local approximation errors affect the overall finite-horizon system performance.

**Main result.** It establishes an exact one-step repairability boundary and provides a framework to evaluate approximate solvers and ML-proposed corrections through independent residual certification, even when integrating quantum solvers.

**Method.** The authors derive an exact residual-drift identity for the covariance response and compare classical iterative solvers (like CG) against quantum-inspired solvers (VQLS) within a state-space estimation framework.

**Model / system.** The core model is a fixed linear Kalman model used for recursive state estimation, applicable to systems like power grids or adaptive optics. The analysis uses a finite-horizon linear-Gaussian state-space model.

**Key observables.** Local defect ($E_k$), inherited gain drift ($\Gamma_k$), finite-horizon covariance response (showing quartic contributions), and the minimum conjugate-gradient iteration count.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis depends on the prescribed subspace, the Frobenius-norm budget for corrections, and the tolerance levels ($\eta$) used in the solvers.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial analysis assumes a fixed linear model and matched initialization, though the framework is designed to evaluate extensions to more complex dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the separation of current solve error from inherited drift, the opposing quartic contributions to the covariance response, and deployment trajectories comparing different solver policies across various IEEE test systems.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from characterizing the error propagation via an exact identity, defining the local repairability boundary, integrating ML-proposed corrections via a residual certificate, and finally validating the framework by applying it to power-grid state estimation using quantum and classical solvers.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recursive state estimation often executes approximate numerical solutions inside a feedback loop, where highly accurate local steps do not guarantee better overall results. For a fixed linear Kalman model, we characterize when a correction within a prescribed subspace and norm budget can meet a local admissibility tolerance, and how the defects actually executed affect the finite-horizon covariance response. Centering each defect on the exact gain for the implemented covariance separates current solve error from inherited gain drift. Expanding the exact residual-drift identity reveals opposing quartic contributions beyond the quadratic response: innovation-covariance inflation enters positively, while local-gain reoptimization enters subtractively. Under matched initialization, an absolute sixth-order remainder bound, uniform over bounded defect sequences at fixed horizon, gives sufficient conditions for quadratic under- or overprediction. Machine learning proposes bounded corrections, while a learner-independent residual certificate and verified fallback govern execution of classical and quantum candidates without changing the reference estimator. In a power-grid tolerance study, learned correction lowers the minimum conjugate-gradient iteration count for deployment without fallback relative to uncorrected solves under the same residual certificate. Gains reconstructed from a variational quantum linear solver and from an annealing-based binary encoding, with small-scale terminal measurements on superconducting hardware and sampling on a quantum annealer, are executed through the same interface. By linking local repairability to nonlinear error propagation, the framework evaluates approximate solvers and learned corrections through independent certification and finite-horizon response, providing a practical basis for studying hybrid quantum--classical computation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details a theoretical sub-Doppler cooling scheme for trapped-ion crystals using optical tweezers and microwave drives. By modeling the system as a three-level atom coupled to motional modes, the authors numerically demonstrate fast cooling rates for multiple ion chains. This method offers a versatile tool for preparing quantum states in trapped-ion quantum simulators.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for implementing advanced cooling techniques in trapped ions, moving beyond standard Doppler cooling by leveraging engineered light-matter interactions in a controlled manner.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes and investigates a novel sub-Doppler cooling scheme for trapped-ion crystals using optical tweezers.

**Main result.** The scheme achieves fast cooling rates, reaching $1/e$ cooling times below 2 ms for the center of mass mode, and is suitable as an intermediate cooling stage between Doppler and ground-state sideband cooling.

**Method.** The dynamics are modeled using a three-level system and solved by numerically simulating the open quantum system evolution via the Lindblad master equation and quantum trajectories.

**Model / system.** The physical system is trapped ions in Paul traps, cooled by a combination of a hollow optical tweezer and microwave drive. The internal states are modeled as a three-level system ($\{|gangle, |eangle, |aangle\}$) which is reduced to an effective two-level system for analysis.

**Key observables.** Cooling time ($	au$), steady-state excitation number ($ar{n}_{	ext{com}}$), and the evolution of motional modes (center of mass, stretch).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Tweezer detunings ($\Delta_L$), Rabi frequencies ($\Omega$), and trapping frequencies ($\omega_P, \omega_T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The scheme does not rely on the Lamb-Dicke regime and utilizes off-resonant light. The analysis often involves adiabatic elimination of auxiliary states.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate the cooling mechanism (Sisyphus cooling) and show the time evolution of the center of mass mode occupation for one, two, and three ion crystals, comparing numerical results with variational ansatz predictions.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the cooling scheme and the underlying physics, develops the Hamiltonian and master equation, and then uses advanced numerical techniques (quantum trajectories, variational ansatz) to simulate and optimize the cooling parameters for various ion crystal sizes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a sub-Doppler cooling scheme for trapped-ion crystals in Paul traps. The combination of a hollow tweezer and microwave drive creates an effective two-level system which is cooled through attractive Sisyphus cooling. For ${}^{171}\rm{Yb}^+$ ions, we numerically identify optimal parameters for one- and two-ion crystals, and investigate the cooling rate in ion crystals with up to three ions. Our cooling scheme utilizes a single ion addressed with a tweezer to achieve sub-Doppler cooling of the $N$ axial motional modes perpendicular to the tweezer propagation direction, does not rely on the Lamb-Dicke regime and uses off-resonant light. We reach $1/e$ cooling times for the center of mass mode of $τ_{\rm com}<2$ ms, and a steady-state excitation number of $\bar{n}_{\rm{com}}\approx 0.7$. Our scheme is well-suited as an intermediate stage between Doppler cooling and ground-state sideband cooling, and can be readily realized in trapped-ion platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a theoretical framework to optimize local oscillators for measuring multiphoton quantum states via homodyne detection. It reformulates the problem as finding the leading eigenpair of the state's Joint Spectral Amplitude tensor, using HOSVD for initial estimates. The results guide experimentalists on when simple spectral analysis suffices versus when complex, full optimization is required.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous, computationally guided framework for designing experimental measurement settings (LOs) necessary to characterize and verify non-Gaussian quantum states generated in quantum optics experiments.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a method for optimizing the local oscillator (LO) used in homodyne detection to maximize the detection efficiency of multiphoton-number states.

**Main result.** For weakly correlated states, the leading HOSVD mode provides the optimal LO, achieving near-unity visibility; however, strongly correlated states require full, complex optimization and saturate below unit visibility.

**Method.** The optimization problem is formulated as finding the leading eigenpair of the Joint Spectral Amplitude (JSA) tensor, utilizing the Tucker Higher-Order Singular Value Decomposition (HOSVD) for initial bounds and gradient-based optimization for refinement.

**Model / system.** The system involves the homodyne detection of $N$-photon states, whose spectral properties are encoded in the totally symmetric $N$-photon JSA tensor $\psi(\omega_1, \dots, \omega_N)$.

**Key observables.** Homodyne visibility ($\eta$), which quantifies the detection efficiency, and the convergence trace of $\eta$ during optimization.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The photon number ($N$), the spectral resolution ($n$), and the degree of correlation within the multiphoton state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary bottleneck is the explicit storage of the JSA tensor, which scales exponentially ($O(n^N)$), necessitating approximations or symmetry exploitation.

**Figures summary.** Figures show convergence traces of the objective function $\eta$ over outer iterations for optimization methods like APH, comparing convergence rates across different photon numbers ($N=3, 4, 5$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the mathematical equivalence between optimal LO design and finding the leading tensor eigenpair of the JSA. It then details the use of HOSVD for bounding and initialization, followed by comparisons of advanced optimization algorithms (e.g., APH vs. TRBH) for the general case.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a framework for optimizing pulsed local oscillators (LO) for homodyne detection of multiphoton-number states by exploiting the tensor structure of their joint-spectral amplitude (JSA). We show that finding the optimal LO is equivalent to computing the JSA tensor's leading unitary eigenpair and that the factor matrices of the JSA's Tucker higher-order singular value decomposition (HOSVD) coincide with the Schmidt modes of the photon number state's single-particle reduced density matrix. We use the HOSVD to bound the optimal homodyne visibility and to initialize gradient based optimization. In simulated JSAs, weakly correlated, few-mode states reach near-unity visibility, with the leading HOSVD mode being the optimal LO, while strongly correlated, multimode states require full optimization and saturate below unit visibility even at the true optimum. These results offer a practical route to design LOs for homodyne detection experiments with realistic sources of photon-number states, which contributes to the practical implementation of sources of non-Gaussian quantum states.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces quantum score matching, adapting a powerful classical generative model technique to learn unknown quantum thermal states (Gibbs states). By establishing a theoretically sound framework and demonstrating its effectiveness on real quantum hardware, it offers a new, experimentally realizable route for quantum state tomography and characterization.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, experimentally viable machine learning paradigm for characterizing thermal quantum states, which is crucial for simulating and understanding complex many-body physics beyond simple mean-field approximations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is extending classical score matching, a generative learning technique, to the quantum domain where states are governed by noncommuting density operators.

**Main result.** The authors establish a theoretically guaranteed quantum score-matching framework that achieves information-theoretic optimal sample complexity for learning high-temperature Gibbs states.

**Method.** They define a symmetric quantum score and formulate an objective function (squared score discrepancy) that can be evaluated using Monte Carlo sampling from the unknown target state.

**Model / system.** The focus is on learning quantum Gibbs states, $\sigma \propto e^{-eta H(	heta^*)} / 	ext{Tr}(e^{-eta H(	heta^*)})$, parameterized by a Hamiltonian $H(	heta)$. The method is tested on IBM quantum hardware.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the quantum scores ($S_a$), the objective function $J_Q(	heta)$, and the relative Hamiltonian-parameter error.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the inverse temperature ($eta$), the measurement budget (limited shots), and the locality/interaction degree of the Hamiltonian.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes the existence of a 'sufficiently rich' derivation frame for global identifiability, and the theoretical guarantees are strongest in the high-temperature regime.

**Figures summary.** The notes describe a table (Table 3) tracking the mean and relative parameter errors of observables ($ar{J}, ar{h}$) over 46 epochs of optimization on IBM hardware for two different temperatures ($eta=0.2$ and $eta=0.4$).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the quantum score-matching framework, derives the objective function and gradient estimators, proves theoretical guarantees (including sample complexity bounds), and validates the method through numerical simulations and hardware experiments.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Score matching has driven major advances in classical generative learning by enabling models to learn from data without evaluating intractable normalization constants, or partition functions. Yet, extending this principle to quantum learning requires rethinking its foundations, as quantum states are described by noncommuting density operators rather than scalar probabilities. The noncommutativity creates fundamental challenges not only in defining quantum scores, but also in developing a training framework with efficient circuit implementations and rigorous theoretical guarantees. In this work, we bridge this gap by establishing a general quantum score-matching framework with end-to-end theoretical guarantees. Applied to Gibbs-state learning, our approach avoids additional thermal-state preparation and achieves information-theoretically optimal sample complexity in the high-temperature regime for Hamiltonians with bounded locality and interaction degree. This positions score matching as a new route to state-of-the-art performance in learning quantum Gibbs states.   Beyond these theoretical results, numerical simulations show that our method remains effective even when gradients are estimated inaccurately under limited measurement budgets. Experiments on IBM quantum hardware further demonstrate that quantum score matching is NISQ-friendly: without any error mitigation or correction, it reduces the relative Hamiltonian-parameter error from 64% to approximately 10%. Together, these results extend score matching into an experimentally realizable paradigm for quantum-state learning.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes and experimentally demonstrates a novel quantum memory scheme, HAGEM, using $	ext{Eu}^{3+}$ ions engineered within molecular complexes. By chemically tailoring the hyperfine structure, the authors achieved high quantum optical storage efficiency and bandwidth. This establishes a new paradigm where molecular engineering enables quantum applications unattainable with conventional solid-state rare-earth materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum networking by providing a solid-state platform for quantum memory, bridging the gap between molecular chemistry and quantum information science.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a broadband quantum memory device essential for future quantum networks.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a quantum optical storage efficiency of 14.9% and a memory bandwidth of 200 MHz using a novel molecularly engineered $	ext{Eu}^{3+}$ complex.

**Method.** The work combines theoretical modeling of hyperfine structures with experimental demonstrations using spectral hole burning and quantum optical storage protocols.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes $	ext{Eu}^{3+}$ ions embedded in molecular complexes, employing the Hole Anti-hole Grating Echo Memory (HAGEM) protocol, which relies on specific correlations in hyperfine level separations.

**Key observables.** Quantum optical storage efficiency (14.9%), memory bandwidth (200 MHz), hyperfine level separations ($\delta$), and spectral grating structure.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Memory bandwidth (extendable to GHz), hyperfine level structure correlations, and the coupling between molecular engineering and quantum memory function.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The protocol requires specific hyperfine level structures that are engineered into the molecular complex, overcoming limitations of naturally occurring solid-state materials.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the HAGEM memory protocol, showing spectral hole burning simulations and the resulting broadband spectrum achievable with the designed $	ext{Eu}^{3+}$ complex.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for broadband quantum memory, proposing the HAGEM scheme, detailing the molecular engineering approach using $	ext{Eu}^{3+}$ complexes, and finally presenting experimental demonstrations of storage efficiency and bandwidth.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Broadband quantum memory devices are essential elements for future quantum networks. Here we propose a broadband quantum memory scheme called Hole Anti-hole Grating Echo Memory (HAGEM) for rare-earth ions in solids. We provide a Eu$^\text{3+}$ molecular complex with special hyperfine level structures of which the hyperfine level separations are in a specific mathematical correlation that can be obtained by harnessing chemical engineering. Using the memory protocol and material, we experimentally demonstrate a quantum optical storage efficiency of 14.9% and a memory bandwidth of 200MHz, which can easily be extended to a few GHz. With this demonstration, we show the first quantum application enabled by molecular engineering which cannot be achieved by any other existing Eu$^\text{3+}$ solid-state materials. In addition, we provide a framework for the chemical engineering of solid-state materials with rare-earth ions for quantum applications consisting of material design, synthesis & characterization techniques, and analytical methods for the quantum properties of rare-earth (RE) ions in solids. This work establishes a new direction in which molecular rare-earth ions can be used for a wide range of quantum applications, which cannot be realized by existing solid-state materials. This will greatly facilitate the development of molecular quantum emitter systems for real world applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a method to measure temperature gradients across a surface using quantum probes. By coupling a probe TLS to an auxiliary TLS, the system generates temperature-sensitive coherence in the probe state. This coherence enhances the measurement precision, allowing for the determination of a local spatial temperature distinguishability bound.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum metrology using open quantum systems, specifically leveraging coherence in coupled quantum probes to surpass classical limits for spatial sensing, which is highly relevant to AMO physics and quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper extends quantum thermometry from measuring temperature at a single point to estimating temperature profiles $T(x)$ across a spatial region with enhanced precision.

**Main result.** The use of quantum coherence in the probe state leads to a Quantum Fisher Information exponentially larger at low temperatures compared to measurements relying only on population differences.

**Method.** The authors derive the reduced probe state by tracing out an auxiliary TLS from the Gibbs state and calculate the Quantum Fisher Information (QFI) using the Bloch vector representation.

**Model / system.** The model involves a probe two-level system (TLS) coupled asymmetrically to an auxiliary TLS, which equilibrates with the sample exhibiting the temperature profile $T(x)$. The dynamics are governed by a Markovian master equation.

**Key observables.** Quantum Fisher Information (QFI), local spatial distinguishability bound ($\Delta x_{	ext{loc}}$), and the reduced probe state coherence $c(T)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperature regime ($T 	o 0$), coupling strengths ($g$), and the temperature gradient $|	ext{T}'(x)|$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The validity of the reparametrization requires a sufficiently small relative temperature uncertainty, and the system is assumed to relax via a Markovian master equation.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the temperature sensing protocol involving the probe, auxiliary TLS, and sample $T(x)$. Figure 2 plots the QFI in different regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the framework for spatial quantum thermometry, derives the enhanced QFI due to coherence, and then discusses the resulting local spatial distinguishability bound and experimental realization via a Mach-Zehnder interferometer.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum thermometry has long promised sensitivities beyond classical limits, with recent paradigms highlighting quantum coherence as a primary driver of precision. While most studies focus on single-point temperature estimation, we extend this framework to the spatial domain. We consider a probe two-level system (TLS) coupled asymmetrically to an auxiliary TLS that equilibrates with a sample of profile T(x). The reduced probe state carries coherence in its local basis that remains temperature-sensitive after the probe transition has frozen out, yielding a Fisher information exponentially larger at low temperature than that of a population-only readout. An exact reparametrization converts the thermometric precision into a local spatial distinguishability bound; its validity requires only a sufficiently small relative temperature uncertainty and yields an explicit measurement budget. Adding auxiliary TLSs shifts the array's optimal working temperature upward and broadens the useful sensing window, with the achievable gain ultimately limited by the total available coupling rather than by an indefinite scaling law. Finally, we propose a microwave Mach-Zehnder interferometer that maps the temperature-dependent probe coherence onto an experimentally accessible quadrature observable.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes the complex, non-equilibrium dynamics of self-attention in a recurrent transformer model. It identifies three distinct collective phenomena—temporal activity, attention condensation, and geometric clustering—that emerge from the feedback between token representations and attention weights. The work is significant for showing that sparse attention can maintain rich, persistent dynamics rather than leading to system stagnation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides a rich, non-equilibrium dynamical framework for understanding how information processing units (like attention heads) can exhibit complex, collective, and persistent dynamics analogous to phase transitions in condensed matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the nonequilibrium dynamics of self-attention mechanisms, focusing on the emergence of collective behaviors like temporal activity, attention condensation, and geometric clustering.

**Main result.** The research establishes that these three phenomena are distinct, showing that sparse attention can sustain persistent dynamics rather than causing the system to freeze.

**Method.** The analysis employs dynamical systems theory, bifurcation analysis, and scaling arguments across various dimensional limits ($d=2$ and $d=N 	o \infty$).

**Model / system.** The model is a minimal recurrent transformer with $N$ tokens, utilizing a negative value map ($V=-I$) and similarity-based attention, which creates a feedback loop between representation geometry and attention routing.

**Key observables.** Inverse Participation Ratio ($Y_A$), Maximal Lyapunov exponent ($\lambda_{\max}$), and the stability of geometric configurations (e.g., simplices).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Attention feedback strength ($\gamma$), softmax sharpness ($eta$), and the dimensionality ($d$ or $N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes its findings from finite-model artifacts observed in pre-trained LLMs, and some local stability tests are limited to linearization.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate dynamical regimes in $d=2$ (polygon stability loss), the emergence of the butterfly cone, and the comparison between geometric and attention clustering in high dimensions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by analyzing the system in low dimensions ($d=2$) to characterize bifurcations, then moves to the scaling and high-dimensional limits ($d=N 	o \infty$) to rigorously define attention condensation and its associated phases.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the nonequilibrium dynamics of a minimal recurrent transformer with $N$ normalized tokens, $Q=K=I$, and a negative value map $V=-I$. Similarity-based attention selects nearby representations, while the negative value map drives tokens away from the selected field. This feedback can continually reorganize both the representation geometry and the attention network. For $d=2$, the tokens lie on a circle, where the regular polygon is an exact fixed point. As the attention feedback strength $γ$ is increased, the polygon loses stability through a flip bifurcation, giving rise to period-two motion, chaos, and cluster-exchange or cluster-flip states. Despite this temporal complexity, attention remains diffuse as $N\to\infty$ at finite fixed softmax sharpness $β$. Attention condensation instead emerges in the scaling regime $β\sim N^2$. In the hard-routing limit, repulsive updates amplify local perturbations and routing-partner switches transmit them ballistically, producing an emergent butterfly cone in representation space. High-dimensional geometry provides a distinct route to localization. For $d=N\to\infty$, simulations from Gaussian initial conditions provide evidence for a condensation transition at $β=O(1)$, driven by dynamically generated finite overlap gaps. Depending on $γ$, the resulting phases include diffuse simplex-like states, consensus flips, condensed active routing with signatures of chaos, and fragmented cluster flips. These results establish temporal activity, attention condensation, and geometric clustering as distinct collective phenomena, and show that sparse attention can sustain persistent dynamics rather than freeze it.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously determines the optimal mathematical constraints governing weak integrability breaking in qutrit quantum systems. It proves a cubic relationship between symmetry defects and current residuals, and shows that structural compensation can dramatically extend the time window for observing conserved energy-current correlations. The findings establish robust bounds on thermal memory even when the system is perturbed away from perfect integrability.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides quantitative, rigorous bounds on how far a quantum system can be driven from integrability while retaining measurable, long-lived correlations, which is crucial for understanding thermalization dynamics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the fundamental limits on weak integrability breaking and the persistence of protected thermal memory in quantum systems near a qutrit exchange interaction.

**Main result.** It establishes a sharp cubic bound ($\delta^3 \le C\epsilon$) relating the symmetry defect to the current residual, and demonstrates that optimal compensation can extend the guaranteed energy-current correlation window to order $|\lambda|^{-3}$.

**Method.** The analysis employs rigorous mathematical techniques involving Yang-Baxter deformations, commutator expansions, and the study of spectral measures in the thermodynamic limit.

**Model / system.** The system is a qutrit chain undergoing exchange interactions, analyzed through the framework of Yang-Baxter deformations and weak perturbations ($\lambda$).

**Key observables.** Symmetry defect ($\delta$), local current-conservation residual ($\epsilon$), energy-current correlation window, and the eight-dimensional charge memory matrix.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Perturbation strength ($\lambda$), time scale ($t$), and the system size ($L$ in the thermodynamic limit).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of jointly analytic, regular Yang-Baxter deformations and that the thermodynamic limit is taken before the weak-coupling limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the evolution of local residuals and initial current forces for raw versus optimally compensated paths, illustrating the improvement in scaling exponents.

**Paper structure.** The structure moves from establishing the fundamental symmetry constraints (Theorem 1) to deriving sharp quantitative bounds ($\delta^3 \le C\epsilon$), demonstrating the effect of structural compensation, and finally analyzing the resulting protected memory in the thermodynamic limit.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Although integrability does not universally require a continuous one-site symmetry, we rigorously prove that every jointly analytic, regular Yang-Baxter deformation of the qutrit exchange interaction necessarily retains a nontrivial, analytically varying one-site charge. Breaking this local symmetry imposes a fundamental physical constraint on approximate conservation, governed by the optimal uniform bound $δ^3 \le C\varepsilon$ that explicitly relates the minimal one-site symmetry defect $δ$ to the local current-conservation residual $\varepsilon$. While breaking all one-site charges strictly forbids an exact integrable completion, an optimally compensated nearest-neighbor interaction saturates this cubic limit and anomalously extends the guaranteed infinite-temperature energy-current correlation window to order $|λ|^{-3}$ in the perturbation strength $λ$. Furthermore, we reveal a fundamental resonance obstruction for intrinsic conversion perturbations that strictly prevents any exact first-order repair of a broken one-site charge on any finite ring. Nevertheless, we demonstrate that the complete eight-dimensional charge memory matrix remains thermodynamically protected and approaches the identity for timescales $t=o(|λ|^{-3/2})$, a robust feature of the full infinite-temperature dynamics when the thermodynamic limit is taken before weak coupling.

</details>
