**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that voltage bias can be used to engineer single-molecule plasmonic cavities within STM-BJ junctions. By tuning the bias, researchers can drive an interfacial exciton while actively suppressing its decay into metallic loss channels. This establishes a powerful, electrically controllable platform for molecular quantum control.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses open quantum systems dynamics (exciton decay) in a solid-state, nanoscale environment, providing a clear mechanism (voltage tuning) to suppress decoherence in a highly lossy setting, which is central to quantum optics and molecular physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is realizing strong light-matter coupling at the single-molecule level within highly lossy metallic environments.

**Main result.** The applied voltage can be used to drive an interfacial exciton while simultaneously suppressing its coupling to metallic loss channels, establishing voltage-tunable plasmonic cavities.

**Method.** The study employs a combination of Linear Response/Real Time TDDFT for electronic structure and dynamics, and time-harmonic Maxwell equations for plasmonic mode confinement.

**Model / system.** The system is a Scanning Tunneling Microscope Break Junction (STM-BJ) acting as a plasmonic cavity coupled to a single molecule (e.g., BP). The physics is modeled using a minimal molecule-metal Hamiltonian and analyzing the decay of an interfacial exciton.

**Key observables.** Exciton lifetime/population decay rate ($	ext{w}_T$), Rabi splitting, plasmonic mode confinement, and the bias-dependent orbital overlap.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Applied bias voltage ($V$), coupling strength ($g_c$), and the metal continuum density of states ($\Pi(E_k)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on modeling the system with a minimal molecule-metal Hamiltonian and assumes the continuum limit for decay rate calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the STM-BJ setup, compare absorption spectra, show orbital hybridization confirming interfacial transitions, and map out electric field distributions under bias.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theoretical framework by defining the coupled Hamiltonian, calculating the decay rate via Fermi's golden rule, and then applying TDDFT and Maxwell simulations to show how bias controls the exciton lifetime and mode structure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Strong light-matter coupling offers a powerful framework for modifying molecular structure, dynamics, and optical response, yet achieving this regime at the single-molecule level remains a central challenge. Scanning Tunneling Microscope Break Junctions (STM-BJ) provide a promising route by combining single-molecule addressability with extreme nanoscale field confinement. However, the metallic environment that enables such confinement also introduces substantial plasmonic and excitonic losses, raising the question of how strong coupling can be realized in a metal-molecule-metal junction. Here, we identify the voltage as the key control parameter. We show that the applied bias not only drives the formation of an interfacial exciton through resonant charge transport but also suppresses its coupling to metallic loss channels. Simultaneously, the plasmonic cavity retains extreme mode confinement while the availability of modes is moderately tuned by voltage. Together, these results establish voltage-driven STM-BJs as electrically tunable single-molecule plasmonic cavities and open new routes for using the powerful STM-BJ toolbox, including quantum transport, tracking chemical dynamics, optoelectronics, and molecular-scale quantum control under strong light-matter coupling.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes signal recovery from data corrupted by sparse Wishart noise using advanced random matrix techniques. It establishes a critical signal strength threshold ($\theta_{\text{crit}}$) that dictates whether the underlying signal can be reliably extracted from the matrix's top eigenvector. The work provides rigorous analytical results confirming a BBP-like phase transition in this sparse setting.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical random matrix theory, the mathematical tools (replica method, phase transitions) are highly relevant to understanding spectral properties in complex, disordered quantum systems or neural network models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the high-dimensional recovery of a rank-one signal vector embedded within a matrix corrupted by sparse Wishart-like noise.

**Main result.** A critical threshold, $\theta_{\text{crit}}$, is identified: signal recovery is only possible when the signal strength $\theta$ exceeds this threshold, marking a BBP-like phase transition.

**Method.** The analysis employs the replica method combined with a Population Dynamics algorithm to analytically compute the top eigenpair statistics.

**Model / system.** The system is defined by the matrix $A = J + (\theta/N)\mathbf{x}\mathbf{x}^	op$, where $J = \mathbf{X}^	op\mathbf{X}$ is a Wishart-like matrix derived from a sparse random matrix $\mathbf{X}$.

**Key observables.** Average largest eigenvalue $\langle\lambda_1\rangle_A$, average top eigenvector component density, and the average overlap between the top eigenvector and the signal $\mathbf{x}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Signal strength $\theta$, rectangularity ratio $\alpha=M/N$, and the average connectivity of the noise.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the replica formalism and the assumption that the system can be analyzed in the thermodynamic limit.

**Figures summary.** No specific figure summaries were extracted, but the analysis relates to spectral observables and phase transitions.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews literature on signal inference and spiked random matrices, formulates the sparse Wishart model, and then presents the main results detailing the BBP-like transition and recovery conditions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the high-dimensional recovery of a signal vector $\mathbf{x}$ in the presence of sparse Wishart-like noise. We define an $N \times N$ matrix $A = J+(θ/N)\mathbf{xx}^{\top}$, where $\mathbf{xx}^{\top}$ is the rank-one deformation of the random noise matrix $J$. We consider a Wishart-like matrix $J={X}^{\top} X$, where $X$ is a sparse $M \times N$ random matrix with entries $X_{ij} = c_{ij}W_{ij}$, with $c_{ij}$ regulating the density of non-zero elements, and $W_{ij}$ the bond weights. Using the replica method, we compute analytically the top eigenpair statistics of $A$, and their dependence on the signal strength $θ$, the rectangularity ratio $α=\sqrt{M/N}$, and the average connectivity of the noise. The spectral observables are expressed in terms of a system of Recursive Distributional Equations, which are efficiently solved via a Population Dynamics algorithm. They allow us to compute the average largest eigenvalue $\langleλ_1\rangle_{A}$, the average top eigenvector component density, and the average overlap between the top eigenvector of $A$ and $\mathbf{x}$. We identify a critical threshold $θ_{\mathrm{crit}}$--depending on the average connectivity of the noise--that marks a BBP-like phase transition: below this value, $\langleλ_1\rangle_{A}$ is unaffected by the signal, and the overlap vanishes. Thus, the signal is not recoverable from the top eigenvector of $A$. For $θ>θ_{\mathrm{crit}}$, the signal-related outlier eigenvalue becomes $\langleλ_1\rangle_{A}$ and the overlap is nonzero, allowing for recovery of the signal. The results are in excellent agreement with numerical diagonalisation. We show that in the dense limit, the recovery threshold and eigen-statistics converge to the results predicted by the classical BBP transition for additive rank-one deformations of dense Wishart matrices.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers experimentally observed that propagating phonon transport in a ferromagnetic/piezoelectric bilayer is significantly enhanced only when the acoustic wave frequency matches the ferromagnetic resonance frequency of the magnetic film. This confirms a direct, resonant energy transfer mechanism from magnons to phonons, establishing a new method for actively controlling acoustic energy flow using magnetism.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a direct experimental realization of energy transfer between magnetic excitations (magnons) and mechanical waves (phonons) in a controlled, resonant manner, which is highly relevant for understanding coupled quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To experimentally demonstrate and investigate the phenomenon of resonantly enhanced propagating phonon transport induced by magnon pumping in a ferromagnetic/piezoelectric bilayer.

**Main result.** The enhancement of the transmitted acoustic signal occurs exclusively when the SAW frequency matches the ferromagnetic resonance (FMR) condition, confirming resonant magnon-phonon coupling.

**Method.** The study measures the SAW transmission coefficient ($S_{21}$) spectrum while sweeping the external magnetic field, comparing results on the bilayer versus a bare substrate.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a Co/LiNbO3 hybrid bilayer. The interaction mechanism relies on magnetoelastic coupling between propagating Surface Acoustic Waves (SAWs) and the magnetization dynamics of the Co film.

**Key observables.** The enhanced transmitted acoustic signal ($\Delta S_{21}$) measured as a function of the applied magnetic field and SAW harmonic.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The SAW frequency must satisfy the Kittel dispersion relation for the Co film's FMR condition.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed enhancement is solely due to the dynamic, resonant interaction between propagating phonons and coherent magnetization precession.

**Figures summary.** Figures show the device schematic, the measured SAW transmission spectrum ($S_{21}$) highlighting the resonance, and comparisons showing that only the SAW harmonic matching the FMR condition exhibits the enhancement.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical setup, uses FMR measurements to predict the resonance condition (Kittel fit), and then measures the SAW transmission, demonstrating that the enhancement is localized precisely at the predicted resonance field and is frequency-selective.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report the first experimental observation of resonantly enhanced propagating phonon transport induced by magnon pumping in a ferromagnetic/piezoelectric bilayer. Surface acoustic waves (SAWs) generated in a 128$^{\circ}$ Y-cut LiNbO$_3$ delay-line device resonantly excite magnetization dynamics in an adjacent Co film through magnetoelastic coupling. The enhancement of the transmitted acoustic signal occurs exclusively when the SAW frequency satisfies the ferromagnetic resonance condition predicted by the Kittel dispersion, providing a direct experimental fingerprint of resonant magnon-phonon coupling. A systematic comparison between the Co/LiNbO$_3$ bilayer and the bare LiNbO$_3$ substrate demonstrates that the observed transmission enhancement originates solely from the dynamic interaction between propagating phonons and coherent magnetization precession. Furthermore, measurements performed at different SAW harmonics reveal that only the harmonic satisfying the FMR condition produces a measurable enhancement, confirming the frequency-selective nature of the phenomenon. These findings establish an efficient mechanism for transferring energy from magnons to propagating phonons and provide a new strategy for actively controlling coherent acoustic transport in hybrid spintronic, straintronic, and quantum phononic platforms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the speed of the virtual mediator boson in deep inelastic scattering by treating the interaction region as a dispersive medium. It develops a theoretical framework relating group and phase velocities and applies it to HERA data. The key finding is that the derived speed can exceed the speed of light and exhibits strong singularities as the virtual boson's energy approaches zero.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the core physics is high-energy particle physics, the methodology—using dispersion relations and velocity measurements to probe medium properties—shares conceptual overlap with how collective excitations are studied in condensed matter systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper proposes a novel method to measure the characteristics of a fermion-boson medium formed during deep inelastic electron-proton scattering by analyzing the group and phase velocities of the mediating wave process.

**Main result.** Analysis of HERA data suggests that the effective mediator boson's speed can exceed the speed of light in free space, and the derived model shows a strong, singular dependence on the virtual boson's energy ($q_0$) as $q_0$ approaches zero.

**Method.** The authors use the Rayleigh differential condition and derive expressions for group and phase velocities from a relativistic dispersion relation model, comparing these theoretical predictions against indirect measurements from HERA collider data.

**Model / system.** The physical system is deep inelastic $ep$ scattering, where the interaction is mediated by an effective, non-perturbative boson ($B$). The analysis utilizes a relativistic dispersion relation model ($\omega^2 = \omega_0^2 + c^2 \sum k_i^2$) to define the velocities.

**Key observables.** Group velocity modulus ($U^*_B$), Phase velocity ($V^*_B$), Normalized speed ($eta^*$), and the absorption mean free path ($L^*$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Kinematic variables like $Q^2$, Bjorken variable $x$, and the virtual boson energy $q_0$. The analysis focuses on the limits $q_0 	o 0$ and $y 	o 1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the applicability of the Rayleigh condition and relies on deriving velocity estimates from modified Heisenberg uncertainty relations and specific kinematic limits.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the kinematic view of the scattering process, the dependence of the normalized speed ($eta^*$) on $q_0$ (showing divergence near $q_0=0$), and the extracted $eta^*$ vs. $Q^2$ from HERA II data.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing the theoretical framework (velocity definitions, Rayleigh condition), applying this framework to the DIS process, deriving model predictions for $eta^*(q_0)$, and finally comparing these model results against actual HERA experimental data across various kinematic limits.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a new method for measuring several characteristics of a fermion-boson medium formed during deep inelastic particle scattering. This method involves measuring the group and phase velocities of the accompanying wave process. The phase and group velocities of a wave process are related by the Rayleigh differential condition. The refractive index of a medium is determined by the phase velocity, while the group velocity characterises the medium's dispersion properties. In the case of deep inelastic $ep$ scattering under consideration, the wave process in an individual event is understood to be the exchange between an electron and a proton of an effective, non-perturbative mediator of interaction (${\cal B}$) with the quantum numbers of a neutral current. In the first report, we present new data on the group velocity of interaction carriers ${\cal B}$. We extracted the group velocity modulus values, $U_{_{\cal B}}^*$, from the HERA collider data using a formula derived from modified Heisenberg uncertainty relations and applying the indirect measurement method.. etc...(see preprint text)

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the Thermodynamic Uncertainty Relations in chaotic Andreev billiards, a system exhibiting particle-hole symmetry. By using RMT, the authors test how quantum interference and barrier transparency affect the TUR across various symmetry classes. They confirm that the fundamental thermodynamic bound remains satisfied, establishing TUR as a powerful symmetry-sensitive diagnostic tool for superconducting quantum transport.

**Why it may be interesting.** The investigation of TUR in superconducting mesoscopic systems provides a unique, symmetry-sensitive probe of universal transport, linking quantum chaos, superconductivity, and fundamental thermodynamic laws.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how particle-hole symmetry, quantum interference, and tunnel barriers influence the Thermodynamic Uncertainty Relations (TUR) in chaotic Andreev billiards.

**Main result.** The standard TUR bound is consistently satisfied across all investigated regimes, confirming that Andreev billiards remain compatible with fundamental thermodynamic constraints.

**Method.** The research employs Random Matrix Theory (RMT) and the Mahaux–Weidenmüller scattering approach to analyze transport properties.

**Model / system.** The system is an open chaotic Andreev billiard, modeled using the Bogoliubov-de Gennes (BdG) Hamiltonian, which inherently possesses particle-hole symmetry.

**Key observables.** Charge conductance, shot noise power, and the TUR observable, $Q$, which is compared using two ensemble averages ($Q_R$ and $Q_{MS}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis spans multiple regimes: the extreme quantum limit ($N=1$) to the semiclassical multichannel regime ($N=10$), and varying barrier transparencies ($\Gamma$) from ideal to opaque.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the Random Matrix Theory framework and assumes operation within the universal RMT regime ($	au_\phi, 	au_D \gg 	au_E$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of conductance, noise, and the TUR observable on barrier transparency ($\Gamma$) for different symmetry classes and system sizes, showing the relative contributions of quantum interference.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the TUR across different symmetry classes and transport regimes, comparing two distinct definitions of the TUR observable to probe mesoscopic fluctuations and the role of quantum interference corrections.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate how particle-hole symmetry, quantum interference, and tunnel barriers shape thermodynamic uncertainty relations in chaotic Andreev billiards. Using random-matrix theory and the Mahaux-Weidenmüller scattering approach, we study charge conductance and shot noise across the four Altland-Zirnbauer symmetry classes, from the single-channel extreme quantum limit to the multichannel semiclassical regime and from ideal to opaque contacts. We characterize thermodynamic precision through two complementary ensemble observables: either by averaging the sample-resolved noise-to-conductance ratio, or from the separately averaging noise and conductance. Their pronounced discrepancy in the extreme quantum regime reveals the non-self-averaging character of mesoscopic transport and persists over a broad range of barrier transparencies. For ideal contacts, the first provides a sensitive fingerprint of the Altland-Zirnbauer symmetry class. In the opaque regime, this hierarchy changes. Despite these strong symmetry- and barrier-dependent effects, the standard thermodynamic uncertainty relation remains satisfied throughout all regimes investigated. Our results establish thermodynamic uncertainty as a symmetry-sensitive probe of universal transport in chaotic normal-superconducting systems.

</details>

### [Correlated quasiperiodicity enables efficient thermoelectric energy conversion](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28021v1)

**Authors:** Swaraj Biswas, Santanu K. Maiti  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** disordered systems and neural networks · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.28021v1>

**Summary.** This work proposes using correlated quasiperiodic nanostructures to boost thermoelectric energy conversion efficiency. By modeling a 1D chain with an AAH-type potential, the authors show that the resulting non-uniform electronic transmission spectrum is crucial. This engineered disorder allows for high ZT values, surpassing those achievable in simpler or perfectly periodic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The core mechanism relies on engineering the electronic density of states via quasiperiodicity, a concept deeply related to localization phenomena studied in disordered systems, making it relevant to understanding transport in complex, aperiodic materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates methods to enhance thermoelectric energy conversion efficiency in nanoscale systems by engineering the electronic energy landscape.

**Main result.** Correlated quasiperiodic potentials, specifically weakly varying Aubry-André-Harper (AAH) potentials, can induce highly non-uniform electronic transmission spectra, leading to a figure of merit (ZT) exceeding 2.

**Method.** The study employs the tight-binding framework combined with the non-equilibrium Green's function (NEGF) formalism and the Landauer approach to calculate transport coefficients.

**Model / system.** The model is a one-dimensional quantum chain coupled to source and drain reservoirs, featuring a modulated on-site energy potential belonging to the AAH family. The potential is characterized by an incommensurability parameter ($
u$).

**Key observables.** Figure of Merit (ZT), electrical conductance (G), Seebeck coefficient (S), electronic thermal conductance ($\kappa_e$), and phonon thermal conductance ($\kappa_{ph}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Incommensurability factor ($
u$), disorder amplitude ($W$), Fermi energy ($E_F$), and temperature ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed in the linear response regime, assuming small temperature differences ($\Delta T$), and includes detailed analysis of phonon contributions.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the electronic transmission spectrum ($	au(E)$) for perfect ($
u=0$) versus correlated ($
u=0.55$) lattices, showing the latter yields necessary asymmetry for high ZT. Other figures plot ZT vs. $E_F$, $
u$, and $T$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically introduces the Hamiltonian and methods (NEGF/Landauer). It compares the results for perfect vs. correlated disorder, demonstrating that the non-uniformity induced by correlated disorder is key to maximizing ZT. Finally, it analyzes the dependence of ZT on coupling asymmetry and temperature.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate a route to enhanced thermoelectric energy conversion in nanoscale systems by exploiting a correlated quasiperiodic energy landscape in a one-dimensional chain coupled to source and drain reservoirs. The considered modulation generates a highly non-uniform electronic transmission spectrum, providing favorable conditions for achieving a large thermoelectric figure of merit. By systematically tuning the incommensurability parameter, a variety of quasiperiodic configurations are explored, several of which yield high values of the figure of merit exceeding $2$. Electronic transport properties are evaluated within a tight-binding framework using the non-equilibrium Green's function formalism, while the thermoelectric coefficients, including electrical conductance, Seebeck coefficient, and electronic thermal conductance, are determined through the Landauer approach. The underlying quasiperiodic potential belongs to the Aubry-André-Harper (AAH) family and exhibits a weakly varying spatial profile, leading to transmission characteristics that are favorable for thermoelectric optimization. The influence of phonon thermal conductance on the overall energy-conversion efficiency is also analyzed in detail. For the sake of completeness, we also critically inspect the effect of conductor to electrode coupling and the coupling asymmetry on $ZT$. We also check the thermoelectric response of conventional AAH system and an elaborate comparison is made with our chosen quantum system. Our findings highlight the potential of correlated quasiperiodic nanostructures as promising candidates for efficient thermoelectric applications.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses angle- and field-dependent magnetic torque measurements on $2H$-AgNiO$_2$ to map out its complex magnetic phase diagram. By comparing these experimental results with theoretical simulations, the authors confirm the existence of multiple field-induced transitions, providing strong evidence for a magnetic supersolid phase in this anisotropic material.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides a concrete, measurable platform (magnetic torque) to probe exotic quantum phases like supersolids in a strongly correlated magnetic insulator, linking macroscopic measurements to microscopic spin models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the complex magnetic phase transitions, particularly the potential supersolid phase, in the triangular lattice antiferromagnet $2H$-AgNiO$_2$.

**Main result.** The angular and field-dependent magnetic torque measurements reveal characteristic sawtooth signals and multiple anomalies, which are qualitatively explained by crossing different predicted phase boundaries.

**Method.** The research combines detailed experimental measurements of magnetic torque ($	au$) with theoretical modeling using mean-field and Monte-Carlo simulations on a minimal spin model.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the delafossite $2H$-AgNiO$_2$, which features localized $S=1$ moments on a triangular lattice interacting with itinerant electrons. The theory uses a minimal easy-axis $J_1-J_2$ spin model.

**Key observables.** Magnetic torque ($	au$) as a function of angle ($	heta$) and applied field ($H$), and the identification of multiple field-induced anomalies ($H_1$ to $H_4$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Critical temperatures ($T_N \approx 19$ K), applied fields up to 45 T, and anisotropy parameters ($D/J_1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The theoretical analysis relies on a minimal spin model and semi-classical approximations, potentially neglecting quadrupolar moments or itinerant electron effects for certain anomalies.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the magnetic ordering (CAF, SS1, SS2, etc.) and show the evolution of the angular dependence of the torque ($	au$) at various temperatures and fields, highlighting kinks and anomalies corresponding to phase boundaries.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by presenting experimental torque measurements across varying fields and angles, correlating the observed anomalies with theoretical phase diagrams constructed via mean-field and Monte-Carlo simulations to map out the complex magnetic phase space.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Easy-axis frustrated triangular lattice antiferromagnets provide an important playground for stabilising a wide range of exotic magnetic phases. The delafossite $2H$-AgNiO$_2$ offers a unique model system containing a lattice of localised ($S=1$) moments surrounded by a honeycomb of itinerant electrons. Below $T_{\rm N} =19$ K, the system exhibits collinear stripe antiferromagnetic order, whereas applied magnetic fields induce a cascade of transitions which has been proposed to contain a magnetic supersolid phase. In this study, we perform detailed angular and field-dependent torque measurements in static fields of up to 45~T on single crystal samples. The angular dependence of the torque displays a sawtooth-shaped signal close to the first supersolid phase, while near $T_{\rm N}$, an unexpected additional modulation emerges. On the other hand, the field dependence of the torque indicates the presence of four field-induced anomalies which evolve in distinct ways as a function of field orientation. To understand these complex behaviours and assess the role of local moments, we employ mean-field and Monte-Carlo calculations on a minimal spin model and construct comprehensive polar phase diagrams at different temperatures. We find that the angular dependence of the torque can be understood in terms of crossing different phase boundaries by field rotation, and we identify qualitative similarities between the experimental and theoretical polar phase diagrams. Our study highlights $2H$-AgNiO$_2$ as a rich anisotropic system that provides a robust example of how spin supersolid orders can manifest in angle-dependent magnetic torque.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel discrete diffusion model utilizing Variational Autoregressive Networks (VAN) to accurately parameterize normalized probability distributions for spin systems. By applying this framework to the 2D and 3D Ising models, the authors successfully compute key thermodynamic quantities across different phases. This establishes a powerful, scalable neural-network approach for statistical mechanics simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** The integration of advanced deep learning (autoregressive networks) with established statistical physics tools (Ising models, MCMC) provides a powerful, generalizable computational framework for analyzing complex many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the need for a discrete diffusion model capable of parameterizing normalized probability distributions, overcoming limitations in both conventional score-based models and low-dimensional tensor-network approaches.

**Main result.** They introduce a framework using Variational Autoregressive Networks (VAN) that successfully models normalized distributions for spin systems on higher-dimensional lattices, allowing for accurate computation of thermodynamic quantities.

**Method.** The core method involves training a VAN to approximate the target Boltzmann distribution, governing the forward noising and reverse denoising dynamics via explicit Markov jump operators.

**Model / system.** The model is applied to spin systems, specifically the two- and three-dimensional Ising model, whose target distribution follows the Boltzmann distribution $P(\sigma) \propto e^{-eta E(\sigma)}$.

**Key observables.** Free energy, energy, and magnetization per site.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inverse temperature $eta$ (spanning ordered, critical, and disordered regimes); lattice dimensions (2D and 3D).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is the use of the variational autoregressive factorization $P_	heta(\sigma) = \prod_{i=1}^D P_	heta(\sigma_i | \sigma_1, \dots, \sigma_{i-1})$ to decompose the joint probability.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 contrasts the proposed normalized VAN model against standard unnormalized diffusion models. Figure 2 provides an overview of the framework, detailing training, denoising strategies, and MCMC integration.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework by defining the VAN-based discrete diffusion model, details the dynamics using Markov jump operators, applies it to the Ising model across various regimes, and integrates the method with MCMC sampling techniques.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Conventional score-based diffusion models learn scores without representing normalized densities, whereas tractable normalized models support both sampling and direct likelihood evaluation. A recent tensor-network approach provides such a representation but is largely restricted to low-dimensional lattices. Here we introduce a discrete diffusion model that parameterizes normalized probability distributions using variational autoregressive networks. Explicit Markov jump operators govern the forward noising and reverse denoising dynamics, extending discrete diffusion models with normalized distributions to spin systems on higher-dimensional lattices. We apply this framework to the two- and three-dimensional Ising models across ordered, critical, and disordered regimes, accurately computing thermodynamic quantities including free energy, energy, and magnetization. We further integrate the framework with Monte Carlo sampling, using adaptive diffusion steps to maintain high acceptance rates even at low temperatures while enhancing sample diversity. These results establish a neural-network framework for the discrete diffusion model with normalized probability distributions.

</details>

### [Free semigroup non-relativistic phase space states quantisation](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27004v1)

**Authors:** Georgii Koniukov

**Summary.** This paper proposes a novel quantization procedure for non-relativistic quantum mechanics by reformulating classical dynamics using a 'state language' based on free semigroups. It successfully derives the Schrödinger equation and the Born rule by analyzing the geometry of phase space states and path integral amplitudes. The work offers a mathematically rigorous, foundational approach to bridging classical and quantum descriptions.

**Why it may be interesting.** The deep mathematical restructuring of classical dynamics using semigroup theory and the derivation of fundamental quantum rules like the Born rule from first principles make it highly relevant for foundational quantum theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the quantization of non-relativistic phase space states by developing a formalism based on the free semigroup contribution of time, aiming to replace the principle of stationary action.

**Main result.** The formalism successfully derives the Lagrangian and the Schrödinger equation from a state-vector approach, and it derives the Born rule by comparing path integral amplitudes with density matrix calculations.

**Method.** The methodology involves introducing a 'state language/Dirac notation' for classical mechanics, dividing quantization into two stages (operator action and Planck scale consideration), and utilizing path integrals with time-slicing.

**Model / system.** The model is non-relativistic quantum mechanics applied to a general 1D particle system, utilizing phase space concepts, state vectors, and path integrals. The Hamiltonian structure is central to the analysis.

**Key observables.** The solid angle ($\Omega$) between states, the probability $p$ (for the Born rule), and the state vectors themselves ($|qangle, |pangle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Planck length ($\hbar$), the free semigroup time contribution, and the scale relative to $\hbar$ (quantum vs. classical limits).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The formalism assumes the structure of time contribution as a free semigroup and relies on specific conventions for state definitions and operator ordering.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Legendre transformation structure, the quantum limit of phase space states, and the quasi-classical limit of phase space states.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by establishing a state language for classical mechanics, deriving the Lagrangian, analyzing the solid angle in phase space, deriving the path integral propagator, and finally applying the formalism to derive the Born rule.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantisation is considered as an action of classical point operators on field states at the Planck scale of the phase space. Emphasis is placed on the sequence of physical variables. A "state language/Dirac notation" for classical mechanics is introduced; in many cases, it replaces the principle of stationary action for conservative systems. Observed classical and quantum dynamics are considered as different vectorisations of the free semigroup contribution of time. There is a dream of describing classical dynamics as complex transformations of state vectors over such a free semigroup, rather than solving differential equations. The transition from the alphabet to states, from states to state functions, and from state functions to measurable quantities is managed by projectors. An attempt is made to provide a derivation of the Legendre transformation. The trivial multiplication of the potential energy by the wave function in the coordinate representation and its non-trivial operator action in the momentum representation are obtained without a Fourier transform nor the Planck scale. The quantisation procedure is divided into two stages: first, the action of point-like classical operators on field functions; and second, accounting for the physically finite scale of the Planck length against the backdrop of the possible infinitesimality in the classical phase space. The concept of an angle between states is introduced, defining the measure of "quantumness". The free semigroup time contribution is studied in the context of the Born rule.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel geometric framework by mapping the Quantum Rabi Model (QRM) onto a curved nanowire system. This approach allows the authors to reveal a 'super-universality' in the adiabatic regime and provides a geometric interpretation for the QRM's quantum phase transition. The method offers a powerful tool for gaining deeper insights into light-matter interactions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges geometry (curved manifolds) with fundamental quantum physics (light-matter interaction), offering a powerful, generalizable framework for understanding complex quantum dynamics beyond simple approximations.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a unified, geometric perspective for the Quantum Rabi Model (QRM) to understand its universal properties and the nature of its quantum phase transitions (QPTs).

**Main result.** The geometric mapping reveals a 'super-universality' in the adiabatic regime and provides a geometric interpretation for the QPT via a changeover in the non-adiabatic spin texture.

**Method.** The authors map the QRM to a curved nanowire system, employing geometric analysis, local diagonalization, and semiclassical approximations to analyze the system's behavior.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on the Quantum Rabi Model (QRM), a fundamental model for light-matter interactions (LMIs). The analysis involves mapping this system onto a curved nanowire Hamiltonian that incorporates curvature-induced spin-orbit coupling (SOC).

**Key observables.** Super-universality of spin texture, changeover of tangential spin swing direction, spectral bifurcation, and spectral oscillations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** LMI strength ($g$), frequency ratio ($\omega/\Omega$), and the curvature of the nanowire geometry.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping the system to a curved geometry and utilizing approximations like the semiclassical limit in certain regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 compares adiabatic basis results with exact diagonalization for QPT analysis. Figure 2 illustrates the mapping of the QRM onto a curved nanowire system, showing paths dependent on coupling and frequency.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the QRM, develops the geometric mapping to a curved nanowire, analyzes the adiabatic and non-adiabatic regimes to find universal scaling laws, and interprets the QPT through geometric non-adiabaticity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum Rabi model (QRM) is a fundamental model in light-matter interactions (LMIs), whereas this finite-component system manifests infinite physics to be explored and a full understanding has not yet been reached after a hundred years since it was proposed. In the present work we create a geometric perspective for the QRM by mapping to a curved nanowire system, with curvature-induced spin-orbit coupling (SOC) manipulatable by LMI strength and frequency ratio, which is capable of revealing and perceiving the property universality and diversity from the ground state to the whole spectrum in a unified picture. Indeed, we unveil a super university in the adiabatic regime which manifests a universal scaling of spin texture ubiquitously valid for different couplings, different frequencies and different energy levels. The curve frame also facilitates our finding that a direction changeover of tangential spin swing emerges in the non-adiabatic spin texture in association with the quantum phase transition (QPT) in the QRM, which endows a geometric non-adiabaticity interpretation and connotation to the QPT. We also clarify that, among the curvature-induced effects, the SOC accounts for the spectral bifurcation and oscillations. Our method and analysis, readily generalized to other light-matter models, opens a dialogue between geometry and physics, thus paving a novel way to gain deeper insight into LMIs.

</details>

**Summary.** This work proposes a hybrid quantum-classical framework for image compression, combining Variational Quantum Wavelet Transforms and Quantum CNNs for quantum feature extraction. The quantum latent representation is then refined using a classical Super-Resolution GAN, achieving state-of-the-art perceptual quality metrics. It demonstrates the potential of quantum computation to enhance classical signal processing tasks.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on image processing, the reliance on variational quantum circuits (V-QDWT, QCNN) for feature extraction and dimensionality reduction provides a concrete, near-term application of quantum machine learning algorithms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of efficient and high-fidelity image compression and decompression by integrating quantum computational techniques with classical deep learning models.

**Main result.** The hybrid quantum-classical framework achieves superior structural fidelity, with the final reconstruction significantly surpassing baseline quantum methods by incorporating a classical Super-Resolution GAN.

**Method.** The process involves encoding images into quantum states (NASS), performing multi-resolution analysis using a Variational Quantum Daubechies Wavelet Transform (V-QDWT), and feature reduction via a trainable Quantum Convolutional Neural Network (QCNN).

**Model / system.** The core model is a hybrid quantum-classical pipeline tested on NISQ hardware (IBM Fez) and simulated. It utilizes quantum circuits for state preparation, wavelet transforms, and convolutional layers, followed by classical SRGAN refinement.

**Key observables.** PSNR (30.0667 dB), SSIM (0.8744), and Histogram Correlation (0.9244) are key performance metrics used to validate reconstruction quality.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The compression ratio is mathematically defined (e.g., 4:1), and the process involves mapping pixel intensities to quantum rotation angles ($\phi_i$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes the feasibility of implementing inverse QCNN and QDWT circuits, and that classical SRGAN post-processing is necessary to restore perceptual quality lost during quantum compression.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the overall hybrid framework schematic, quantum circuits for NASS state preparation and wavelet transforms, and comparisons showing the improvement from base quantum reconstruction to SRGAN-refined output.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the encoding (NASS), the quantum compression pipeline (V-QDWT $ightarrow$ QCNN), the decompression pipeline (Inverse QCNN $ightarrow$ IQDWT $ightarrow$ SRGAN), and includes ablation studies comparing quantum vs. classical components.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This paper presents a hybrid quantum-classical framework for grayscale image compression and decompression, leveraging the strengths of quantum computing and deep learning. The compression pipeline integrates a Variational Quantum Daubechies Wavelet Transform (V-QDWT) and a trainable Quantum Convolutional Neural Network (QCNN) optimized end-to-end to achieve efficient, image-adaptive multi-resolution analysis and entanglement-based feature reduction. Input images are encoded using the Normal Arbitrary Superposition State (NASS) representation, enabling compact and scalable quantum storage. For decompression, we implement inverse QCNN and V-QDWT circuits to reconstruct coarse image features natively, followed by a classical Super-Resolution Generative Adversarial Network (SRGAN) to enhance perceptual quality. Experimental evaluations on benchmark grayscale datasets demonstrate the efficacy of our hybrid approach. By jointly training the quantum layers, the base quantum pipeline closely rivals classical JPEG2000 standards. Subsequent SRGAN refinement substantially pushes the boundaries of the reconstruction, achieving superior structural fidelity (PSNR: 30.0667 dB, SSIM: 0.8744, Histogram Correlation: 0.9244). Histogram analysis and qualitative comparisons further validate the restoration of fine textures and intensity distributions. Our findings highlight the potential of combining variational quantum compression with classical deep learning to enable efficient, scalable, and perceptually-aware image processing in quantum-enhanced computing environments.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.26912v1>

**Summary.** This paper provides a detailed theoretical analysis of how laser phase noise affects the fidelity of the Mølmer-Sørensen gate in trapped-ion quantum computing. The authors derive analytical expressions for the gate error by separating the noise into 'fast' and 'slow' spectral components. This work offers essential quantitative guidelines for improving laser spectral purity in quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial, detailed analytical tools for quantifying decoherence sources specific to trapped-ion quantum gates, which is vital for designing fault-tolerant quantum hardware.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper analyzes how laser phase noise degrades the fidelity of the Mølmer-Sørensen (MS) entangling gate used in trapped-ion quantum computing.

**Main result.** The gate error is shown to be determined by the convolution of the laser noise Power Spectral Density (PSD) with analytically derived filter functions, which are separated into 'fast' and 'slow' noise regimes.

**Method.** The authors perform a comprehensive theoretical analysis, deriving noise Hamiltonians and calculating the average gate error using the Dyson series formalism, leading to analytical expressions for the error.

**Model / system.** The system is trapped-ion quantum computing utilizing the Mølmer-Sørensen gate. The analysis models the influence of intrinsic laser phase noise on the qubit dynamics.

**Key observables.** Gate Fidelity and the average gate error ($\epsilon$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Laser noise Power Spectral Density (PSD), gate time ($	au$), and spectral frequency ranges ('fast' near motional spectrum, 'slow' near inverse gate time).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the noise is intrinsic phase noise of the common laser source, and it utilizes approximations valid in the Lamb-Dicke regime.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the MS gate level scheme with four Raman paths, and Figure 2 shows how the laser field PSD overlaps with system energy levels, distinguishing between 'slow' and 'fast' noise components.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the MS gate and the source of noise, to deriving the noise Hamiltonians for different geometries, calculating the error using the Dyson series, and finally presenting analytical expressions for the error in both fast and slow noise regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

High-fidelity two-qubit entangling gates are essential for the realization of useful quantum algorithms on quantum processors. The Mølmer-Sørensen (MS) gate has become a common choice for trapped-ion quantum computing due to its resilience to ion temperature and its demonstrated record fidelities. However, the spectral impurity of the driving laser field impacts gate performance, with phase noise influencing the qubit dynamics through mechanisms operating on different timescales. In this work, we present a comprehensive theoretical analysis of laser phase noise in the MS gate, identifying two spectral ranges which influence the gate fidelity the most: "fast" noise at frequencies near the motional mode spectrum, and "slow" noise at frequencies on the order of the inverse gate time. We derive the noise Hamiltonians for two common laser beam geometries and obtain analytical expressions for the average gate error in terms of the laser noise power spectral density and gate parameters. For slowly varying noise spectra, we provide simplified error estimates. In addition, we validate our findings against previously published numerical simulations.

</details>

### [Efficient Learning of Clifford-Scrambled Product States](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27128v1)

**Authors:** Tobias Haug  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.27128v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **2/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.27128_figures/2609.27128_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Learning product subsystems hidden by a Clifford circuit. (a) The unknown Clifford C spreads the input blocks across the system and can generate volume-law entanglement. (b) We measure pairs of copies in the Bell basis. Affine equa- tions identify Pauli stabilizers, and quadratic equations on the residual samples determine the hidden subsystems. Applying the learned Clifford decoder D† to fresh copies disentangles the subsystems, followed by tomography of each block.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work presents a method to efficiently determine the underlying product structure of quantum states that have been scrambled by Clifford circuits. By analyzing quadratic relations derived from two-copy Bell sampling, the authors can pinpoint the hidden independent subsystems. This advances the field by providing scalable measurement-based techniques for characterizing complex quantum entanglement structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** The techniques developed for state tomography and structure recovery using limited measurements (Bell samples) are highly relevant for characterizing complex quantum states generated in quantum computation or quantum simulation settings.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of efficiently recovering the hidden product structure of quantum states that have been scrambled by a global Clifford circuit.

**Main result.** The hidden product structure can be efficiently recovered using two-copy Bell sampling by analyzing the quadratic relations satisfied by the samples, which determine unique irreducible symplectic subspaces.

**Method.** The core method involves using two-copy Bell sampling to generate quadratic equations, which are then analyzed via polarization to extract bilinear forms that identify the independent product subsystems.

**Model / system.** The system under study consists of $n$-qubit pure states prepared by applying a global Clifford circuit to independent product blocks, often analyzed via their Choi states or Bell samples.

**Key observables.** Clifford-product width ($w_{CP}(\psi)$), quadratic relations satisfied by Bell samples, and the gap ($\gamma_2$) between the state and the Haar random ensemble.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The required number of Bell samples scales polynomially, often $O(n^2)$, for both exact and approximate learning.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Approximate learning relies on the state remaining sufficiently separated from acquiring additional Pauli stabilizers, and exact recovery depends on the gap $\gamma_2$ being large enough.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the process: (a) a Clifford-scrambled product state, and (b) the recovery process using Bell samples where affine equations identify stabilizers and quadratic equations determine hidden subsystems.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general framework using two-copy Bell sampling to deriving theorems that classify the product structure using quadratic forms (Theorem 1). It then quantifies the sample complexity for exact recovery (Theorem 2) and extends this to approximate learning (Theorem 4), concluding with distinguishing these states from Haar random states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Clifford circuits acting on product magic states provide a compact ansatz exhibiting extensive magic, volume-law entanglement, and even classically hard sampling under standard complexity assumptions. Here, we efficiently recover its hidden product subsystems from two-copy Bell sampling. Quadratic relations between Bell samples determine the irreducible blocks after removing Pauli stabilizers, and binary linear algebra constructs a Clifford disentangler. For logarithmic-size blocks, approximate learning of the full state is efficient whenever the state remains inverse-polynomially separated from acquiring additional Pauli stabilizers. We efficiently learn $n$-qubit states prepared by random $T$-doped Clifford circuits with $T$-gate density below one via $O(n^2)$ Bell samples, and disentangle hidden product blocks in Clifford-augmented matrix product states. For unitary learning, we exactly learn $T$-depth-one circuits using $O(n^2)$ queries. Finally, we rule out pseudorandom states and unitaries with a product bipartition hidden by Clifford circuits.

</details>

**Summary.** This work tackles the computationally difficult problem of simulating the 2D Majorana-Hubbard model at finite hopping using advanced Quantum Monte Carlo methods based on Pfaffians. The authors successfully remove the sign problem for a significant parameter range and find that the system remains a gapless semimetal, confirming theoretical predictions about its electronic structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous application of advanced QMC techniques (Pfaffians) to a model featuring Majorana fermions and emergent symmetries provides deep insights into topological phases relevant to condensed matter physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is performing nonperturbative lattice calculations for the two-dimensional Majorana-Hubbard model at finite hopping, which was previously limited to zero hopping.

**Main result.** The simulations consistently show that the system remains in a gapless semimetal phase across the sign-problem-minimal parameter window, confirming the expected behavior.

**Method.** Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) simulations are employed using novel Hamiltonian fermion bags, which utilize Pfaffians derived from a continuous-time expansion to manage the sign problem.

**Model / system.** The system is the two-dimensional Majorana-Hubbard model defined on a $\pi$-flux lattice, involving one relativistic Majorana fermion per site. The Hamiltonian includes hopping terms ($t$) and interaction terms ($g$).

**Key observables.** Dimerization correlators (charged doublet vs. neutral singlet), the average sign, and the free-fermion spectrum $\epsilon(\mathbf{k})$ showing Dirac points.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strengths ($g/t$), hopping parameter ($t$), and lattice size/inverse temperature ($eta=L/4$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on the Pfaffian calculation being stable and successfully removing the sign problem for couplings $g \lesssim 0.7$ and specific $eta$ values.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the $\pi$-flux lattice structure, the free-fermion spectrum with Dirac points, and the evolution of the average sign and correlation functions as a function of interaction strength $g/t$.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the model and the computational difficulty (sign problem), details the Hamiltonian fermion bag QMC method using Pfaffians, presents results showing sign-problem removal, and concludes by analyzing the symmetry and gapless nature of the resulting semimetal.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The two-dimensional Majorana-Hubbard model on the $π$-flux lattice involves the most local interaction possible for a lattice with one relativistic Majorana fermion per site. It is predicted to host a Majorana semimetal, dimerized phases, and quantum-critical points with an emergent $U(1)$ symmetry. Nonperturbative lattice calculations for this model remain a major open challenge, thus far limited to zero hopping. We present quantum Monte Carlo simulations of the model at finite hopping using Hamiltonian fermion bags, which are based on a continuous-time expansion with Boltzmann weights that are Pfaffians of a matrix with dimension set by the expansion order, and which give the sign of the weight. Sign problem severity is related to the location of zero modes in the free hopping matrix, and for couplings $g\lesssim 0.7$ and $β=L/4$ it is fully removed for lattices up to at least $256$ sites. The interaction strength $g>0$ enhances the $U(1)$-charged dimerization doublet and suppresses the neutral channel, as the renormalization group predicts. Increasing the interactions also restores $U(1)$ symmetry in the component of the dimerization correlator that is even under bond orientation exchange, but not its odd component. There is a persistent anisotropy in the odd component with the symmetry of a lattice nematic. The data is consistent with a gapless semimetal throughout the sign-problem-minimal window.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the robustness of topological quantization in a disordered 3D axion pump. The authors show that the second Chern number ($	ext{Ch}_2$) remains quantized even when the system's spectral gap closes, a phenomenon distinct from the behavior of the Středa-Hall response. This demonstrates a robust topological protection mechanism surviving deep into the mobility-gap regime.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of mobility-gap protection for topological invariants is highly relevant to understanding robust quantum states in realistic, disordered condensed matter systems, which is a key area for quantum simulation and topological quantum computing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the robustness of the quantized second Chern number ($	ext{Ch}_2$) in a disordered, dynamically pumped axion insulator, specifically comparing its protection mechanism to the Středa-Hall response ($\partial\phi_{	ext{Ch}0,3}$).

**Main result.** The second Chern number ($	ext{Ch}_2$) remains quantized well into the mobility-gap regime beyond the global spectral gap closure, while the Středa-Hall response rapidly departs, demonstrating a distinct form of topological protection.

**Method.** The study employs large-scale numerical computations, including the Kernel Polynomial Method (KPM) for calculating topological invariants and analyzing level statistics to detect Anderson transitions.

**Model / system.** The system is a (3+1)D disordered axion pump, modeled as a stack of bilayer Haldane sheets with $	au$-dependent interlayer coupling. A 1D counterpart (Rice–Mele model) is also analyzed for contrast.

**Key observables.** Second Chern number ($	ext{Ch}_2$), Středa-Hall response ($\partial\phi_{	ext{Ch}0,3}$), Spectral Gap ($\Delta$), Level-spacing ratio ($\langle r angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Disorder strength ($W$), System size ($L$), Time/cycle parameter ($	au$), Critical disorder ($W_c \approx 18$), Global spectral gap closure ($W_g \approx 14.5$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximations like the Kernel Polynomial Method (KPM) to handle large system sizes, and it distinguishes between gap-dependent and mobility-gap protection mechanisms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison of $	ext{Ch}_2$ and $\partial\phi_{	ext{Ch}0,3}$ across disorder strengths, showing the separation beyond the spectral gap closure ($W_g$) and the identification of the mobility-edge bottleneck ($W_c$).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the model and key diagnostics, then analyzes the behavior of $	ext{Ch}_2$ and $\partial\phi_{	ext{Ch}0,3}$ relative to the spectral gap closure ($W_g$). It then uses level statistics to pinpoint the mobility-edge bottleneck ($W_c$), contrasting the 3D robustness with the breakdown observed in a 1D model.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We show that the noncommutative second Chern number of a $(3+1)$D disordered axion pump remains quantized beyond global spectral gap closure at $W_g \approx 14.5$. Large-scale computation of the second Chern number and finite-size scaling demonstrate convergence of $\mathrm{Ch}_2$ toward $-1$ well into the mobility-gap regime ($W \lesssim 16.5$), while the Středa-Hall response $\partial_φ\mathrm{Ch}_{0,3}$ departs rapidly after global spectral gap closure, serving as an internal control that distinguishes bulk mobility-gap protection from finite-size artifacts. Level statistics identify a 3D unitary Anderson transition at each pump slice and locate the mobility-edge bottleneck at $τ=0$, with critical disorder $W_c \approx 18$.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper proves the quantum soundness of a specific quantum test called the total-degree diagonal line-versus-point test. It achieves this by mathematically reducing the complex total-degree test to a known individual-degree soundness theorem. The work is significant for establishing the security and reliability of quantum protocols based on polynomial constraints.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the techniques used for bounding quantum correlations and establishing soundness in quantum testing protocols are foundational to quantum information theory and cryptography.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to prove the quantum soundness of the total-degree diagonal line-versus-point test.

**Main result.** It establishes the quantum soundness of this test by reducing it to an existing individual-degree soundness theorem, although the resulting bound retains a polynomial dependence on the dimension $m$.

**Method.** The proof employs a random change of coordinates and utilizes a reduction strategy that leverages the individual-degree soundness theorem, combined with bounding techniques for high-degree outcomes.

**Model / system.** The analysis is set up in a two-prover quantum test scenario involving measurements on a shared entangled state $|\psiangle$. The test itself is the total-degree diagonal line-vs-point test.

**Key observables.** Inconsistency errors ($
u_{	ext{tot}}, 
u_{	ext{ind}}$) for projective measurements ($	ext{eGA}, 	ext{eGB}$) on the shared quantum state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Field size $q$, dimension $m$, degree bound $d$, and test failure probability $\epsilon$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof assumes $q$ is sufficiently large relative to $md$ for the main theorems to hold, and the reduction process relies on the existence of specific invertible linear maps $T$.

**Paper structure.** The structure involves applying the individual-degree theorem via a random change of coordinates, followed by bounding the contribution of high-degree outcomes using the uniform-line slice, and culminating in the final soundness bound.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove quantum soundness of the total-degree diagonal line-vs-point test using the individual-degree soundness theorem of Ji, Natarajan, Vidick, Wright, and Yuen. A random change of coordinates yields projective polynomial decoders of total degree at most $md$. The uniform-line slice of the test bounds the weight of outcomes of degree greater than $d$, which are removed by a common relabeling. This reduction does not yield a dimension-independent soundness bound: the $\operatorname{poly}(m)$ dependence of the individual-degree theorem persists, as discussed in Section 1.2 of arXiv:2009.12982.

</details>

### [Quantum Topological Data Analysis Beyond Betti Numbers: Complexity Hardness $\&$ An Algorithm for Torsion Witness](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28112v1)

**Authors:** Nhat A. Nghiem, Dominic W. Berry, Trung V. Phan  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.28112v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **2/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.28112_figures/2609.28112_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Torsion-free and torsion-bearing manifolds. Torsion is detected in the homology classes of closed cycles. (A) An annulus with a noncontractible Z-cycle. (B) A Klein bottle with a Z-cycle and a Z2-cycle γ. Although γ is homologically nontrivial, traversing it twice yields a cycle homologous to zero. The fundamental polygon on the right shows the edge identifications responsible for this Z2-torsion.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper advances quantum TDA by moving beyond simple connectivity measures (Betti numbers) to analyze the computationally difficult torsion structure of homology groups. It proves this detection problem is NP-hard and presents a quantum algorithm that achieves a near-quadratic speedup for detecting this crucial, discrete topological information.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between algebraic topology (homology/torsion) and computational complexity, coupled with a quantum speedup claim, provides a new, hard-to-compute diagnostic tool for quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates extending Topological Data Analysis (TDA) from Betti numbers to include the full integral homology structure, specifically the torsion subgroup, which captures global constraints.

**Main result.** It proves that determining the presence of $p$-torsion in the homology group is NP-hard, and develops a quantum algorithm achieving a near-quadratic speedup to witness this torsion.

**Method.** The work combines complexity theory (NP-hardness proofs via reduction) with quantum algorithm design, utilizing techniques like the Quantum Singular Value Transformation (QSVT) framework.

**Model / system.** The mathematical model is a simplicial complex $K$ derived from a graph $G$, whose homology groups $H_r(K, \mathbb{Z})$ are analyzed. Physical relevance is drawn from homological quantum rotor codes and gauge theory.

**Key observables.** Betti numbers ($eta_r$) and the torsion subgroup ($	ext{Tor}_r(K, \mathbb{Z})$), which quantifies discrete topological information.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The prime $p$ and the set of primes $P$ used in the quantum algorithm are key parameters. The complexity analysis depends on the size of the complex and the number of primes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The quantum algorithm provides a one-sided witness (WITNESS or INCONCLUSIVE) and does not fully determine the torsion structure, only its presence for specific primes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the difference between torsion-free and torsion-bearing manifolds (e.g., Annulus vs. Klein bottle) in the context of homology classes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the NP-hardness of detecting $p$-torsion in homology groups. It then develops a quantum algorithm to act as a one-sided torsion witness, followed by detailed mathematical sections on finite field arithmetic and reversible quantum circuit construction.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Recent advances have revealed an interplay between quantum computing and topological data analysis (TDA). Most quantum TDA has focused on Betti numbers, which characterize the connectivity and ``holes'' of a dataset. Homology, however, contains additional information in the form of torsion: a nontrivial cycle can become trivial after being repeated finitely, revealing global constraints on how cycles combine and wrap around one another. Beyond applications in biomolecular studies, torsion appears in physical settings including homological quantum rotor codes, discrete charges, and gauge sectors. We study torsion from both classical and quantum perspectives. Given a graph $G$ and its clique complex $K = \mathrm{Cl}(G)$, we first prove that, for fixed $r$ and prime $p$, deciding whether $H_r(K,\mathbb{Z})$ contains $p$-torsion is NP-hard. As a corollary, when a homological rotor code is specified by $G$, deciding whether the code has a finite-dimensional logical sector of a given order is NP-hard. We discuss related problems, including the Bockstein homomorphism, Smith normal form, lattice saturation, and cohomology. Second, for a finite set of primes $P$, we develop a quantum algorithm that serves as a one-sided torsion witness. For fixed $r$, it outputs WITNESS or INCONCLUSIVE, i.e. WITNESS certifies that either $H_r(K,\mathbb{Z})$ or $H_{r-1}(K,\mathbb{Z})$ contains $p$-torsion for some $p\in P$ while INCONCLUSIVE makes no claim about its presence or absence. We identify a regime in which the algorithm achieves a near-quadratic quantum speedup over the corresponding classical algorithm under the same input model. Our NP-hardness result complements recent hardness results for estimating Betti numbers, adding a complexity-theoretic perspective to quantum TDA. Together, these results demonstrate that integral homology, beyond its Betti numbers, can be computationally challenging.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes how Gaussian quantum states behave when encoded into the GKP logical subspace using the stabilizer subsystem decomposition. The authors derive geometric bounds showing that these states can exhibit non-stabilizer properties, which they use to create novel certification tools. These tools allow for quantifying resources like 'set-magic' and certifying cryptographic randomness based on measurable squeezing levels.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous mathematical framework connecting continuous-variable resource theory (squeezing, Gaussian states) to discrete-variable quantum information concepts (stabilizer codes, magic states, randomness certification).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes the logical set of Gaussian states when mapped into the Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill (GKP) logical subspace via the stabilizer subsystem decomposition (SSD). It aims to derive practical certification tools based on this geometric understanding.

**Main result.** The logical image of single-mode Gaussian states can violate the stabilizer bound, certifying a resource called "set-magic." Furthermore, violating the classical bound allows for the certification of cryptographic randomness at moderate squeezing levels.

**Method.** The authors derive exact series expressions for the logical Bloch vector components and analyze the resulting reachable set geometry. They apply this framework by mapping discrete-variable witnesses to continuous-variable squeezing witnesses.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on continuous-variable (CV) bosonic systems, specifically single-mode Gaussian states, and their behavior when subjected to the GKP logical subspace extraction channel (SSD).

**Key observables.** Logical Bloch vector components (X, Y, Z), robustness of magic $R(1)(ho_L)$, and the conditional min-entropy $H_{\min}(B|E)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Squeezing parameter ($r$), displacement ($\mathbf{d}$), and the time/witness parameter ($t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the ideal GKP error correction and the faithful SSD extraction channel. Approximations are used for the maximum robustness and worst-case entropy calculations.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the XZ projections of the logical state set, comparing the exact boundary to leading-harmonic approximations, and show the relationship between various bounds and the certified min-entropy.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first characterizing the logical set geometry, deriving series expressions for the Bloch vector, and establishing the relationship between Gaussian inputs and the stabilizer polytope. It then applies this geometry to develop certification tools for set-magic and cryptographic randomness.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Universal quantum computation requires non-Gaussianity in continuous-variable systems and non-stabilizerness in discrete-variable systems. Yet, mapping a Gaussian state into the Gottesman-Kitaev-Preskill logical subspace can yield a logical qubit with non-stabilizer resources. To understand how the continuous-variable structure determines logical resources, we characterize the image of single-mode Gaussian states under the corresponding extraction channel, known as the stabilizer subsystem decomposition. Specifically, we derive series expressions for the logical Bloch vector and characterize the resulting reachable set, enabling an analytical treatment of the robustness of magic of the logical states. We then turn this geometric framework into a certification tool: by mapping a discrete-variable qubit witness to a continuous-variable squeezing witness, we obtain lower bounds on the squeezing of prepared states. We also show that ensembles of Gaussian states can violate both stabilizer and classical bounds. Violation of the stabilizer bound certifies the relational resource known as "set-magic," while violation of the classical bound enables the certification of cryptographic randomness at moderate squeezing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles deep foundational questions regarding what constitutes a 'physical' state in quantum theory, especially when operators are unbounded. The author develops a hierarchical view of physicality, arguing that physical reality is not monolithic but layered. This framework is used to critique existing arguments and to provide a potential resolution for interpretational problems in Quantum Field Theory.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the development of a 'hierarchical view of physicality' provides a novel meta-framework for interpreting foundational inconsistencies arising in advanced quantum theories, which is relevant to understanding the limits of current quantum descriptions.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper tackles the foundational issue of the physicality of the Hilbert space in quantum mechanics, particularly when dealing with unbounded operators and defining the set of physical states.

**Main result.** The author proposes a hierarchical view of physicality, arguing that physicality is layered and admits degrees, which helps reconcile foundational issues in quantum field theory.

**Method.** The methodology involves distinguishing between ontological/modal and pragmatic/operational readings of physicality, and applying modal metaphysics to quantum structures.

**Model / system.** The discussion centers on standard quantum mechanics axioms, the position and Hamiltonian operators, and extends to the algebraic structures of Quantum Field Theory (QFT) involving the Weyl algebra.

**Key observables.** Position operator expectation values ($\langle x angle_\psi$), probability distributions of measurement results, and the structure of physical states defined by normal states in QFT.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The distinction between potential and actual infinities, and the nature of singularities versus the existence of infinite expectation values.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The paper assumes that the difficulties raised by restricting the state space (e.g., by Heathcote or Carcassi et al.) are artifacts of an overly rigid interpretation of physical axioms.

**Figures summary.** The figures illustrate the hierarchy of logical, metaphysical, and physical possible worlds, suggesting multiple, nested structures of physical possibility.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first addressing the Hilbert space physicality problem with unbounded operators, then applying this framework to critique arguments regarding infinite expectation values in position measurements, and finally extending the hierarchical view to resolve interpretational issues in QFT.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper, I address the problem regarding the physicality of Hilbert space in quantum mechanics, a topic brought into focus by Carcassi et al.'s paper "The unphysicality of Hilbert spaces" recently. I argue for the physicality of the Hilbert space in quantum mechanics, and demonstrate that there are inevitable ambiguity and arbitrariness if we want to identify the set of physical states with a proper subset of it. Moreover, I distinguish the ontological and modal reading of physicality from the pragmatic and operational reading of physicality, and then develop what I call the hierarchical view of physicality. According to this view, physicality is not a monolithic concept, but a layered one that admits degrees. I show the connection between the concept of physicality and modal metaphysics, and finally explain how it can be used to address the interpretational problem of quantum field theory brought by the existence of inequivalent representations.

</details>

<sub>Fig. 4(b) exhibits rich behavior of the MFPT. First, as α  0, the MFPT converges to τr regardless of its value. This is not a coincidence. In this limit, the Mittag-Leffler distribution becomes increasingly concentrated at two extremes: rate zero and rate infinity. The probability of sampling either converges to half. Thus, sampling a rate is analogous to flipping a coin: heads for rate zero; tails for rate infinity. When we flip tails (rate infinity), the walker reaches the target in zero time. This occurs on average after two flips. As we first flip at t = 0, and then again every τr, the second flip is at t = τr. Therefore, the MFPT is τr. A detailed derivation of this result is provided...</sub>
