**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes a random walk whose jump rate fluctuates stochastically. It discovers a critical transition point: if the rate fluctuations are strong enough, the mean time to reach a target site becomes finite, overcoming the natural divergence seen in simple random walks. This demonstrates that temporal noise can fundamentally regularize spatial transport dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of temporal fluctuations regularizing otherwise divergent spatial transport times is highly relevant to open quantum systems, anomalous diffusion in disordered media, and quantum transport where noise sources are inherent.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how stochastic fluctuations in the jump rate of a random walk fundamentally alter its mean first-passage time (MFPT), potentially causing a transition from an infinite to a finite mean.

**Main result.** A phase transition occurs at a critical fluctuation parameter ($\alpha_c = 1/2$): when fluctuations are sufficiently broad ($\alpha < 1/2$), the MFPT becomes finite, whereas weaker fluctuations ($\alpha \ge 1/2$) leave it infinite.

**Method.** The analysis uses the framework of the Doubly Stochastic Continuous-Time Random Walk (DSCTRW), employing Laplace transforms and asymptotic analysis of the survival probability $S(t|x_0)$.

**Model / system.** The model is a simple symmetric random walk on a 1D lattice, generalized to a DSCTRW where the jump rate ($\lambda_t$) itself is a stochastic process, specifically resampled from a Mittag-Leffler distribution.

**Key observables.** Mean First Passage Time (MFPT) and the Survival Probability $S(t|x_0)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Mittag-Leffler distribution parameter $\alpha$, which dictates the rate fluctuation strength; the critical value $\alpha_c = 1/2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The jump rate is assumed to be resampled every fixed time interval $	au_r$, and the analysis relies on asymptotic power-law decay of the survival probability.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the DSCTRW process, the asymptotic power-law decay of the survival probability $S(t|x_0) \propto t^{-1/2\alpha}$, and the phase transition of the MFPT versus the fluctuation parameter $\alpha$ and relaxation time $	au_r$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the mathematical machinery for the DSCTRW, derives the asymptotic decay of the survival probability using Laplace transforms, and then analyzes the resulting MFPT to pinpoint the critical transition point governed by the rate fluctuation parameter $\alpha$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A simple symmetric random walk on a one-dimensional lattice is guaranteed to reach any target site, yet its mean first-passage time diverges. Here, we show that stochastic fluctuations in the jump rate can fundamentally alter this classic result. We find a phase transition in the mean first-passage time: sufficiently broad rate fluctuations render it finite, whereas weaker fluctuations leave it infinite. Our results demonstrate that temporal fluctuations in the jump rate---and hence in the diffusivity---can overcome spatial wandering and regularize otherwise divergent first-passage times.

</details>

**Summary.** This experimental study uses trARPES on a magnetic topological insulator to investigate light-induced exciton condensation. By observing a transient state with a characteristic Mexican-hat dispersion and long lifetime, the authors provide strong evidence for a metastable, BCS-like excitonic condensate. This work establishes magnetic topological insulators as a promising platform for studying correlated many-body physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work bridges concepts from topological physics (TSS) with strongly correlated many-body physics (excitonic condensation), providing an experimental platform to study non-equilibrium correlated states in novel materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to find direct evidence for a light-induced exciton condensate (EC) state within the bulk of a quantum material.

**Main result.** The authors observed spectral signatures, including a Mexican-hat-like quasiparticle dispersion and a microsecond lifetime, consistent with the formation of a metastable BCS-type excitonic condensate.

**Method.** Time- and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (trARPES) was employed to probe the evolution of photoexcited carriers over time delays.

**Model / system.** The physical system is an intrinsic magnetic topological insulator, specifically MnBi2Te4 doped with Sb to tune the chemical potential into the topological surface state. The analysis focuses on the many-body physics of photoexcited electron-hole pairs.

**Key observables.** Quasiparticle dispersion shape (Mexican-hat profile), transient state lifetime (microsecond scale), Exciton Binding Energy (EB), and BCS gap ($\Delta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (110 K vs 300 K), doping concentration (Sb substitution), and time delay ($\Delta t$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed spectral signatures are interpreted as evidence for a metastable excitonic condensate state, and the analysis relies on fitting procedures like Lorentzian functions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the evolution of Energy-Distribution Curves (EDCs) showing peak flattening and the development of the Mexican-hat shape over time, alongside comparisons of spectra taken through and away from the proposed EC state.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the experimental setup and tuning the material into the topological surface state, then presenting time-resolved measurements showing the delayed emergence of a transient state, and finally analyzing the spectral features (dispersion, gap opening) to confirm the BCS-like nature of the condensate.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Exciton condensates are long-sought correlated quantum states arising from macroscopic coherence of bound electron-hole pairs. Although equilibrium and transient excitonic states have been reported in several material platforms, direct evidence for a light-induced exciton condensate state has been challenging to achieve as an intrinsic property of a bulk quantum material. Here, we use time- and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy to investigate long-lived photoexcited carriers in the intrinsic magnetic topological insulator MnBi$_2$Te$_4$ with the chemical potential tuned to the topological surface state by Sb substitution. Following optical excitation, we observe the delayed emergence of a transient state whose formation coincides with depopulation of the bulk conduction band and whose lifetime extends to the microsecond timescale. The quasiparticle dispersion exhibits a pronounced flattening and develops a Mexican-hat-like profile. These spectral signatures are consistent with the formation of a metastable excitonic condensate state. Our results establish magnetic topological insulators as a promising platform for investigating long-lived photoinduced many-body states and their interplay with topology and magnetism.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper investigates the sensitivity of Hellinger-distance coherence in two-flavor neutrino oscillations to weak decoherence. It finds that while most quantum measures decay linearly with the damping parameter, the Hellinger coherence exhibits a distinct non-analytic $\sqrt{\epsilon}$ decay. This provides a powerful diagnostic tool for characterizing decoherence effects in neutrino physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study provides a detailed comparison of how different quantum information measures decay under decoherence, highlighting the unique non-analytic scaling of the Hellinger coherence, which is valuable for understanding quantum process monitoring in particle physics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper evaluates how Hellinger-distance based coherence in two-flavor neutrino oscillations responds to weak decoherence induced by Lindblad damping.

**Main result.** The Hellinger coherence exhibits a nonanalytic $\sqrt{\epsilon}$ correction in the weak-damping limit, contrasting with other quantum measures that respond linearly in the damping parameter $\epsilon$.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving closed-form expressions for various coherence measures and comparing the results obtained from the weak-damping expansion ($\epsilon 	o 0$) against the exact solutions.

**Model / system.** The system is two-flavor neutrino oscillations, modeled using a density matrix $ho$ subjected to Lindblad damping in the mass basis, characterized by a damping factor $\kappa = e^{-\Gamma L}$.

**Key observables.** Hellinger-distance coherence ($C_H$), flavor-transition probability, $l_1$ coherence, concurrence, entanglement of formation, and local quantum Fisher information.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The weak-damping parameter $\epsilon = 1 - \kappa$, and oscillation parameters derived from Daya Bay, KamLAND, and MINOS working points.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the two-flavor vacuum treatment with negligible wave-packet separation, and the weak-damping expansion is only valid near $\epsilon = 0$.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the exact Hellinger coherence ratio against the asymptotic law and the $l_1$ result, and show the coherence decay/revival under correlated dephasing channels.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the mathematical framework for coherence measures, derives the closed-form expression for $C_H$, analyzes the weak-damping expansion showing the $\sqrt{\epsilon}$ dependence, and concludes with quantitative comparisons using experimental data points.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We evaluate the Hellinger-distance coherence of the two-flavor neutrino state subject to Lindblad damping of the mass-eigenstate interference term, and obtain a closed expression in terms of the flavor-basis density-matrix elements. In the two-flavor vacuum treatment with negligible wave-packet separation, the undamped state is pure, and the smallest eigenvalue of the damped state grows linearly with $ε=1-κ$, where $κ=e^{-ΓL}$. An expansion about the pure state then yields a nonanalytic $\sqrtε$ correction to the Hellinger coherence, with coefficient $K=2\sqrt{2}\,u\sin2θ/\sqrt{1-2u}$, whereas the flavor-transition probability, the $l_1$ coherence, the concurrence, the entanglement of formation and the local quantum Fisher information all respond linearly in $ε$. Using representative Daya Bay, KamLAND and MINOS working points together with a current $90\%$ C.L. bound on energy-independent damping, we find fractional changes in the Hellinger coherence of $0.18\%$, $9.40\%$ and $21.51\%$, against $0.0009\%$, $0.43\%$ and $1.81\%$ for the concurrence. The comparison concerns the response of the quantifiers to the damping parameter and carries no implication about experimental resolution. The same mechanism operates, with a different coefficient, for a correlated dephasing channel acting on the flavor coherence, where the non-Markovian regime produces damped revivals.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a spectral transfer formalism to analyze nonlinear synchronization in coupled oscillator networks, moving beyond simple global measures. By projecting dynamics onto the graph Laplacian eigenbasis, the authors quantify how activity redistributes across structural scales via a transfer matrix. This reveals hidden, structured spectral reorganizations that persist even when the overall system appears macroscopically stable.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of spectral reorganisation and the separation of global coherence from underlying spectral dynamics provides a powerful, multiscale diagnostic tool applicable to analyzing complex, non-equilibrium dynamics in many-body systems, which is relevant to open quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work aims to resolve how internal dynamical activity redistributes across network scales during nonlinear synchronization, moving beyond traditional global observables like the Kuramoto order parameter.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates a clear separation between macroscopic coherence and microscopic spectral interactions, showing that structured, fluctuating internal redistribution dynamics persist even when global synchronisation is nearly stationary.

**Method.** A spectral transfer formalism is introduced by projecting Kuramoto phase dynamics onto the eigenbasis of the graph Laplacian, constructing a time-dependent transfer matrix.

**Model / system.** The model uses the Kuramoto oscillator system coupled on a general graph structure, with analysis focusing on modular and hierarchical network architectures.

**Key observables.** Spectral flux, Spectral Modal Energy ($E_m(t)$), Transfer Matrix elements ($T_{m 	o k}(t)$), Net Spectral Flux ($\Pi(t, K_0)$), and the conventional Kuramoto order parameter ($R(t)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coupling strength ($K$), frequency heterogeneity ($\sigma_{\omega}$), and the structural scales defined by the graph Laplacian eigenmodes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The spectral flux quantifies redistribution between *structural* dynamical scales defined by the Laplacian eigenbasis, not physical transport across nodes.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate graph spectral decomposition (showing mode hierarchies) and the temporal evolution of spectral activity, including cross-sections of the transfer matrix and the spectral flux ($\Pi$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds the spectral transfer formalism by decomposing the state onto graph eigenmodes, constructing the transfer matrix to quantify mode exchange, and analyzing the resulting spectral flux and various transfer observables to characterize different dynamical regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

This work introduces a spectral transfer formalism for nonlinear synchronisation dynamics to resolve how internal dynamical activity redistributes across network scales, moving beyond traditional global observables like the Kuramoto order parameter. By projecting Kuramoto phase dynamics onto the eigenbasis of the graph Laplacian, we construct a time-dependent transfer matrix and derive novel network metrics, including the spectral flux, which quantifies directional redistribution between structural dynamical scales rather than physical transport across nodes. Numerical simulations on modular and hierarchical networks reveal highly structured, intermittent spectral transfer episodes characterised by forward and inverse spectral cascades, directional reversals, and complex interaction geometries. Crucially, we demonstrate a clear separation between macroscopic coherence and microscopic spectral interactions, showing that highly organised, fluctuating internal redistribution dynamics persist even beneath nearly stationary and featureless global synchronisation states. This framework extends conventional synchronisation analysis and establishes a multiscale approach for interpreting network dynamics as an evolving process of spectral reorganisation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, miniaturized Atom-Filtered Laser (AFL) using a micro Faraday anomalous dispersion optical filter ($\mu$FADOF). By engineering the filter to achieve a wide, flat-top transmission window, the authors overcome the size limitations of conventional AFLs, resulting in a compact 75 mL device. This device demonstrates excellent stability and turn-key operation, making it highly promising for next-generation quantum technology.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the engineering challenges of integrating atomic physics principles into practical, deployable quantum hardware, which is a major focus area for AMO physicists.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Miniaturizing and stabilizing Atom-Filtered Lasers (AFLs) for quantum devices, which are currently limited by the bulky size and narrow bandwidth of conventional atomic filters.

**Main result.** The authors developed a micro Faraday anomalous dispersion optical filter (μFADOF) that achieves a 7.5 GHz flat-top transmission window, enabling a 30-fold volume reduction to a compact 75 mL package while maintaining high frequency stability.

**Method.** The research involves designing and characterizing the $\mu$FADOF operating in the extreme hyperfine Paschen-Back regime, optimizing its operation through active temperature and current control to leverage stable optical self-feedback.

**Model / system.** The core system is the AFL utilizing the $\mu$FADOF, which is based on the anomalous dispersion properties of alkali atoms (like ${}^{85}	ext{Rb}$). The operation relies on achieving a stable self-feedback regime where the filter's transmission profile is robust against environmental fluctuations.

**Key observables.** Power instability ($9 	imes 10^{-6}$ at 1 s), long-term frequency stability ($3 	imes 10^{-13}$ at 10000 s), and the achieved flat-top transmission bandwidth (7.5 GHz).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating regime (hyperfine Paschen-Back), bandwidth (7.5 GHz), volume reduction (30-fold to 75 mL), and stabilization parameters (temperature/current control).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The stability of the laser output is critically dependent on operating the $\mu$FADOF in a specific, stable optical self-feedback regime.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the external structure of the 75-mL AFL and its applications; one figure details the working principle of the FADOF using energy-level diagrams for ${}^{85}	ext{Rb}$ to derive the dispersion profile.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the miniaturization bottleneck of AFLs to proposing the $\mu$FADOF solution. It details the physics of the filter (operating regimes, intensity dependence) and concludes with experimental results demonstrating high stability and compactness.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Numerous quantum devices require lasers strictly locked to atomic transitions. Atom-filtered lasers (AFLs) are considered a leading candidate for quantum device laser sources due to their ability to self-align to atomic transitions. However, the sub-GHz sharp transmission spectra of conventional atomic filters impose constraints on both laser miniaturization and output stability. Herein, we demonstrate a micro Faraday anomalous dispersion optical filter (μFADOF) operating within the extreme hyperfine Paschen-Back regime, generating a 7.5 GHz flat-top transmission window. By integrating this filter, the miniaturization bottleneck of the AFLs is overcome, achieving a 30-fold volume reduction to a compact package volume of 75 mL. Simultaneously, investigations into the optical self-feedback characteristics of the μFADOF reveal a stable operating regime, where the optical self-feedback of the atomic filter acts as a stabilizing mechanism, enabling the laser to achieve a power instability of 9 x 10^-6 at 1 s and 3.2 x 10^-5 at 8400 s. The optical frequency standard based on this 75-mL AFL achieves a further improvement in long-term frequency stability to 3 x 10^-13 at 10000 s and maintains turn-key operation under environmental shocks, highlighting the critical role of this laser in the development of deployable quantum devices.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper analyzes the boundary information encoded in the full Pauli spectrum of critical quantum chains, showing it goes beyond what is captured by standard Rényi entropy measures. By mapping the system to classical root systems, the authors derive exact lattice invariants that serve as precise targets for understanding defect-junction amplitudes in boundary conformal field theory. The results confirm that the full spectrum retains crucial boundary details lost in the continuum limit.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a deep, mathematically rigorous link between critical quantum systems, boundary conformal field theory, and algebraic structures (root systems), which is highly relevant for understanding boundary effects in quantum many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates what boundary information is retained in the full Pauli distribution of critical quantum states, which may be invisible when only considering single Rényi entropy moments or the infrared fixed point.

**Main result.** The full Pauli spectrum resolves boundary information by generating specific multiplicative invariants related to classical root systems (A, B, C, D), providing concrete lattice targets for defect-measurement-boundary junction amplitudes.

**Method.** The analysis uses advanced techniques involving Selberg ensembles, Cauchy determinants, and the comparison of ratios of partition functions derived from different boundary conditions.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on critical Ising chains, whose properties are analyzed using Pauli strings and mapped onto structures governed by classical root systems A, B, C, and D.

**Key observables.** Stabilizer Rényi Entropy moments, Pauli magnitudes, and ratios of moments $R(\eta)_{\alpha,L}$ and $R(	imes)_{\alpha,L}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The Renyi index $\alpha$, the system length $L$, and boundary field parameters $\eta_b$ are crucial parameters explored across various limits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the lattice ratios converge to universal continuum junction targets in the thermodynamic limit, despite microscopic differences between boundary realizations.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the convergence of ratios for different root system realizations (B, C, D) towards common theoretical limits, and show the flow of observables away from exactly solvable points.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the connection between Pauli distributions and root systems, deriving exact lattice invariants, and then performing detailed finite-size and strong-boundary limit analyses to confirm the universality of the derived junction amplitudes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Universal information extracted from a critical quantum state depends on how the state is probed. Spatial entanglement, participation statistics, and stabilizer Rényi entropy correspond to different replica geometries and need not isolate the same boundary data. We show that the complete distribution of Pauli magnitudes in critical Ising chains carries an exact geometric fingerprint of boundary termination, distinguishing lattice realizations that flow to the same infrared boundary fixed point. This structure is organized by discrete Selberg ensembles associated with the classical root systems $A$, $B$, $C$, and $D$: three open chains sharing the free--free Ising boundary condition retain distinct $B_L$, $C_L$, and $D_L$ trigonometric wall geometries. The standard symmetry-breaking representatives instead produce rectangular, pinned, and character-inserted $C$ ensembles. Their four boundary moments contain exactly two independent multiplicative combinations invariant under local endpoint normalizations. One of these reduces exactly at finite rank to a moment of the fundamental symplectic character and provides a lattice target for a defect--measurement-boundary junction amplitude. A conditional Gaussian-character law predicts its values throughout the unlocked phase, while exact-arithmetic finite-rank results at the $α=4$ transition depart sharply from the Gaussian continuation. These results show that the full Pauli spectrum resolves boundary information invisible to the infrared boundary fixed point alone and provide concrete targets for replicated boundary conformal field theory.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel mathematical technique using the Lanczos transformation to bridge two distinct physical descriptions of non-Hermiticity: non-reciprocal couplings and imaginary potentials. It demonstrates that a generalized Hatano-Nelson model can be mapped to an equivalent model with real couplings and an imaginary on-site potential. This provides a versatile framework for designing and understanding complex non-Hermitian quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful mathematical tool (the Lanczos mapping) to unify the understanding of different non-Hermitian physics phenomena, which is highly relevant for designing and analyzing open quantum systems in optics and condensed matter.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to establish an isospectral mapping between two distinct families of non-Hermiticity: those arising from non-reciprocal couplings and those involving an imaginary on-site potential.

**Main result.** The Lanczos transformation, when applied with specific initial vectors, successfully maps a generalized Hatano-Nelson model to an equivalent tight-binding model featuring real symmetric couplings and an imaginary on-site potential.

**Method.** The core method is the bi-Lanczos transformation, adapted for non-symmetric Hamiltonians, which constructs an isospectral mapping between the two non-Hermitian forms.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on 1D and 2D tight-binding lattice models, specifically generalizing the Hatano-Nelson model, which incorporates non-reciprocal couplings and potentially imaginary on-site potentials.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the couplings (real vs. imaginary) and the resulting energy spectrum, which is shown to be isospectral between the original and transformed Hamiltonians.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The initial vectors $|u_0angle$ and $\langle v_0|$ are crucial parameters, as their specific choice dictates whether the resulting model has an imaginary potential or real couplings.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The success of the mapping relies on choosing non-standard initial vectors for the bi-Lanczos transformation, and the method is shown to hold even when the original Hamiltonian already contains imaginary potentials or exceptional points.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the transformation on a 1D lattice, showing how different choices of initial vectors control the real/imaginary nature of the on-site potential and couplings. Further figures demonstrate the mapping in 2D (triangular ribbon) and the behavior of exceptional points.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the two non-Hermitian families, develops the Lanczos mapping technique using specific initial vectors, proves the isospectrality, and then applies this framework to construct novel non-Hermitian systems with desired spectral properties (e.g., real spectrum without PT symmetry).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Two types of non-Hermiticity found in physical systems, i.e., non-reciprocal couplings and complex potentials, have gained considerable interest recently. The former leads to the non-Hermitian skin effect, while the latter gives rise to a range of non-Hermitian symmetries such as parity-time ($PT$) and particle-hole symmetries. Although these two non-Hermitian forms have been incorporated in the same models, so far they have been treated as distinct families of non-Hermiticity. In this work, we establish an isospectral mapping between them using the Lanczos transformation. More specifically, we show that a generalized Hatano-Nelson model $H$ with non-reciprocal and spatially varying couplings can be mapped to a tight-binding model $H_L$ with real symmetric couplings and an imaginary on-site potential. This result holds even when the original $H$ already has an imaginary potential and exceptional points. Our work provides a refreshed understanding of the connection between different forms of non-Hermiticity. It also offers an approach to obtaining an intuitive physical understanding of exceptional points in $H_L$ that are elusive in $H$. Finally, our findings also warrant a versatile approach to constructing non-Hermitian systems with a complex potential and a real spectrum, without $PT$ symmetry.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes how self-propulsion introduces asymmetry in the passage times of an active particle moving against or with an external bias. The core finding is that the path ensembles are related by a spatial reflection symmetry, which yields an exact reweighting functional. This functional allows researchers to calculate rare event statistics (uphill) using more common measurements (downhill).

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of exact path reweighting based on symmetry is a powerful tool applicable to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics problems, suggesting general protocols for analyzing directional biases in active matter.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the asymmetry in first passage processes (uphill vs. downhill) for an Active Brownian Particle (ABP) driven by an external force between absorbing boundaries.

**Main result.** While self-propulsion breaks the equality of unweighted first passage time distributions, the two directional path ensembles remain exactly related by spatial reflection, allowing for an exact reweighting to reconstruct rare uphill statistics from common downhill trajectories.

**Method.** The analysis employs path-weighting techniques, deriving an exact reweighting functional based on spatial reflection symmetry, and uses perturbative/asymptotic expansions to calculate mean first passage times.

**Model / system.** The system is an Active Brownian Particle (ABP) moving in 2D under a constant external force, whose dynamics are reduced to a 1D stochastic process $(x(t), \phi(t))$ in the overdamped limit.

**Key observables.** Splitting probabilities, conditional first passage time distributions, and the path-dependent asymmetry functional.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Self-propulsion speed ($U$), external force bias ($F$), and rotational diffusion coefficient ($D_r$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed in the overdamped regime, and later sections analyze the rapid angular relaxation limit ($\epsilon 	o 0$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate planar trajectories showing both uphill and downhill paths, and projected $X(	au)$ trajectories demonstrating the effect of persistence ($U$) on directional contrast.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the ABP model, derives the exact path-reweighting relation based on spatial reflection, and then uses this relation to calculate the directional first passage time distributions and mean times via expansions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

First passage processes in active systems combine stochastic transport with self-propulsion and orientational persistence, making motion along and against an external bias sensitive to the internal active dynamics. For passive biased diffusion, opposite exits can have different splitting probabilities while their conditional first passage time distributions remain identical. We study how this relation changes for an active Brownian particle driven by a constant external force between two absorbing boundaries. Self-propulsion breaks the equality of the uphill and downhill first passage time distributions and modifies the splitting probabilities. Nevertheless, the two directional path ensembles remain exactly related by spatial reflection, which pairs downhill and uphill first passage paths of the same duration while preserving their orientational history. The log-ratio of the probabilities of a path and its reflected partner defines a path-dependent asymmetry functional and provides an exact reweighting between the two ensembles. In the symmetric half-weighted representation, the uphill and downhill first passage time distributions coincide for arbitrary orientational persistence. The same path relation also allows rare uphill statistics to be reconstructed from the more frequently sampled downhill trajectories. Numerical simulations confirm the weighted equality across the explored bias and persistence regimes, while perturbative and asymptotic analyses clarify how orientational persistence produces the directional asymmetry of the unweighted statistics. The exact relation between the two directional path ensembles suggests that similar symmetry-based reconstruction protocols may be found in other nonequilibrium first-passage problems.

</details>

**Authors:** Lorenzo Bagnasacco, Vittorio Giovannetti

[14], together with the diagonal (thin, solid). **Summary.** This paper enhances the mathematical tool known as the Fer expansion, which calculates quantum time evolution operators using a product of unitary matrix exponentials. By optimizing the bounding technique for commutators, the authors increase the guaranteed convergence radius from 2 to approximately 2.6058. This improvement is vital for accurately simulating quantum dynamics governed by time-dependent Hamiltonians.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a significant quantitative improvement to a fundamental mathematical tool (the Fer expansion) used for time evolution in quantum mechanics, which is crucial for simulating dynamics in AMO physics and quantum information.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to improve the convergence radius for the Fer expansion, a method used to calculate the time-evolution operator for quantum systems governed by time-dependent Hamiltonians.

**Main result.** The authors successfully improve the known convergence radius for Hermitian generators from $2$ to approximately $2.6058$, representing an improvement of about $30\%$.

**Method.** The improvement is achieved by optimizing the bound on the commutator $\left[e^{-\mu X}, Yight]$ over a free parameter $c$, which is then used to refine the recursive bound governing the expansion's convergence.

**Model / system.** The analysis concerns a quantum system described by a time-dependent Hamiltonian $H(t)$, whose time evolution is captured by the operator $U(t)$. The mathematical framework involves comparing the Dyson series with the unitary-preserving Fer expansion.

**Key observables.** The convergence radius, defined by the maximum value of the time integral $\int_0^t \|H(s)\| ds$ for which the expansion is guaranteed to converge.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The convergence radius $ho$, which is determined by the fixed point of the recursion map $\Psi(x)$, and the Hamiltonian $H(t)$ itself.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The convergence is only guaranteed within a finite radius, and the derivation relies on bounding the commutator using spectral properties of $e^{-\mu X}$ on the unit circle.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the spectrum of $e^{-\mu X}$ for anti-Hermitian $X$ lying on an arc of the unit circle. Figure 2 depicts the recursion relation as a map showing the improvement from the old bound ($ho_{old}$) to the new bound ($ho_{new}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds upon the known Fer expansion, derives a refined bound for the commutator using optimization over a scalar multiple of the identity, and propagates this improved bound through the recursive relation to determine the new, larger convergence radius.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Dyson series expands the propagator of a time-dependent Hamiltonian in powers of the Hamiltonian, but its truncations are in general not unitary. The Fer expansion writes the same propagator as an infinite product of matrix exponentials, each of them unitary, and its remainder decays doubly exponentially with the number of factors. Convergence, however, is guaranteed only within a finite radius: the time integral of the norm of the Hamiltonian must be smaller than $2$. This is the best value known to date. Here we improve it by about $30\%$, raising it to about $2.6058$.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces the Chern-Simons descendant marker, a novel, local, and state-based tool to characterize the $\mathbb{Z}_2$ topological invariant in 2D time-reversal-invariant systems. It achieves this by deriving the marker from the one-particle density matrix and systematically optimizing an auxiliary operator $S$ to ensure the marker remains well-defined even in the presence of disorder. This provides a powerful, practical diagnostic tool for condensed matter physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of a local, measurable marker for topological invariants in disordered systems is crucial for understanding real-world materials where perfect crystalline order is absent, bridging abstract topology with measurable physical quantities.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop a practical, real-space, and state-based local topological marker for characterizing topological phases in two-dimensional time-reversal-invariant systems.

**Main result.** A local Chern-Simons descendant marker is derived directly from the one-particle density matrix, providing a robust $\mathbb{Z}_2$ invariant that remains well-defined even when the system is disordered or when simple commutation relations are not met.

**Method.** The marker is obtained via dimensional reduction from an auxiliary three-dimensional state, requiring the identification and optimization of a local Hermitian involution $S$ that maximizes the spectral gap of an associated operator $\Gamma$.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on two-dimensional time-reversal-invariant near-Gaussian states, utilizing the one-particle density matrix $\rho$ as the primary input. The method is tested in models exhibiting strong Rashba and Dresselhaus spin-orbit coupling.

**Key observables.** Local Chern-Simons descendant marker, $\mathbb{Z}_2$ invariant, spectral gap of $\Gamma$, one-particle density matrix ($\rho$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The optimal angle $\theta$ used to define the involution $S$, which is shown to be robust across different physical regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on the properties of Gaussian states and the assumption that the spectral gap condition on $\Gamma$ is the key requirement for the marker's definition, rather than exact commutation $[S, \rho]=0$.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the concept of local topological markers, derives the Chern-Simons descendant marker through dimensional reduction, and details the systematic procedure for selecting and optimizing the auxiliary operator $S$ by maximizing the spectral gap of $\Gamma$.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Local topological markers provide a real-space characterization of topological phases and are suited to noncrystalline models. We derive a local topological marker, the Chern-Simons descendant marker, for characterizing the topology of two-dimensional time-reversal-invariant near-Gaussian states from the one-particle density matrix \(ρ\). We construct an auxiliary three-dimensional state characterized by the local Chern-Simons marker, and use dimensional reduction to obtain the Chern-Simons descendant marker for the original two-dimensional state. This derivation introduces a local Hermitian involution \(S\) that is odd under time reversal. When \([S,ρ]=0\), the local Chern-Simons descendant marker reduces to half the difference of the local Chern markers in the \(S=\pm1\) sectors. Constructing an \(S\) that commutes exactly with \(ρ\) generally requires fine-tuning, but this is not needed---the local Chern-Simon marker remains well defined whenever \(Γ=\{ρ,S\}-S\) has a spectral gap around zero, even when \([S,ρ]\neq0\). This leaves the practical problem of identifying a suitable \(S\) for a given state, which, importantly, is straightforward even when spin is not a candidate. We show this by considering both strong Rashba and Dresselhaus spin-orbit coupling, for which knowledge of the physical origin of the state reduces the general problem of choosing $S$ to a one-parameter family \(S(θ)\). We evaluate the optimal angle $θ$ maximizing the spectral gap of $Γ$ analytically assuming translation invariance, and find that the gap of $Γ$ remains open for a broad range of angles around the optimum. The same value of \(θ\) remains valid in disordered systems, providing an easy way to obtain choice of \(S\) for evaluating the local Chern-Simons descendant marker of the state.

</details>

### [Matrix Density Waves and Fractionally Charged Point Defects in Flavor Weyl Semimetals](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26957v1)

**Authors:** Shantonu Mukherjee, Hridis K. Pal  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.26957v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.26957_figures/2609.26957_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Order-parameter manifolds of the scalar and flavor- adjoint Weyl CDWs. For a nondegenerate Weyl pair, the internode order is a complex scalar, ∆= ∆0eiθ, with fixed- amplitude manifold Mscalar = S1. For a flavor doublet, the adjoint condensate ∆= Φ0eiθn · τ carries both a CDW phase and an internal orientation n ∈S2. The identification (n, θ) ∼(−n, θ + π) gives M = (S2 × S1)/Z2.</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2609.26957_figures/2609.26957_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2. Topological defects and their fermionic consequences in the matrix density wave. (a) The elementary line defect is a half-quantum vortex in which the density-wave phase winds by π while the flavor orientation reverses, n →−n. Its mass determinant winds once, so the resulting Alice axion string carries a single chiral mode, νline = 1. (b) A unit hedgehog, n(r) = ˆr, forces the adjoint mass to vanish at its core and binds one normalizable zero mode with charge sectors Q = ±e/2 at neutrality in the spectrally symmetric limit. (c) The line and point defects are intertwined: transporting a hedgehog around the Alice string reverses its winding, N →−N.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes Weyl semimetals exhibiting a Matrix Density Wave order, showing that internal flavor degrees of freedom induce complex topological defects. These defects include unit hedgehogs and Alice strings, which are linked by the condensate structure. The key finding is the realization of point-defect fractionalization, where localized zero modes carry fractional electronic charges ($\pm e/2$).

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of fractionalization and the interplay between different types of topological defects (point vs. line) is highly relevant to understanding emergent phenomena in strongly correlated electronic systems, such as fractional quantum Hall states or topological superconductors.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Investigating how adding internal flavor degrees of freedom to interaction-driven Weyl density waves leads to point-defect fractionalization and intertwines point- and line-defect physics.

**Main result.** The system supports both unit hedgehog monopoles and half-quantum Alice strings, which are linked by the condensate structure and result in fractional charges ($\pm e/2$) at the point defects.

**Method.** Theoretical analysis using low-energy effective Hamiltonians, topological index theorems (Callias index), and solving radial differential equations for zero modes.

**Model / system.** A minimal model based on a pair of opposite-chirality Weyl nodes exhibiting a Matrix Density Wave (MDW) order parameter, driven by a local flavor-symmetric repulsion.

**Key observables.** Fractional charges ($\pm e/2$), chiral electronic modes ($
u_{	ext{line}}=1$), and the winding number ($N$) characterizing point defects.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The interaction strength ($U$), the separation distance ($d$) between defects, and the topological winding number ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the adjoint component and assumes the asymptotic gap remains open for index theorem application.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the topological defects: the half-quantum vortex (Alice string) and the unit hedgehog monopole, showing how transporting one around the other reverses the winding number ($N 	o -N$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general MDW order parameter, deriving the topological classification of line and point defects, calculating the fermionic zero modes using index theorems, and finally analyzing the fractional charge carried by isolated and paired defects.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A conventional interaction-driven Weyl density wave is a complex scalar and supports vortex lines, but no topologically stable point defects. We show that two Weyl flavors do not merely duplicate this order. Starting from the matrix-valued internode coherence, a local flavor-symmetric repulsion selects a traceless adjoint condensate within the density-wave sector, while the fermionic ground-state energy locks its complex components into the collinear form $\mathbfΔ=Φ_0\mathbf{n} e^{iθ}$. The resulting order-parameter manifold, $(S^2\times S^1)/\mathbb Z_2$, supports both unit hedgehogs and half-quantum Alice strings of the same electronic mass that gaps the Weyl fermions. An elementary hedgehog binds a single normalizable zero mode whose empty and occupied sectors carry charges $-e/2$ and $+e/2$ at neutrality. An elementary Alice string carries a single chiral electronic mode despite its $π$ phase winding, and transporting a hedgehog around it reverses the hedgehog winding, $N\rightarrow-N$. Flavor therefore converts spontaneous translation breaking into a route to point-defect fractionalization and intertwines the point- and line-defect sectors of an interaction-generated electronic mass.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles the computational challenge of simulating battery cells by mapping the governing non-linear PDEs onto a quantum circuit framework. They propose and demonstrate a hybrid quantum-classical algorithm using the Feynman-Kitaev Hamiltonian to achieve the first quantum simulation of the SPMe model. This work shows a viable pathway for quantum computation to accelerate materials science simulations.

**Why it may be interesting.** It represents a direct application of quantum computing principles (Hamiltonian encoding, variational algorithms) to solve complex, time-dependent, non-linear partial differential equations arising in condensed matter electrochemistry.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Simulating complex electrochemical materials and systems, like battery cells, which are computationally limited by classical methods.

**Main result.** The authors successfully demonstrated the first quantum simulation of a battery cell using the Single Particle Model with electrolyte (SPMe) via a hybrid quantum-classical algorithm.

**Method.** A hybrid quantum-classical algorithm is employed, utilizing the Feynman-Kitaev Hamiltonian to encode the non-linear partial differential equations governing charge transport and reactions.

**Model / system.** The system is a battery cell modeled by the SPMe, involving coupled non-linear PDEs for concentration and potential. The physics is encoded into a Hamiltonian structure suitable for quantum circuit implementation.

**Key observables.** Cell voltage (U), solid concentration (c_s,k), and electrochemical potential ($\phi_k$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Diffusion coefficients ($D_{s,k}$), particle radii ($R^*_k$), and current density ($j_k$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The use of a hybrid quantum-classical approach is necessary due to current quantum hardware noise (NISQ era).

**Figures summary.** Figures show the evolution of concentration and potential during charging and GITT cycles, comparing quantum simulation results to established numerical solutions.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the computational bottleneck, details the adaptation of the SPMe equations into a quantum Hamiltonian formulation, describes the hybrid quantum-classical algorithm, and validates the method by simulating charging and GITT cycles.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Simulations of electrochemical materials and systems accelerate technological progress, but are still limited by computational power. In particular, quantum computing offers prospects for higher resolutions, due to the exponential amount of data that can be stored in a quantum state. As current quantum computers are still noisy, we consider a hybrid quantum-classical algorithm, that divides the problem into smaller computational tasks. We describe how to implement such an algorithm for non-linear partial differential equations, the Feynman-Kitaev Hamiltonian, in a scalable way for an electrochemical system. We show how it can evaluate general electrochemical models and present a quantum simulation of the Single Particle Model with electrolyte (SPMe) as the first quantum simulation of a battery cell.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a powerful mathematical criterion to prove the spectral gap of quantum systems modeled by PEPS, particularly for the AKLT model on the hexagonal lattice. By analyzing how boundary states factorize across overlapping regions, the authors establish that exponential decay of this factorization error guarantees a positive spectral gap. This provides a systematic theoretical tool for characterizing the ground state properties of 2D quantum magnets.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous connection between local correlation decay (boundary response) and global spectral properties (spectral gap) is fundamental for understanding the stability and ground state properties of quantum materials modeled by PEPS.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes a rigorous, systematic condition based on the approximate factorization of overlapping boundary states to prove the existence of a spectral gap for quantum lattice models described by PEPS.

**Main result.** The method successfully yields a uniform spectral gap for the hexagonal AKLT model and provides a general framework connecting local boundary response properties to the spectral gap of 2D parent Hamiltonians.

**Method.** The core technique involves decomposing the boundary response induced by cutting bonds into a common, absorbable contribution and a remainder controlled by an instantaneous boundary metric, leading to a sufficient condition for the gap.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on Projected Entangled Pair States (PEPS) applied to specific models, notably the Ising PEPS and the spin-3/2 AKLT model defined on the hexagonal lattice.

**Key observables.** The spectral gap ($\lambda(\Lambda)$), the boundary state ($ho_{\partial R}$), and the factorization error $\lVert P_{AB}P_{BC} - P_{ABC} Vert$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The overlap width ($\ell_B$) and the lattice geometry (hexagonal lattice).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes the existence of a common linear transport to absorb the common boundary response, and for PEPS, it often requires compatible injective regional contractions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the PEPS tensor structure, the boundary state calculation via double contraction, and the geometric decomposition used for the martingale criterion on rectangular regions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the boundary state criterion, then developing the common-response theorem using linear transport, and finally applying this framework to the Ising PEPS (reducing to a Dobrushin-Shlosman-type condition) and the hexagonal AKLT model (using rooted path expansions).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We establish a sufficient condition under which overlapping PEPS boundary states satisfy the approximate factorization criterion for a spectral gap. The condition is formulated in terms of the boundary response induced by cutting bonds through the center of a rectangular region. We decompose this response into a contribution common to all four associated regions and a remainder measured in the instantaneous boundary metric. A single linear transport absorbs the common contribution and yields compatible factorization operators, with an error controlled solely by the remainder.   We apply this framework to Ising PEPS and to the spin-3/2 AKLT model on the hexagonal lattice. For Ising PEPS, the required boundary-response estimate reduces to a Dobrushin-Shlosman-type condition. For the hexagonal AKLT model, a rooted expansion in paths and loops isolates the common response, while a local comparison of boundary states together with a scalar Kotecký-Preiss estimate controls the remaining terms. In both cases, the factorization error decays exponentially with the overlap width, up to a prefactor proportional to the cut length. For AKLT, we establish the corresponding physical projector estimate and obtain a uniform spectral gap. For Ising PEPS, the gap implication additionally requires compatible injective regional contractions, as specified in the paper. More generally, the method provides a systematic route from locality of PEPS boundary response to spectral gaps of two-dimensional parent Hamiltonians.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes a novel topological data analysis framework using persistent homology to robustly identify commensurate moiré order in twisted bilayer materials. By filtering out noise using a small-neighborhood separation technique, the authors show that their topological descriptors accurately pinpoint known commensurate angles even when the atomic structure is perturbed by disorder or sampled sparsely. This establishes a powerful, disorder-resilient tool for characterizing moiré superlattices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the focus is on structural characterization rather than quantum dynamics, the use of topological data analysis (TDA) to extract robust, symmetry-aware fingerprints from complex, disordered atomic structures is highly relevant for characterizing the underlying physical states in quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing a robust method to identify commensurate moiré order in twisted bilayer materials directly from atomic coordinates, overcoming limitations of traditional spectral or geometric methods sensitive to disorder.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a data-driven topological framework using persistent homology with a small-neighborhood separation filter, which successfully recovers sharp signatures corresponding to known commensurate angles even under significant positional disorder.

**Method.** The method treats atomic configurations as point clouds and applies persistent homology to extract multiscale topological signatures. A key innovation is the small-neighborhood separation filter applied to persistence diagrams to enhance the signal related to periodicity.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on Moiré patterns in twisted bilayer materials, using twisted MoTe2 dichalcogenide bilayers and twisted bilayer graphene as test cases. The analysis involves projecting 3D atomic coordinates into 2D point clouds.

**Key observables.** Topological descriptors derived from persistent homology, specifically sharp minima in Wasserstein distances between twisted and untwisted reference configurations, which correlate with commensurate angles.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Twist angle ($	heta$), disorder magnitude ($\epsilon$), and sampling ratio ($ho$) are key parameters used to test the robustness of the descriptor.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that the topological signature of commensurate order manifests as a distinct, sharp feature (minimum) in the calculated topological distance metric.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the performance of various structural descriptors (SSF, Hausdorff, PDs) for detecting commensurate order, showing that the proposed $\delta$-separated topological score yields the clearest and most robust correlation with known angles.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the limitations of existing methods, introducing the topological framework using persistent homology, benchmarking this framework against geometric/spectral measures, and finally demonstrating its robustness by testing it under controlled levels of positional disorder and sparsity.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Moiré patterns in twisted bilayer materials exhibit long-range periodic order that is highly sensitive to twist angle. Identifying commensurate angles from real-space atomic structures remains challenging, as spectral and geometric methods emphasize global periodicity and are sensitive to disorder and finite-size effects. Here, we introduce a data-driven topological framework that quantifies moiré periodicity by treating atomic configurations as point-cloud data and extracting multiscale signatures using persistent homology. At the core of our approach is a small-neighborhood separation filter that removes redundant local motifs in persistence diagrams while preserving key structural features, enabling sharp minima in Wasserstein distances between twisted and untwisted reference configurations that accurately recover commensurate angles. We benchmark this framework against geometric and spectral similarity measures and show that the resulting topological descriptors remain stable under positional disorder ranging from weak to strong perturbations in the bond length. We further transfer these descriptors using a constrained Gaussian process surrogate to sparsely sampled configurations of a twisted MoTe$_2$ dichalcogenide bilayer. These results establish topological descriptors with neighborhood separation as a robust framework for identifying commensurate order in moiré systems and tracking commensurate structural similarity under disorder, sparsity, and finite-size effects.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper characterizes the precise quantum states that can function as resources for encrypted quantum cloning. It establishes that exact recovery requires specific correlation structures within the resource, not just high entanglement. The findings exclude large families of states, such as the Dicke states, from being valid resources for this cryptographic task.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides deep structural constraints on quantum resources, showing that mere entanglement quantity is insufficient; the specific correlation structure relative to the encoding scheme dictates feasibility.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper characterizes the exact multipartite pure quantum states that can serve as resources for performing 'Encrypted Quantum Cloning' (EQC).

**Main result.** The resource state's suitability depends not only on its total entanglement but critically on how its correlations are organized relative to the specific encoding architecture. The entire Dicke family of states is proven to be excluded for this task.

**Method.** The authors derive necessary and sufficient conditions for exact recovery by analyzing the structure of the signal-noise cut and applying information-theoretic criteria derived from the cloning process.

**Model / system.** The model involves distributing an unknown $k$-qubit state ($\vert \psi angle_A$) among $m$ encrypted clones using a resource state $\vert R angle_{SN}$ and a sector-wise two-Pauli encoder.

**Key observables.** Signal-noise entanglement (ebits), the rank of the signal-noise cut matrix, and the structure of the signal marginal $ho_S$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of clones ($m \ge 2$), number of input qubits ($k$), and the specific encoder architecture.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is performed for a fixed sector-wise two-Pauli encoder, although general bounds independent of the encoder are also derived.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the operational architecture of EQC, showing the encoding process and the recovery mechanism from individual clones and the key.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the EQC problem, deriving necessary conditions for exact recovery, characterizing the required structure of the resource state $ho_S$ for fixed encoders, and finally testing these conditions against specific resource families like graph states and Dicke states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Encrypted quantum cloning distributes an unknown $k$-qubit state among $m$ encrypted clones so that no individual clone reveals the input, yet the state can be recovered from any one clone together with a common quantum key. We ask the inverse question: for a fixed encoding architecture, which multipartite pure states can serve as exact resources for this task? For $m\ge2$, we completely characterize the pure resources compatible with a sector-wise two-Pauli encoder, with necessity holding for arbitrary completely positive trace-preserving (CPTP) recovery maps. For even $m$, exact recovery requires maximal entanglement across the signal--noise cut. For odd $m$, less entanglement can suffice, provided that the surviving signal correlations have the structure selected by the encoder. We further show, without fixing the encoder, that exact recovery from every authorized subsystem already implies perfect concealment of each individual signal and requires at least $(m-1)k$ ebits of signal--noise entanglement. For graph states, the resource classification reduces to an exact condition on the kernel of the signal--noise cut matrix, leading to binary certification and constructive Clifford recovery. We identify rank-deficient graph resources that attain the architecture-independent entanglement bound and prove that the entire Dicke family, including $W$ states, is excluded for every sector-wise two-Pauli encoder. Our results show that encrypted recovery depends not only on how much entanglement a resource contains, but also on how its correlations are organized relative to the encoder.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.27565v1>

From m Bell samples {qi}i of an unknown state vector |ψ⟩, Gaussian elimination on the lifted vectors q⊗2 i ∈F2n 2 ⊗F2n 2 , yields a basis of the quadratic kernel Ker2,m(ψ) of the distribution, consisting of quadratic forms M satisfying qT i Mqi = c for every observed outcome. For state vectors of the form |ψ⟩= UC Nm i=1 |ψi⟩ci this kernel has a common hidden block structure. Indeed, before the action of UC, the Bell distribution factorizes across the product decomposition, forcing its quadratic symmetries to respect the corresponding blocks. The unknown Clifford scrambles both the distribution and its symmetries through an affine...</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents efficient algorithms to recover the hidden product structure within quantum states that have been scrambled by an unknown Clifford unitary. By analyzing the quadratic symmetries of the state's Bell distribution, the authors linearize the problem, enabling the recovery of the disentangling Clifford and the underlying partitions. This framework is particularly powerful for learning complex, T-doped quantum circuits previously thought inaccessible.

**Why it may be interesting.** The techniques developed for characterizing structured quantum states via symmetry analysis are highly relevant for certifying quantum computations and understanding the limitations imposed by gate sets (like Clifford vs. T-gates).

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops efficient algorithms to test for and recover hidden tensor-product structure in unknown pure quantum states $|\psiangle = U_C igotimes_i |\psi_iangle$, where $U_C$ is an unknown Clifford unitary.

**Main result.** The authors provide polynomial-time algorithms to recover the disentangling Clifford $U_C$ and the hidden product partitions, extending these methods to efficiently learn typical $t$-doped Clifford unitaries in regimes with exponentially more T gates.

**Method.** The core method involves analyzing the quadratic symmetries of the state's Bell distribution, linearizing the problem by block-diagonalizing these symmetries, and utilizing classical post-processing.

**Model / system.** The system consists of pure quantum states on $n$ qubits, $|\psiangle$, whose structure is obscured by an arbitrary unknown Clifford unitary $U_C$. The analysis focuses on the algebraic properties of the state's Bell distribution.

**Key observables.** Quadratic symmetries of the Bell distribution, $q^T M q = c$, and the ability to block-diagonalize the symmetry kernel $	ext{Ker}_2(\psi)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits ($n$), the degree of T-doping ($t$), and the required sample complexity ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The state must possess a hidden tensor-product structure of the form $|\psiangle = U_C igotimes_i |\psi_iangle$, and the symmetries must retain a characteristic quadratic structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the problem (testing/learning hidden structure) to developing the mathematical machinery (analyzing quadratic symmetries and block-diagonalization) and concluding with concrete applications (learning $t$-doped unitaries and Hamiltonian compression).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Highly entangled and highly non-stabilizer quantum states need not be hard to learn. We give efficient algorithms for testing and recovering hidden tensor-product structure in unknown pure state vectors of the form $\lvertψ\rangle = U_C \bigotimes_i \lvertψ_i\rangle$, where $U_C$ is an arbitrary unknown Clifford unitary. Although the Clifford can thoroughly scramble the visible product structure, we prove that the Bell distribution retains a characteristic family of quadratic symmetries. By simultaneously block-diagonalising these symmetries, our algorithms linearize the problem and manage to recover both a disentangling Clifford and the hidden partitions with polynomial sample and computational complexity. This may be viewed as an extension of the abelian StateHSP paradigm in which classical post-processing exposes genuinely quadratic structure. Applied to Choi states, the method yields efficient proper learning algorithms for typical $t$-doped Clifford unitaries in regimes containing exponentially more $T$ gates than previously accessible: the required condition fails only for an exponentially small fraction of circuits when $t\sim n$, and continues to hold for a constant fraction even when $t=2n$. Our framework also provides tools for compressing structured many-body Hamiltonians and suggests benchmarking protocols for encoded logical product states in the early fault-tolerant regime.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an Equivariant Projection Network (EPN) that allows ordinary neural networks to generate predictions that inherently respect the underlying physical symmetries of lattice Hamiltonians. By averaging predictions across symmetry transformations, the method bypasses the need for specialized equivariant network architectures. This provides an accessible and accurate tool for simulating complex, symmetry-governed dynamics in correlated electron systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The development of symmetry-preserving ML surrogates is crucial for simulating complex quantum materials dynamics, providing a computationally tractable route to study phenomena like charge ordering and phase transitions in systems where traditional methods are too slow.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing efficient, symmetry-preserving machine learning surrogates to replace computationally expensive electronic structure calculations for large-scale simulations of correlated electron systems.

**Main result.** The proposed Equivariant Projection Network (EPN) framework successfully generates exactly equivariant surrogates for electronic forces and transition energies directly from ordinary MLPs.

**Method.** The EPN enforces symmetry by evaluating an ordinary MLP on all symmetry-related configurations and averaging the results according to the finite-group averaging formula.

**Model / system.** The work applies to correlated electron systems, specifically demonstrating success with the Falicov–Kimball model and the Holstein model, which involve lattice dynamics and charge ordering.

**Key observables.** Directional hopping free-energy differences ($\Delta E_{i,\alpha}$), conservative lattice forces ($\mathbf{F}_{	ext{ML}}$), and charge-density-wave coarsening.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The method is applicable to systems with known, finite lattice point groups ($G$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approximation relies on the assumption of electronic nearsightedness and that the local environment contains sufficient information to predict local dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the EPN for Falicov–Kimball dynamics using $D_4$ symmetry. Figure 2 compares predicted hopping free-energy differences ($\Delta E_{ML}$) against Exact Diagonalization (ED) values and shows agreement in kMC simulations.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the EPN framework, demonstrates its application to calculate directional hopping free-energy differences in the Falicov-Kimball model, and then applies it to generate conservative lattice forces for the Holstein model, validating results against ED benchmarks and large-scale simulations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Large-scale simulations of correlated electron systems require repeated evaluations of the electronic forces and transition energies driving collective dynamics. Machine-learning surrogates alleviate this bottleneck, but incorporating symmetry often involves carefully designed descriptors or specialized network architectures. We show that ordinary multilayer perceptrons equipped with finite-group averaging provide exactly equivariant surrogates directly from microscopic configurations. The construction separates symmetry enforcement from the internal network architecture and applies to both discrete and continuous lattice degrees of freedom. In the Falicov--Kimball model, it predicts directional hopping free-energy differences; in the Holstein model, its invariant limit generates conservative lattice forces. Benchmarks against exact diagonalization establish microscopic accuracy and agreement of dynamical correlations, while large-scale simulations recover multiscale charge ordering and charge-density-wave coarsening. These results demonstrate an accessible, reusable route to symmetry-preserving many-body dynamics for systems with finite lattice point groups.

</details>

### [Maximal Total Quantum Dimension at Bounded Rank in Dijkgraaf-Witten Theories](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26914v1)

**Authors:** Ce Shen  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** strongly correlated electrons · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.26914v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `analog quantum simulation` **1/5**

The state |a⟩corresponds to threading an anyon flux label a through the cylinder, giving rise to the universal TEE in (2.3). (Reproduced from Ref. [15].)</sub>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper studies the maximum possible topological complexity, quantified by the total quantum dimension, in 2+1 dimensional topological phases modeled by Dijkgraaf-Witten theories. By analyzing the impact of gauge group structure and topological twists, the authors derive sharp asymptotic bounds on this dimension relative to the system's rank. This work provides quantitative constraints crucial for classifying exotic topological order phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between topological invariants (like quantum dimension) and entanglement entropy provides deep insights into the nature of quantum order, which is fundamental to understanding strongly correlated electron systems and topological phases.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the maximal total quantum dimension achievable in three-dimensional Dijkgraaf-Witten (DW) theories subject to a bound on the categorical rank.

**Main result.** It establishes that introducing cocycle twists in Abelian gauge groups can elevate the growth of the total quantum dimension from square-root to linear in the rank cutoff, and provides upper bounds like $O((\log R)^2)$ for almost-simple groups.

**Method.** The analysis uses advanced group theory, optimization techniques, and asymptotic analysis to relate topological invariants (quantum dimensions) to the constraints imposed by the categorical rank.

**Model / system.** The theoretical framework is based on (2+1)-dimensional topological phases described by Unitary Modular Tensor Categories, specifically realized through Dijkgraaf-Witten theories involving finite gauge groups and 3-cocycle twists.

**Key observables.** Total Quantum Dimension ($D(C)$), Topological Entanglement Entropy (TEE), and Categorical Rank ($r(C)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The rank cutoff $R$, the structure of the gauge group $G$ (e.g., $(\mathbb{Z}_p)^n$, almost-simple groups), and the topological twist $\omega$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the validity of the DW theory description for the topological order and relies on specific structural properties of the gauge groups being studied.

**Figures summary.** Not specified, but the notes refer to Figure 1 illustrating the cylindrical bipartition for TEE calculation.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by defining the optimization problem, analyzing the effect of cocycle twists on Abelian groups, deriving sharp envelopes for untwisted theories (like almost-simple groups), and concluding with a comparison to WZW models and the general conjecture.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the maximal total quantum dimension at a bounded categorical rank cutoff $R$ in three-dimensional Dijkgraaf--Witten theories. This optimization problem physically governs the maximal state-optimized torus topological entanglement entropy at a fixed torus ground-state degeneracy, while providing a quantitative constraint on the search space required to classify these topological order phases. We analyze how topological twists (3-cocycles) and gauge-group structures independently govern this trade-off. For elementary-Abelian gauge groups at a fixed prime $p$, we prove that introducing cocycle twists elevates the maximum total quantum dimension from square-root growth $Θ_p(R^{1/2})$ to linear growth $Θ_p(R)$ in the rank cutoff, driven by the geometric protection of cyclic holonomies. For untwisted theories, we establish sharp envelopes for symmetric and alternating gauge groups, and derive group-theoretic bounds across almost-simple, solvable, and radical-free families. In particular, almost-simple groups and their direct products obey a quadratic-logarithmic upper envelope: $\log\mathcal{D} = O((\log R)^2)$. Finally, we compare these scales against WZW models and discuss the status of the general quadratic-logarithmic envelope conjecture.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops a method to find a minimal, computationally tractable representation for complex quantum-like systems built from the Cartesian product of smaller units. By employing equitable partitions and framing the problem in terms of graph fibrations, the authors show that the structure of the composite system can be analyzed by studying the product of the individual component structures. This circumvents the exponential scaling problem inherent in direct product constructions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful mathematical tool (equitable partitions/fibrations) to manage the complexity of large, composite quantum-like systems, circumventing exponential resource scaling issues common in quantum information theory.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the exponential computational cost associated with constructing composite Quantum-Like (QL) systems by taking the Cartesian product of multiple QL-bit graphs.

**Main result.** It provides an exact, minimal representation for this graph Cartesian product using equitable partitions, showing that the resulting quotient graph is itself a Cartesian product of the individual quotient graphs.

**Method.** The core method involves applying graph theory concepts, specifically utilizing equitable partitions and relating the structure to graph fibrations, which allows for the decomposition of the large system into smaller, manageable components.

**Model / system.** The model uses QL-bits, which are represented by classical synchronizing networks built from two interacting $k$-regular random subgraphs. Composite systems are formed via the graph Cartesian product, $G = igotimes G(q)$.

**Key observables.** The emergent eigenvalues of the composite system, which are sums of constituent eigenvalues, and the structure of the quotient graph.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of QL-bits ($N_{QL}$), the regularity parameters ($k$ and $\ell$), and the structure of the equitable partition.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of an equitable partition and relies on the commutativity of taking the Cartesian product and constructing equitable partitions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1(b) illustrates the construction of the Cartesian product graph, $G = G(1) \square G(2)$, and the mapping to the tensor product of Hilbert spaces.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the QL-bit model and the challenge of the Cartesian product. It then introduces equitable partitions to find a minimal representation, proving that the quotient graph structure preserves the product nature. Finally, it generalizes this to associativity using graph fibrations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We provide an equitable partition that gives an exact, minimal representation for the graph Cartesian product formed from quantum-like bits that preserves the relevant spectral and topological properties. We show that this result follows from the fact that the operations of taking the Cartesian product of graphs and constructing equitable partition of the graphs commute. Numerical simulations illustrate the preserved emergent eigenstates in the reduced structures. Additionally, we provide a construction of the minimal structure without passing through the Cartesian product. Finally, we frame quantum-like structures in the language of topology and fibrations.

</details>
