**Summary.** This paper presents a novel, general method for designing highly efficient auxiliary systems for quantum error correction surgery on LDPC codes. By relating these gadgets to linear Probabilistically Checkable Proofs of Proximity (PCPPs), the authors achieve space overhead that is asymptotically optimal for measuring multiple logical operators. This advances the theoretical understanding of fault-tolerant quantum computation.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between topological/homological methods (cochain complexes) and computational complexity tools (PCPPs) provides a powerful, abstract framework for analyzing resource bounds in quantum error correction.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is designing sparse, high-rate auxiliary systems for quantum error correction surgery using LDPC codes to measure large sets of logical operators in parallel.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a general method achieving a space overhead of $\mu^{1+o(1)}$ for measuring $t$ logicals in a subcode of size $\mu$, which is asymptotically optimal.

**Method.** The technique relates the construction of these surgery gadgets to linear Probabilistically Checkable Proofs of Proximity (PCPPs) and utilizes concepts from homological algebra, specifically cochain complexes.

**Model / system.** The work analyzes quantum Low-Density Parity Check (LDPC) Calderbank-Shor-Steane (CSS) codes undergoing 'surgery' operations. The mathematical framework involves constructing and analyzing cochain complexes associated with these codes.

**Key observables.** Fault distance ($d$), space overhead ($\mu^{1+o(1)}$), and the dimension of homology groups related to logical faults ($ar{X}$ and $ar{Z}$ logicals).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code parameters like blocklength $n$, quantum dimension $k$, and distance $d$; PCPP parameters like randomness complexity $r(n)$ and proof length $\ell(n)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies heavily on advanced mathematical tools, including the Künneth formula and established theorems regarding linear PCPPs.

**Figures summary.** Table 1 compares the space, time, and spacetime volume costs of the new method against prior techniques for measuring a subcode.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the physical problem (surgery gadgets) to introducing the mathematical machinery (PCPPs and relative cosystolic expansion) and culminates in proving the near-optimal bounds on the required resources.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A central problem for surgery with quantum Low-Density Parity Check (LDPC) codes is the design of auxiliary systems which measure large sets of logical operators in parallel, while preserving sparsity and fault distance. We introduce a very general method for designing surgery gadgets which are 'high rate', meaning that the number of operators measured in parallel is large in comparison to the size of the auxiliary system. Given an arbitrary initial $[[ n, k, d ]]$ quantum LDPC Calderbank-Shor-Steane (CSS) code, and an arbitrary subcode of size $μ\leq n$ that contains $t \leq k$ logical qubits, the method produces a sparse high-rate surgery gadget with size $μ(\logμ)^{O(\log\log μ)}=μ^{1+o(1)}$ which measures all $t$ logicals in the subcode. The space overhead is asymptotically optimal up to subpolynomial factors, as the lower bound is $Ω(μ)$. This gadget is produced in time polynomial in $μ$. When logical measurement is performed using these surgery gadgets, the phenomenological fault distance is at least $d$ when performed for $d$ rounds.   Our main result comes from relating surgery gadgets with relative cosystolic expansion to linear Probabilistically Checkable Proofs of Proximity (PCPPs), which allow a randomised verifier to probabilistically verify the input to a linear circuit, using only oracle access to the input and a claimed proof.

</details>

**Summary.** This work models the optimal evasion strategy for a mobile target against a searcher that resets periodically. By analyzing the mean capture time, the authors find that the delay is maximized not at the fastest or slowest movement, but at an intermediate persistence time. This highlights that temporal memory in motion is a key resource for survival in stochastic pursuit problems.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of using temporal correlations as a resource for evasion is a general principle in non-equilibrium statistical physics, applicable to trapping, reaction-diffusion, and search problems in complex media.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates how a mobile target can optimally move to maximize the mean capture time when being pursued by a searcher that periodically resets to a fixed location.

**Main result.** The mean capture time is maximized at an intermediate correlation time, demonstrating that temporal correlations are a resource for evasion, allowing the target to prolong survival by tuning its movement.

**Method.** The problem is formulated using a steady-state partial differential equation (PDE) for the mean capture time, which is analyzed by examining different asymptotic limits (fast and slow target motion).

**Model / system.** The system involves a searcher undergoing Brownian motion with stochastic resetting, and a target following an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (OU) process around a home location. The dynamics are governed by coupled stochastic differential equations.

**Key observables.** Mean Capture Time (MCT), $\langle T angle$, and the ratio $\langle T angle_{	ext{max}} / T_{\infty}$ quantifying the enhancement due to target mobility.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key parameters are the target's correlation time ($	au$), the searcher's resetting rate ($r$), and the target's spatial exploration range ($\sigma$). The dimensionless product $ho = r	au$ is crucial.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on solving a steady-state PDE, and specific asymptotic limits ($ho 	o 0$ and $ho 	o \infty$) are derived analytically, while the intermediate regime requires numerical solutions.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the calculated MCT versus $	au$, showing a clear intermediate maximum, and illustrate the enhancement ratio $\langle T angle_{	ext{max}} / T_{\infty}$ dependence on system parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model and the governing PDE, analyzes the limiting cases (fast/slow target motion) to derive analytical approximations, and then numerically solves the full PDE to locate the optimal correlation time $	au_\star$ where the maximum MCT occurs.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Returning to a familiar location can help a searcher find its target---but how should the target move to avoid being found? We address this question for a resetting Brownian searcher and a mobile target fluctuating around a home location. Keeping the target's typical exploration range fixed, we find that the mean capture time is maximized at an intermediate correlation time: rapid motion promotes encounters, whereas slow motion leaves the target effectively stationary. Between these extremes, persistent excursions away from the searcher's reset point can substantially delay capture. Analytical limiting results explain this optimum, while numerical solutions reveal an approximately linear relation between the maximal mean capture time and the optimal correlation time across many decades. These findings identify temporal correlations as a resource for evasion, allowing a target to prolong survival by changing how it moves rather than where it roams.

</details>

**Authors:** Vincent Lahoche, Dine Ousmane Samary, Parham Radpay  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.28032v1>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a relational background field formulation as a new Renormalization Group technique for discrete quantum gravity models, particularly random matrix theories. By utilizing the $O(N)$ model, the authors show how this method preserves gauge symmetry while defining a stable RG flow. The key finding is the existence of an asymptotic Wilson-Fisher fixed point, offering a controlled path to the continuum limit.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on quantum gravity, the rigorous application of RG techniques, fixed-point analysis, and symmetry preservation in the large-N limit provides advanced theoretical tools applicable to critical phenomena in complex many-body systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a reliable Renormalization Group (RG) approach for discrete quantum gravity models while preserving the underlying gauge symmetry and achieving a satisfactory continuum limit.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate the existence of an asymptotic Wilson-Fisher-type fixed point using a novel relational background field formulation.

**Method.** The method employs a relational background field formulation combined with a partial Hubbard-Stratonovich decomposition to define an RG flow based on the BBP phase transition.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on random matrix models, specifically the $O(N)$ vector model in zero dimensions, which serves as a pedagogical review of the general construction.

**Key observables.** Asymptotic Wilson-Fisher fixed point, relevant critical exponent, free energy singularity exponent (gamma=1/2).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Large N limit, $O(N)$ symmetry, BBP phase transition, dimension $D=2k+3$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on the self-averaging property for large random matrices and is presented as a review of the construction.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the relational background field concept, reviews its application to the $O(N)$ model, details the use of the BBP transition to define the RG flow, and analyzes the resulting fixed point structure.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In a recent paper arxiv.org/abs/2609.21031, we have introduced the concept of a relational background field as a new approach to the renormalization group (RG) for discrete quantum gravity models, focusing on random matrix models. The proposed method offers the dual advantage of preserving the model's gauge symmetry (typically $U(N)$ or $O(N)$), whose breaking in conventional approaches has remained an open problem, and providing a more satisfactory treatment of the continuum or infrared (IR) limit. In this limit, the theory is described by a non-local field theory in dimension $D = 2k + 3$, where $k$ denotes the number of cuts in the effective spectrum as dictated by random matrix theory. This pedagogical paper reviews this construction for real random vector models in zero dimensions, with $O(N)$ as the symmetry group. Following the general strategy of background-field-type formulations, we show, using the BBP (Baik-Ben Arrous-P'ech'e) phase transition, that a partial Hubbard--Stratonovich decomposition of the quartic interaction, involving a matrix-valued intermediate field, allows a preferred notion of scale to emerge. This scale, in turn, defines an RG flow, whose perturbative structure is analyzed. Our calculation demonstrates the existence of an \textit{asymptotic} Wilson--Fisher-type fixed point, whose sole relevant critical exponent is in qualitative agreement with the double-scaling critical exponent of these models.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates the tunable topological properties of the 2D metal-organic framework $	ext{Eu}_2(	ext{C}_6	ext{H}_4)_3$. Using DFT calculations, the authors show that the material functions as a controllable Chern insulator. By applying strain, stacking, and electric fields, they demonstrate three distinct methods to manipulate both the size of the topological gap and the quantized Chern number, offering a versatile platform for quantum electronics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a clear, multi-faceted blueprint for engineering topological electronic states using purely structural and electric field controls, which is highly relevant for designing novel quantum devices.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is improving the operating temperature and controlling the number of edge channels in the Quantum Anomalous Hall Effect (QAHE).

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that mechanical strain, stacking, and out-of-plane electric fields can independently tune both the topological gap and the Chern number in the single material $	ext{Eu}_2(	ext{C}_6	ext{H}_4)_3$.

**Method.** The research relies heavily on first-principles density functional theory (DFT) calculations, supplemented by tight-binding models derived from maximally localized Wannier functions to characterize the topological invariants.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the two-dimensional metal-organic kagome ferromagnet $	ext{Eu}_2(	ext{C}_6	ext{H}_4)_3$. The electronic properties are modeled as an intrinsic Chern insulator whose topology is sensitive to external perturbations.

**Key observables.** Chern number ($\mathcal{C}$), topological gap size, Hall conductance, strain ($\epsilon$), and out-of-plane electric field ($E_{	ext{out-of-plane}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The initial SOC gap is $72.7 	ext{ meV}$, which widens to $124.7 	ext{ meV}$ under $-8\%$ biaxial strain. The system exhibits Chern numbers of $\mathcal{C}=-1$ (monolayer) and $\mathcal{C}=-2$ (bilayer).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the topological gap opening is primarily sourced by the hybridization between C-$p$ states and the $	ext{Eu}$ $5d$ states, and that the material remains stable under applied strain and fields.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the atomic structure, phonon dispersion, and AIMD simulations confirming stability. Band structure plots show the evolution of the topological gap under varying strain, while theoretical calculations map out the transitions between different Chern number phases.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the material's stability and intrinsic topological properties (monolayer, $C=-1$). It then systematically explores the tunability by applying mechanical strain (widening the gap) and stacking (achieving $C=-2$). Finally, it introduces an out-of-plane electric field to drive the system through multiple topological transitions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The quantum anomalous Hall effect carries dissipationless chiral edge currents without an external magnetic field, yet raising its operating temperature and controlling the number of edge channels remain difficult. Using first-principles calculations, we identify the two-dimensional metal-organic kagome ferromagnet Eu$_2$(C$_6$H$_4$)$_3$ as an intrinsic Chern insulator whose topological gap can be enlarged by mechanical strain. The monolayer has a spin-orbit-coupling-induced gap of 72.7 meV that widens to 124.7 meV under -8% biaxial strain, realizing a quantum anomalous Hall phase with Chern number $\mathcal{C}=-1$ and a single chiral edge state. The local moments come from the half-filled Eu $4f^{7}$ shell, while the gap-opening spin-orbit coupling is carried by Eu $5d$ states hybridized into the carbon kagome bands. The AB-stacked bilayer couples ferromagnetically and accumulates the per-layer Chern numbers, giving $\mathcal{C}=-2$ with two co-propagating chiral channels. An out-of-plane electric field then drives the bilayer through a sequence of topological transitions among $\mathcal{C}=-2$, $-3$, and $-1$, switching the number of edge channels. Together, strain, stacking, and gating give three distinct handles on the gap and the Chern number within a single stoichiometric material.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports on the magnetic and magneto-transport properties of a $	ext{LCMO}/	ext{SIO}$ heterostructure. The researchers found that the magnetoresistance exhibits complex, asymmetric behavior linked directly to the magnetic ordering of both the $3d$ and $5d$ layers. This suggests that interfacial spin scattering is a crucial, tunable mechanism for controlling charge transport in these correlated magnetic materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The study explores how interfacial magnetic coupling (via DM interaction and SOC) dictates charge transport properties, which is a key area for understanding correlated electron behavior in engineered quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Investigating the origin and tuning of asymmetric magnetoresistance (MR) in a $3d$-$5d$ magnetic heterostructure.

**Main result.** The unusual MR behavior is attributed to interface-driven varying spin scattering between itinerant and localized electrons, demonstrating tunable interfacial magnetic transport.

**Method.** The study combines epitaxial thin-film growth (PLD) with comprehensive characterization, including magnetic measurements (SQUID) and electrical transport measurements (four-probe technique).

**Model / system.** The system is an epitaxially grown bilayer of a $3d$ ferromagnet, $	ext{La}_{0.67}	ext{Ca}_{0.33}	ext{MnO}_3$ (LCMO), and a $5d$ heavy metal, $	ext{SrIrO}_3$ (SIO), on an $	ext{STO}$ substrate. The physics involves the interplay of spin-orbit coupling, magnetism, and interfacial interactions.

**Key observables.** Magnetoresistance (MR), resistivity $ho(T)$, magnetization $M(H)$, and magnetic ordering temperatures ($	ext{T}_c$ for LCMO, $	ext{T}_p$ for SIO).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (especially near magnetic ordering points), applied magnetic field ($H$), and interfacial coupling strength.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The unusual MR behavior is ascribed to interface-driven varying spin scattering between itinerant and localized electrons.

**Figures summary.** Figures confirm epitaxial growth (XRD), show smooth film morphology (AFM), detail magnetic transitions in both layers (ZFC/FC, M(H)), and present the temperature and field dependence of the MR.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a standard experimental structure: structural characterization (XRD, AFM, SEM) $	o$ magnetic characterization (SQUID) $	o$ transport measurements ($ho(T)$, MR) $	o$ interpretation linking MR evolution to the magnetic state of the bilayer.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The complex interplay between spin-orbit coupling, magnetism, and magnetic field at the interface of a heterostructure consisting of ferromagnet (FM) and heavy metal (HM), often exhibit extraordinary magnetic and electric phenomena where an in-plane Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya (DM) interaction plays a crucial role to stabilize local spin texture in FM layer. Here, we report detailed magnetic and magneto-transport behaviour on an epitaxially grown thin heterostructure with 3$d$ FM La$_{0.67}$Ca$_{0.33}$MnO$_3$ (LCMO) and 5$d$ HM SrIrO$_3$ (SIO). Magnetic measurements show an unusual development of magnetic ordering in SrIrO3 around 42 K, which further leads to a Mn/Ir magnetic coupling at the interface. The magnetoresistance (MR) measurements show an evolution of its value and shape with the magnetic state of this bilayer. While MR is negative at high temperature, it shows a positive to negative crossover with stable magnetic ordering of LCMO, and finally the positive MR exhibits an asymmetry at low temperature with the ordering of SIO. While we ascribe this unusual MR behaviour to interface-driven varying spin scattering between itinerant and localized electrons, our results shed a light to comprehend the complex but tunable interface behavior on charge transport in a particularly designed heterostructure, offering valuable insights for future functional material engineering.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper optimizes the circuit depth for quantum partial-search algorithms by proposing an alternating sequence of global and local Grover operators. By analyzing the expected circuit depth, the authors demonstrate that this new sequence can significantly outperform the established GRK algorithm in depth-constrained scenarios. This advances the practical implementation of quantum search algorithms by providing a depth-optimal alternative.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses resource optimization in quantum computation, providing concrete, improved circuit implementations for search problems that are crucial for quantum algorithm development.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to optimize the circuit depth of quantum partial-search algorithms, moving beyond the established optimality criteria based solely on the number of oracle queries.

**Main result.** An alternating sequence of global and local Grover operators can achieve a significantly lower expected circuit depth (over 20% reduction) compared to the standard Grover-Radhakrishnan-Korepin (GRK) sequence when the oracle and diffusion operator depths are comparable.

**Method.** The authors systematically analyze the expected circuit depth, $E_\ell(S)$, for various sequence structures, comparing the depth-optimal alternating sequence ($S_A$) against the query-optimal GRK sequence ($S_G$).

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum partial-search algorithm applied to a database of size $N=2^n$, utilizing global ($G_n$) and local ($G_m$) Grover operators. The analysis compares depth-based optimization against query-count optimization.

**Key observables.** Expected circuit depth ($E_\ell(S)$), success probability ($	ext{Pr}(S)$), and the ratio of operator depths ($\alpha$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $\alpha$ between the oracle and global diffusion operator depths, and the block size $b$ relative to the total size $N$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on asymptotic approximations for large $N$ and $K$, and the comparison is made between depth-optimal and query-optimal regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the geometric paths of the GRK sequence versus the depth-efficient alternating sequence in the invariant subspace. Figure 3 presents a phase diagram mapping the optimal sequence choice across different depth ratios ($\alpha$).

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews the GRK algorithm's query optimality, proposes the depth-efficient alternating sequence ($S_A$), and then systematically analyzes $S_A$ to derive its exact success probability and expected depth, establishing the boundary separating depth-optimal and oracle-optimal regimes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Grover's algorithm is optimal in terms of oracle queries. We can trade accuracy for speed, which gives rise to the quantum partial-search algorithm. The partial-search algorithm is implemented using two kinds of Grover operators, global and local, where the former is the standard Grover operator for full search and the latter has a diffusion operator acting on the search subspace. The global-local-global sequence, also known as the Grover-Radhakrishnan-Korepin (GRK) algorithm, has been proved optimal in the oracle-query metric. In this work, we show that the alternating sequence of global and local Grover operators, formed by repeatedly applying a fixed product of global and local Grover operators, can achieve a lower expected circuit depth than the GRK algorithm. Through systematic analysis, we obtain its exact success probability, expected depth, and asymptotically optimal parameters. We derive the boundary, characterized by the ratio between the depths of the oracle and the global diffusion operator, separating the depth-optimal and oracle-optimal partial-search algorithms. When the depths of the oracle and the global diffusion operator are comparable, our proposed alternating partial-search sequence can reduce the minimum expected circuit depth by more than 20\% compared with the GRK algorithm.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study applies information-theoretic measures, specifically entropy and complexity, to analyze the quantum dynamics of the $^{48}	ext{Ca}$ nucleus. By comparing results obtained under weak (regular) versus strong (chaotic) quadrupole interactions, the authors demonstrate that the system's complexity is sensitive to the degree of internal interaction, providing a diagnostic tool for quantum chaos in nuclear physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects concepts from quantum chaos theory (regular vs. chaotic dynamics) with information theory (entropy/complexity) in a nuclear structure context, providing a quantitative diagnostic tool for understanding complex many-body quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the differences in information entropy and complexity measures for many-body quantum systems as they transition between regular and chaotic dynamical regimes.

**Main result.** The complexity and entropy of the nuclear states are demonstrably different between the regular and chaotic regimes, indicating that the internal quadrupole interactions significantly influence the system's information structure.

**Method.** The authors diagonalize the two-body Hamiltonian for the $^{48}	ext{Ca}$ nucleus to obtain eigenvectors, and then calculate Shannon entropy ($S_\alpha$) and a disequilibrium measure ($D$) to define complexity ($C=S \cdot D$).

**Model / system.** The system analyzed is the $^{48}	ext{Ca}$ nucleus, modeled using the shell model Hamiltonian $\hat{H} = \hat{H}_m - \chi \hat{Q} \cdot \hat{Q}$. The analysis compares two regimes: a regular one ($\chi = 0.01$) and a chaotic one ($\chi = 0.25$).

**Key observables.** Shannon Entropy ($S_\alpha$), Complexity ($C$), Energy Spectra (Power spectrum $P(f)$), and eigenvector probability distributions ($P_{\alpha k}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The interaction strength parameter $\chi$, which controls the transition from regular ($\chi=0.01$) to chaotic ($\chi=0.25$) behavior.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the validity of using information-theoretic measures (entropy and complexity) to characterize the underlying quantum dynamics and chaos in the nuclear shell model.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the energy levels across different $\chi$ values, the probability distributions of components for both regimes, and plots showing Shannon entropy and Complexity as functions of the basis state ($\alpha$) and the disequilibrium measure ($D$).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the theoretical framework using complexity measures, applies these measures to the $^{48}	ext{Ca}$ nucleus Hamiltonian across varying interaction strengths ($\chi$), and compares the resulting entropy and complexity values to diagnose the transition from regular to chaotic quantum dynamics.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In complex systems theory, there are different ways to describe a system in terms of information, such as emergence (Shannon entropy), self-organization, and com- plexity. These measures provide information about the dynamic behavior of a complex system. We study the differences in entropy and complexity for many-body systems undergoing a transition from a regular to a chaotic regime. To do this, we analyze the eigenvectors of the 48 Ca nucleus for different quadrupole-type two-body interactions. We obtain the eigenvectors by diagonalizing the two-body Hamiltonian for 48 Ca us- ing the Antoine code. We then calculate the entropy and complexity for the different quadrupole-type interactions. The differences found in information entropy and com- plexity are clear when comparing a regular system with a chaotic one. We find that the complexity of the regular and chaotic states of 48 Ca shows differences associated with its internal interactions.

</details>

### [Cubical Sheaf Complexes with Constant Expansion with Applications to Asymptotically Good qLTCs](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28028v1)

**Authors:** Yeyuan Chen, Miryam Mi-Ying Huang, Yinchen Liu, Er-Cheng Tang  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.28028v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.28028_figures/2609.28028_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Edges and cubes in a decorated Cayley cubical complex. In (a), the two paths to the opposite vertex agree by bc′ = cb′. The two blue opposite edges are vertices joined by an edge in the direction-j parallel-face graph. In (b), the three bits record colors in directions (i, j, k); each circle represents a copy of G, and the dots and segments select one cube. The shaded square has type {j, k}.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a sophisticated mathematical construction for building quantum locally testable codes (qLTCs) with optimal asymptotic parameters. It achieves this by embedding the coding problem within the framework of cubical sheaf complexes defined over arithmetic quotients of trees. The work is significant for providing explicit, polynomial-time methods to construct highly efficient quantum error correction codes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the reliance on complex combinatorial structures (sheaves, complexes) to guarantee robust coding properties is a theoretical advancement that could inspire new ways to analyze error correction in complex quantum systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to construct quantum error correcting codes (qLTCs) that simultaneously achieve constant rate, linear distance, and constant soundness while maintaining bounded local check and qubit degrees.

**Main result.** The authors present an explicit, polynomial-time computable family of codes derived from $r$-dimensional cubical sheaf complexes, achieving the desired asymptotic bounds for qLTCs.

**Method.** The construction method combines cubical sheaf complexes with a uniform product-expansion theorem applied to Reed-Solomon codes on arithmetic cubical complexes, leveraging the local-to-global framework.

**Model / system.** The theoretical model is quantum error correction, specifically focusing on CSS codes derived from the structure of cubical sheaf complexes built upon arithmetic quotients of products of trees.

**Key observables.** Code parameters such as rate ($K/N$), minimum distance ($D$), and soundness ($ho$), which are shown to be bounded away from zero or linear in $N$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The construction is parameterized by fixed integers $r \ge 4$ and $2 \le k \le r-2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The construction relies on the existence of arithmetic quotients of products of trees and the successful establishment of uniform product expansion for the local Reed-Solomon codes and their duals.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the structure of a decorated Cayley cubical complex, detailing how edges and cubes are defined and how opposite edges must agree.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the general framework of cubical complexes and sheaf theory, applies this to arithmetic quotients of products of trees, establishes the necessary local coding conditions (product expansion), and culminates in the main theorems proving the existence of the desired quantum codes.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

For every fixed integers $r \ge 4$ and $2 \le k \le r-2$, we construct $r$-dimensional cubical sheaf complexes whose degree-$k$ CSS codes have positive constant rate, linear distance, and constant soundness, with bounded row and column weights. Taking $r=4$ and $k=2$ gives a family of asymptotically good binary qLTCs.   At the core of our construction is a uniform product-expansion theorem for explicit Reed-Solomon codes on norm-one evaluation sets. The key point is that the expansion constant stays bounded away from zero as the local code lengths grow. We place these codes on arithmetic cubical complexes, obtaining constant local expansion for both the resulting sheaf and its dual. Together with the local-to-global framework of Dinur, Lin, and Vidick (FOCS 2024) and sheaf duality, this gives linear distance and constant soundness, while an asymmetric choice of local code dimensions gives positive rate. The resulting codes are explicit and polynomial-time computable.

</details>

### [Distilling Datasets into Shallow Circuits for Quantum Machine Learning](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.28229v1)

**Authors:** Guang Lin, Qibin Zhao  
**Type:** both · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.28229v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.28229_figures/2609.28229_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Three approaches to quantum training data. (a) Per-sample preparation. (b) Hybrid distil- lation with a separate encoding. (c) Quantum dataset distillation (QDD).</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2609.28229_figures/2609.28229_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 2: Finite-shot parameter-shift training on MNIST: test accuracy vs. cumulative shots.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces Quantum Dataset Distillation (QDD) to solve the high computational cost of training quantum models on large datasets. QDD synthesizes the entire dataset into a small set of shallow, executable quantum circuits. This approach drastically reduces the required quantum shots while maintaining high classification accuracy, making QML more practical for real quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work directly addresses the practical resource constraints of running QML algorithms on near-term quantum hardware by creating data representations that are inherently compatible with shallow circuit execution.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Training quantum machine learning models is computationally expensive because the loading circuit must be re-executed for every data sample at every training step.

**Main result.** The proposed Quantum Dataset Distillation (QDD) achieves accuracy comparable to full-data training using only a tiny fraction (e.g., 10 circuits) of the original dataset size.

**Method.** QDD directly distills the entire dataset into a small set of shallow, executable quantum circuits by optimizing circuit parameters using a joint objective function that matches both mean features and density matrices.

**Model / system.** The system involves Quantum Machine Learning (QML) applied to classical data (like MNIST images), where data states are represented by low-rank tensor network states realized by shallow, parameterized quantum circuits.

**Key observables.** Test accuracy on MNIST/Fashion-MNIST, cumulative shot count reduction (up to $100	imes$), and loading circuit cost (number of two-qubit gates).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of distilled circuits per class (IPC), the loading budget, and the weight $\alpha$ balancing distribution matching vs. density-matrix matching.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The key assumption is that the synthetic samples must be parameterized *as* executable circuits, avoiding the information loss associated with post-hoc state-preparation synthesis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between different data preparation methods (per-sample vs. QDD) and show performance metrics like shot savings and accuracy vs. sample budget.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the computational bottleneck, proposes QDD as a solution, details the joint optimization objective (distribution and density matrix matching), presents results on MNIST/Fashion-MNIST showing massive shot savings, and validates the method on real quantum hardware.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In quantum machine learning, training a quantum model requires each sample to be prepared as a quantum state by a loading circuit that must be re-executed for every shot at every training step. The total burden therefore scales with both the number of samples and the cost of preparation. Existing approaches reduce the loading cost of individual inputs or distill data and compress their representations before applying a separate quantum encoding. This separation can leave resulting samples costly to prepare or cause additional loss of the information during subsequent compilation into shallow circuits. We propose quantum dataset distillation (QDD), which distills the full dataset directly into a small set of shallow circuits. Each synthetic sample is parameterized as a staircase circuit corresponding to a low-rank tensor network, making the sample and its loading circuit the same object. The circuit parameters are optimized classically with exact gradients using distribution matching under an explicit loading budget, without post-hoc state-preparation synthesis. On MNIST and Fashion-MNIST, only 10 circuits per class ($0.0017\times$ the full dataset size) achieve accuracy comparable to full-data training and outperform selection baselines under matched sample and loading budgets. In finite-shot training, QDD reaches 95\% of the full-data accuracy with more than $100\times$ fewer cumulative shots. Additionally, we validate QDD on real quantum hardware, demonstrating its practical deployment potential.

</details>

### [Equivalence of maximal and generic reachability for non-universal Variational Quantum Circuits](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27053v1)

**Authors:** Vishal S. Ngairangbam, Michael Spannowsky  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.27053v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.27053_figures/2609.27053_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 1. Stratification of the sphere S2 under SO(2) rotations about the X axis and its relation to different orientations of the solution manifold M∗. At the top left, M∗is a single point on S2 and the initial point must lie on the exact orbit. When M∗</sub>

</details>

<details><summary>📷 Fig 2</summary>

<img src="2609.27053_figures/2609.27053_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2. Median of the ∆Er and its interquartile range as error bars over ten randomly initialised training runs on a fixed random reference state for n = 5 (top) and n = 7 (bottom) verifying that overparametrisation is necessary for improved convergence where ∆¯d0 = 0 denotes the threshold.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work rigorously analyzes the limitations of Variational Quantum Circuits (VQCs) by establishing conditions for state reachability on the quantum state manifold. It derives a crucial dimensional criterion stating that the target set must have sufficient topological dimension relative to the circuit's generic orbit. This provides a powerful diagnostic tool for designing trainable and effective quantum algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous mathematical guarantees regarding the limitations of current quantum machine learning approaches, specifically diagnosing when VQCs are fundamentally incapable of reaching a target state due to geometric constraints.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes the mathematical conditions for determining the reachability and trainability of non-universal Variational Quantum Circuits (VQCs) on the pure state manifold.

**Main result.** It proves the equivalence of maximal and generic reachability and derives a necessary dimensional criterion: the target set's dimension must be at least as large as the co-dimension of the generic orbit for non-zero probability of reachability.

**Method.** The analysis employs tools from differential geometry, including the principal orbit-type theorem, the Quantum Fisher Information Matrix (QFIM), and measure theory to analyze the image of the VQC parameter map.

**Model / system.** The system involves Variational Quantum Circuits (VQCs) acting on an n-qubit system, whose dynamics are analyzed on the pure state manifold (MN). The focus is on the orbit generated by the VQC parameters from a reference state.

**Key observables.** Reachability probability, rank of the QFIM, topological dimension of the solution manifold, and the co-dimension of the generic orbit.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The depth of the VQC (finite-depth optimization), the structure of the reference state ($\psi_r$), and the target state ($\psi^*$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes the global minimum of the cost function lies on a smooth subset and that the solution set can be realized via a real analytic map embedding a solution manifold.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the stratification of the sphere under SO(2) rotations, showing how generic circular orbits cover the sphere except for fixed antipodal points.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the equivalence of maximal and generic reachability using the principal orbit-type theorem, deriving necessary and sufficient conditions based on the smoothness of the cost function minimum, and concluding with a dimensional criterion derived from local surjectivity arguments.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Employing problem-specific non-universal Variational Quantum Circuits aligned with a suitable state preparation has become a standard approach to counter the difficulty in training universal ansätze. However, due to their non-universality, diagnosing their reachability and trainability remains reference-state-specific. In this work, we establish the equivalence of maximal reachability and generic reachability over arbitrary reference states as a consequence of the \emph{principal orbit-type theorem}. Thereafter, assuming that the global minimum of the cost function is achieved on a subset that can be described as the image of a smooth function, we derive necessary and sufficient conditions for non-zero probability of reachability under generically sampled reference states. Furthermore, when the solution set is assumed to be realised through a real analytic map that embeds a solution manifold, we show that local surjectivity is generically obtained on the entire solution manifold if it is attained at a single point. As a practical design rule, the need for local surjectivity automatically translates to a necessary dimensional criterion: reachability requires the target set's topological dimension to be at least as large as the co-dimension of the generic orbit. Numerical simulations show that finite-depth optimisation consistently fails when the dimensional obstruction applies, while unobstructed cases show improved convergence.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the complex interplay between SDW order, nematicity, and superconductivity in FeSe1-xS_x. Using high-field magnetotransport and TDO measurements, the authors report finding an incipient SDW phase stabilized within the nematic state. This suggests that tuning or weakening nematicity is key to stabilizing the magnetic order that promotes superconductivity in these materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The interplay between competing electronic orders (SDW, nematicity, SC) in iron-based superconductors is a canonical problem in strongly correlated electron physics, providing experimental insights into pairing mechanisms.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Investigating the competition and coexistence between Spin-Density Wave (SDW) order and superconductivity (SC) in iron-chalcogenide superconductors, specifically focusing on the role of the nematic electronic state.

**Main result.** The study reports evidence for an incipient SDW phase stabilized within the nematic state of FeSe1-xS_x under high magnetic fields, suggesting that weakening nematicity promotes the dominant superconducting pairing mechanism.

**Method.** The research employed magnetotransport measurements (resistivity, quantum oscillations), Tunnel Diode Oscillator (TDO) frequency response, and magnetic torque anisotropy measurements.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the iron-chalcogenide superconductor FeSe1-xS_x, studied under varying conditions including chemical doping (x), applied hydrostatic pressure, and high magnetic fields (up to 68 T).

**Key observables.** Longitudinal resistivity upturns, TDO frequency anomalies, quantum oscillation frequencies (F), and magnetic torque anisotropy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** High magnetic fields (up to 68 T), chemical doping concentration (x), and applied pressure (p).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The interpretation of resistivity upturns and TDO anomalies as definitive evidence for field-induced electronic order (SDW).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams showing the field-induced SDW phase in relation to doping and pressure, alongside transport and TDO behavior measurements demonstrating the SDW development.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes transport and TDO data across different doping levels and pressures, mapping out the phase boundaries where the field-induced SDW order emerges within the nematic superconducting state.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin-density wave (SDW) order and superconductivity frequently compete and coexist in unconventional superconductors, where spin fluctuations often mediate superconducting pairing. In iron-chalcogenide superconductors, FeSe$_{1-x}$S$_x$, SDW order has only been detected under applied pressure, while both spin and nematic fluctuations are involved in determining their rich superconducting phase diagrams. Here, we report evidence for an incipient SDW phase, within the nematic state of FeSe$_{1-x}$S$_x$, revealed in magnetic fields up to 68~T. Once superconductivity is quenched, we observe sharp upturns in longitudinal resistivity accompanied by anomalies in tunnel diode oscillator frequency response and torque anisotropy, consistent with a field-induced electronic order. Dominant low-frequency quantum oscillations reveal a small reconstructed Fermi surface, consistent with a field-induced SDW order. Direct experimental comparisons with a pressure-tuned nematic, analogue, FeSe$_{0.96}$S$_{0.04}$, demonstrate that SDW phases are stabilized within the nematic phase of FeSe$_{1-x}$S$_x$ via both chemical substitution and applied pressure. These findings reveal that by weakening nematicity, the SDW orders are stabilised, which promotes the dominant superconducting pairing mechanism in iron chalcogenides.

</details>

**Summary.** This work reports the discovery of an intrinsic anomalous Hall effect in the surface conduction layer of the non-magnetic insulator FeSi. By correlating the AHE onset with the emergence of surface conducting states, the authors argue for the existence of an emergent, surface-confined magnetic order. This establishes FeSi as a key platform for studying the interplay between strong correlations and anomalous transport phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** The observation of intrinsic AHE without bulk magnetism provides a clean platform to study emergent magnetic order driven purely by surface electronic structure, which is relevant to understanding topological surface states and symmetry breaking in correlated systems.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research investigates the rare phenomenon of observing an intrinsic anomalous Hall effect (AHE) in a material that lacks bulk magnetism, suggesting surface-confined symmetry breaking.

**Main result.** The authors report a robust, field-hysteretic intrinsic AHE confined to the surface conduction regime of the non-magnetic insulator FeSi, indicating emergent magnetic order at the surface.

**Method.** The study combines systematic single-crystal growth, low-temperature magnetotransport measurements (Hall and longitudinal resistivity), and thermodynamic characterization (susceptibility and specific heat).

**Model / system.** The physical system is the non-magnetic narrow-gap insulator FeSi. The focus is on the electronic properties of the surface conduction states (CSS) that emerge at low temperatures.

**Key observables.** Anomalous Hall conductivity ($\sigma_{Axy}$), low-temperature electrical conductivity ($\sigma_{xx}$), magnetic susceptibility ($\chi(T)$), and specific heat ($C/T$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures ($T_{AHE} \simeq 45-70 	ext{ K}$), surface roughness ($R_q$), and sample thickness.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The AHE is intrinsic and surface-driven, confirmed by conductivity scaling, and the bulk material remains non-magnetic despite surface magnetism.

**Figures summary.** Figures show normalized resistance vs. T for different sample types, Hall resistivity ($ho_{xy}$) vs. applied field isotherms demonstrating hysteresis, and $\sigma_{Axy}(T)$ tracking the CSS temperature scale.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents evidence for surface conduction states (CSS) via thickness/roughness dependence, followed by the observation of a robust, hysteretic AHE confined to this surface regime, while simultaneously ruling out bulk magnetic origins via thermodynamic measurements.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The realization of an intrinsic anomalous Hall effect (AHE) in the absence of bulk magnetism is a rare phenomenon, requiring a spontaneous breaking of the time-reversal symmetry within the boundary states without a thermodynamic phase transition. Here, we report a robust, zero-field-hysteretic intrinsic AHE confined to the low-temperature surface-conduction regime of the prototypical narrow-gap insulator FeSi. By systematically investigating single crystals grown via Czochralski and chemical vapor transport methods with controlled thicknesses and surface preparations, we demonstrate that low-temperature electrical conductivity correlates with surface roughness and anti-correlates with sample thickness, confirming the emergence of surface conducting states (CSS) below $T_{\rm CSS} \simeq 55-75\text{ K}$. Meanwhile, a robust, field-hysteretic and temperature-independent anomalous Hall conductivity is found below $T_{\rm AHE}\simeq45-70\text{ K}$, implying the presence of magnetic order in the surface conduction regime. Bulk magnetic susceptibility and specific-heat measurements reveal no thermodynamic phase transitions, verifying that the sample interior remains a non-magnetic insulator. Analyses of conductivity scaling and effective three-dimensional conductivity reveal the intrinsic nature of this surface-driven AHE, evidencing the presence of an emergent magnetic order within the metallic surface of a non-magnetic bulk insulator. These findings establish FeSi as a compelling platform to investigate the interplay between strong electron correlations and anomalous Hall transport.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides a deep mathematical characterization of entanglement for GHZ diagonal quantum states in multipartite systems. By utilizing the Heisenberg-Weyl decomposition and Fourier analysis, the authors derive multiple necessary and sufficient criteria to distinguish entangled states from fully separable ones within this specific state manifold. This advances the theoretical understanding of multipartite entanglement witnesses.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical characterization of separability boundaries within highly symmetric quantum state manifolds is crucial for designing robust quantum communication protocols and understanding resource theories.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the entanglement properties and develops necessary and sufficient separability criteria for a specific class of mixed multipartite quantum states called GHZ diagonal states.

**Main result.** The authors establish that the set of fully separable states within the GHZ simplex is characterized by the convex hull of depolarized pure product projectors, and they provide multiple criteria (e.g., $\ell_1$ norm, majorization) to detect entanglement.

**Method.** The analysis heavily relies on the Heisenberg-Weyl (HW) decomposition, Fourier transforms (DFT), and applying concepts from majorization theory to derive algebraic and geometric separability witnesses.

**Model / system.** The system is an $N$-qudit quantum system, restricted to states $ho$ that are diagonal in a GHZ-type basis, $ho = \sum_{\Lambda} p_\Lambda |\phi_\Lambdaangle\langle\phi_\Lambda|$.

**Key observables.** GHZ probabilities ($p_\Lambda$), the correlation hypermatrix $\mathcal{C}(ho)$, the $\ell_1$ norm of the flattened correlation tensor ($\Phi_1(f)$), and partial transpose eigenvalues.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qudits ($N$), the local dimension ($d$), and the degree of depolarization ($\epsilon$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to states diagonal in the GHZ basis, and the derived criteria are necessary but not always sufficient for general separability.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2 illustrates the planar shadow of the GHZ simplex for $N=3$ qubits, showing the vertices, the 'FS inner shadow' (green polygon), and the locations of specific test states.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first defining the state class and its algebraic structure via the HW decomposition. It then develops multiple criteria (majorization, $\ell_1$ norm) to test for separability. Finally, it characterizes the intersection of the GHZ simplex with the fully separable set using explicit constructions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper, we investigate the family of GHZ diagonal states, which are mixed multipartite quantum states that are diagonal in a GHZ type basis. We show that the class of GHZ diagonal states is highly symmetric and exhibits rich entanglement properties, making it relevant for applications in multipartite quantum key distribution.

</details>

**Authors:** Luca Pieleanu, Moritz Laber, Narayan G. Sabhahit, Dmitri Krioukov  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.27141v1>

A root vertex in community 1 reaches the giant connected component (GCC) through a potential neighbor in community s with probability p1sξs for all s ∈{1, 2, 3, 4}, where ξs is the probability that a vertex in community s belongs to the GCC. Consequently, 1 −p1sξs is the probability that this neighbor does not con- nect the root vertex to the GCC. Assuming these events are asymptotically independent, which is valid when local neigh- borhoods are asymptotically tree-like, multiplying over all po- tential neighbors yields the probability 1 −ξ1 that the root vertex does not belong to the GCC, giving the self-consistency equation (5).</sub>

**Summary.** This paper extends percolation theory to the Stochastic Block Model by analyzing scenarios where the underlying community structure evolves with network growth. It develops and applies advanced analytical tools, including self-consistent equations and branching processes, to determine the percolation threshold. The key finding is that the critical threshold deviates from simple expectations due to the dynamic nature of community connections.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on network theory, the analysis of phase transitions (percolation) in complex, evolving structures shares mathematical techniques with critical phenomena in statistical mechanics, particularly concerning connectivity thresholds.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the percolation threshold in the Stochastic Block Model (SBM) when the community structure is not fixed but evolves as the network size grows.

**Main result.** The critical average degree for percolation is not generally equal to 1, and the transition depends critically on how connections are distributed across the evolving community structure.

**Method.** The authors employ two primary analytical methods: linearized self-consistent equations for locally tree-like models and a community-scale branching process for models with persistent clustering.

**Model / system.** The system studied is the Stochastic Block Model (SBM), specifically analyzing sequences of SBMs where the number, sizes, and connection probabilities between communities can all change with the network size.

**Key observables.** Percolation threshold (emergence of a Giant Connected Component), average degree ($ar{k}$), and average local clustering coefficient ($ar{c}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The scaling behavior of community sizes and connection probabilities ($	ext{p}_{rs}(n)$) with network size $n$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis distinguishes between regimes: locally tree-like (allowing spectral analysis) versus clustered (requiring branching processes), and notes limitations when intercommunity correlations are strong.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates connection-probability maps for different SBM sequences showing how connection probabilities vary with size. Figure 2 and 3 illustrate the mathematical frameworks used: the self-consistency equation and the community-scale branching process, respectively.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the general framework for evolving SBMs, then applying two distinct analytical tools (self-consistency equations and branching processes) depending on the local structure, and finally comparing these results to highlight the limitations of simpler assumptions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The stochastic block model is a paradigmatic model of networks with community structure. Yet percolation in the model has been studied primarily in cases with a fixed block structure, even though in real networks, the community structure may evolve as the network grows. Here we study percolation in sequences of stochastic block models in which the numbers and sizes of communities, as well as the intra- and intercommunity connection probabilities may all change with the network size. We analyze five such sequences using two methods: linearized self-consistent equations for the locally tree-like models and a branching process at the community scale for the models with nonvanishing clustering. We find that the critical average degree is not generally equal to $1$, even in locally tree-like sequences, because the transition depends on how connections are distributed across the evolving community structure. We also show that the community-scale branching process accurately predicts the transition when intercommunity connections are sufficiently sparse, even in the presence of nonvanishing clustering, while a geometric stochastic block model sequence demonstrates the limitations of this method when correlations between intercommunity connections cannot be neglected. These results extend percolation studies in the stochastic block model to more realistic scenarios with evolving community structure, and may provide new methods to derive the upper and lower bounds for the percolation threshold in geometric long-range percolation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a significant theoretical advancement in fault-tolerant quantum computation, proving that universal computation can be achieved with purely logarithmic time overhead and constant space overhead. The authors achieve this using advanced techniques involving transversal logical gates on locally testable codes. This result significantly improves upon previous state-of-the-art bounds for building scalable quantum computers.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides concrete, optimized theoretical bounds on the resource overheads (time/space) for universal quantum computation, which is foundational knowledge for designing practical quantum hardware architectures.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to achieve fault-tolerant quantum computation with the optimal overheads of purely logarithmic time and constant space overhead.

**Main result.** It proves that such computation is possible using transversal logical CCZ gates on good quantum locally testable codes, achieving a strictly logarithmic time overhead.

**Method.** The construction involves transferring logical qubits between dense storage and active subspaces while reusing ancillary workspace, or alternatively, modifying quantum Reed-Solomon magic-state distillation.

**Model / system.** The model is an adaptive Clifford+CCZ quantum circuit operating under a fault-tolerance framework using CSS codes, specifically $\Delta$-qLDPC and $(ho, \Delta)$-qLTCs.

**Key observables.** The primary observables are the time and space overheads required to simulate the ideal logical gate operations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters include the target error $\epsilon$, the circuit width $W$, and the circuit depth $D$. The time overhead is $O(D \log(WD/\epsilon))$ and the space overhead is constant.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof relies on the fault path avoiding specific 'bad' families of errors, and the analysis is conditional on the existence of certain positive constants.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a rigorous framework by first establishing fault-tolerance using weight enumerators to constrain bad fault paths, then details the main construction using transversal CCZ gates for state transfer, and finally presents an alternative scheme via recursive distillation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We prove that constant-space-overhead fault-tolerant quantum computation can be achieved with provably strictly logarithmic time overhead, improving over the best known results with additional subpolylogarithmic factors. Our main construction uses polynomial-subrank transversal logical $\CCZ$ gates on good quantum locally testable codes to implement addressable universal computation by transferring batches of logical qubits between dense storage and active logical subspaces while reusing the same ancillary workspace. Logical $\CCZ$ gates are implemented directly by the transversal operation, so only stabilizer resource states require separate preparation. Furthermore, we give an alternative construction that also achieves purely logarithmic time overhead based on modifying the quantum Reed--Solomon magic-state distillation scheme of Nguyen and Pattison. Recursively applying a fixed distillation circuit protected by qLTCs of increasing block length eliminates the subpolylogarithmic time factor.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes using Mayer homology to achieve a quantum advantage in Topological Data Analysis (TDA) by developing efficient quantum algorithms. By analyzing Mayer Betti numbers, the authors circumvent limitations found in conventional TDA methods. The work provides theoretical resource estimates and discusses potential real-world applications across various scientific domains.

**Why it may be interesting.** The paper develops novel quantum algorithms for calculating topological invariants, which is a complex area of quantum computation that bridges quantum information theory with advanced mathematics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors address the limited prospect of achieving quantum advantage in conventional Topological Data Analysis (TDA) due to the difficulty in estimating small Betti numbers.

**Main result.** They develop quantum algorithms for Mayer homology, proving that Mayer Betti numbers are often exponentially large in the dense regime, thereby overcoming the normalization bottleneck of conventional quantum TDA.

**Method.** The work involves developing quantum algorithms for Mayer homology, which generalizes simplicial homology using $N$-nilpotent boundary operators, and deriving resource estimates for quantum computation.

**Model / system.** The model is Topological Data Analysis (TDA) applied to simplicial complexes, utilizing the mathematical framework of Mayer homology, which involves roots of unity and iterated boundary operators.

**Key observables.** Mayer Betti numbers ($eta_{d,p}$), minimum singular value of the boundary operator ($\sigma_{\min}$), and the Mayer Laplacian eigenvalue ($\gamma_{d,p}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The quantum resource estimate suggests a few hundred qubits and sixty million Toffoli gates are sufficient for quantum advantage.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the applicability of quantum algorithms to Mayer homology and notes that Mayer Betti numbers are not true topological invariants, depending on triangulation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the difference between conventional and Mayer homology (e.g., for a torus) and show the number of simplices for different dimensions.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews homology theory (simplicial vs. Mayer), introduces the quantum algorithms for Mayer Betti numbers, details the quantum complexity analysis, discusses classical benchmarks, and concludes with real-world applications in genomics and neuroscience.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Prior work has explored quantum algorithms for topological data analysis (TDA), revealing the possibility of exponential quantum speedups in estimating the ratios of Betti numbers to the dimension of the combinatorial Laplacian. However, this quantity is only non-vanishing and efficient-to-quantumly-estimate when Betti numbers are exponentially large, a case for which concrete examples are rarely known. Furthermore, certain randomized classical algorithms are sometimes efficient in this regime. Thus, the prospect of achieving quantum advantage in conventional TDA appears fairly narrow. Here, we address these challenges to the quantum advantage in TDA by developing quantum algorithms for Mayer homology, which generalize simplicial homology to $N$-nilpotent boundary operators ($\partial^N =0$) and have recently been successfully applied to real-world TDA contexts. We introduce an efficient quantum algorithm for estimating Mayer Betti numbers and their persistent counterparts. We then prove that for high-order simplices, Mayer Betti numbers are often exponentially large in the dense regime, which ameliorates the normalization bottleneck of conventional quantum TDA. In the same regime, we argue that existing dequantization algorithms developed for conventional TDA, when applied to Mayer homology, generally lose theoretical guaranties, facing certain structural barriers that prevent their practical utilities. We also provide logical resource estimates revealing that a quantum computer with roughly a few hundred qubits and sixty million Toffoli gates could solve Mayer homology problems beyond the capabilities of known classical approaches. Finally, we discuss real-world applications of Mayer homology in genomics, supersymmetry, drug discovery, and neuroscience, revealing the potential of our quantum algorithm to deliver real-world impacts via Mayer homology.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper establishes the quantum channel Stein's lemma, proving that the optimal error exponent for distinguishing two quantum channels equals the regularized channel relative entropy. It achieves this by developing a powerful uniform Stinespring approximation technique. The result provides a rigorous, exponentially strong converse for quantum hypothesis testing, crucial for understanding quantum channel capacity limits.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides fundamental, quantitative bounds on the distinguishability of quantum processes, which underpins the theory of quantum communication capacity and hypothesis testing.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper establishes the quantum channel Stein's lemma and proves an exponential strong converse for distinguishing between two unknown quantum channels, N and M.

**Main result.** The optimal type-II error exponent is shown to equal the regularized channel relative entropy, and the strong converse dictates that any rate exceeding this divergence forces the acceptance probability to decay exponentially.

**Method.** The proof relies on a uniform Stinespring approximation technique, comparing tensor powers of the null and alternative channel dilations, and utilizing iterative rate reduction from weak testing bounds.

**Model / system.** The system involves comparing two finite-dimensional quantum channels, N and M, acting on a quantum state space. The analysis is performed over multiple uses (tensor powers) of these channels.

**Key observables.** The regularized channel relative entropy, $D_\infty(N\|M)$, and the error probabilities (Type-I and Type-II).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The error tolerance $\epsilon \in (0,1)$, the blocklength $n$, and the divergence rate $d$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** All Hilbert spaces are assumed to be finite dimensional. The analysis requires the existence of a uniform Stinespring approximation.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the classical Stein's lemma to its quantum extension, proving the fixed-error exponent matches the regularized relative entropy. It then develops the core technical tool—the uniform Stinespring approximation—to establish the exponential strong converse for general adaptive strategies.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We establish the quantum channel Stein lemma at every fixed type-I error tolerance in $(0,1)$ and prove an exponential strong converse under arbitrary adaptive strategies. For any finite-dimensional channel pair, the optimal type-II exponent equals the regularized channel relative entropy and is attained by parallel strategies. When this divergence is finite, every strictly larger type-II rate forces the probability of accepting the null hypothesis to decay exponentially, uniformly over strategies and quantum memories. The proof rests on a uniform Stinespring approximation: tensor powers of the null dilation are approximated by tensor powers of the alternative dilation after applying an auxiliary linear map to the environment. The operator-norm error is exponentially small under a squared-norm bound at any rate above the regularized relative entropy. We construct the approximation from a weak testing bound by iterative rate reduction and a fixed-block tensor expansion with an exact residual. It implies right continuity at order one of the regularized sandwiched Rényi channel divergence, which yields the adaptive converse through the channel chain rule. We also obtain a sharp hockey-stick threshold and a subchannel smoothing asymptotic equipartition property for fixed and subexponentially vanishing diamond-norm errors.

</details>

Each QTask includes an abstract circuit with specific technical characteristics. Initially, a feature extractor processes this circuit to extract key task features. By evaluating these task features alongside the dynamic QNode states, the QRLQ core allocates each QTask to the QNode with the highest estimated action value. The abstract circuit, shown in the left panel, must be compiled and mapped to the physical qubit topology of the target quantum hardware, such as the ibm_sydney system depicted in the centre panel. This procedure produces a transpiled circuit, represented as a DAG in the right panel. Notably, the DAG of the transpiled circuit enables execution time estimation via the critical path using operation times Tj, and supports fidelity evaluation through error probabilities Ej.</sub>

**Summary.** This paper introduces QRLQ, a quantum reinforcement learning framework designed to optimally schedule quantum tasks across heterogeneous quantum cloud resources. By integrating Parameterized Quantum Circuits with a D3QN, QRLQ effectively manages the critical trade-off between minimizing execution cost and reducing overall system delay. The results demonstrate that QRLQ offers superior performance compared to existing heuristics while being significantly more parameter-efficient than classical deep RL methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on cloud orchestration, the use of PQCs as compact function approximators for complex resource management decisions is a key topic at the intersection of quantum machine learning and quantum networking.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the complex task of orchestrating quantum cloud computing resources (QaaS) by jointly minimizing execution costs and system delays.

**Main result.** The proposed QRLQ framework significantly outperforms heuristic baselines by achieving lower mean cost and substantial reductions in mean delay while maintaining high execution fidelity.

**Method.** It proposes QRLQ, a novel framework that integrates Parameterized Quantum Circuits (PQCs) with a Dueling Double Deep Q-Network (D3QN) for decision-making.

**Model / system.** The system models a heterogeneous quantum cloud environment composed of multiple Quantum Processing Units (QPUs) accessed via a QaaS model. The problem is formulated as an online sequential decision process (MDP).

**Key observables.** Mean Cost, Mean Delay, Execution Fidelity, Execution Time, and Waiting Time.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Qubit count, quantum volume, gate error rates, and the weighting factors ($\eta$) used in the scheduling score calculation.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The work assumes a uniform time-based pricing model and is motivated by the constraints of the NISQ era, though it aims for future fault-tolerant environments.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the system workflow (Fig. 2) and compare average execution time and waiting time across various scheduling policies (Fig. 6a-d).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem setup, details the QRLQ methodology integrating PQCs into D3QN, presents the cost/delay objective function, and validates the approach through extensive simulations comparing QRLQ against multiple heuristic and classical DRL baselines.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum cloud computing, delivered through the quantum-as-a-service (QaaS) model, provides access to quantum computing resources. However, applying uniform time-based pricing across fundamentally heterogeneous quantum resources significantly complicates task orchestration, particularly when addressing the tradeoff between execution costs and system performance. While heuristic methods rely on predefined scheduling rules, classical deep reinforcement learning (DRL) models may require more trainable parameters in this setting. Motivated by the potential of parameterised quantum circuits (PQCs) as compact function approximators, we propose QRLQ, a cost-delay-aware quantum cloud scheduling framework integrating PQCs with a dueling double deep Q-network (D3QN) to dynamically account for both cost and delay. Our simulation results show that QRLQ achieves lower mean cost and delay than the heuristic baselines, achieving a 5-11% lower mean cost relative to availability-based and rotation-based heuristics and reducing mean delay by 17% and 82% relative to the strongest and weakest heuristic baselines, respectively, while retaining execution fidelity within 2% of a fidelity-greedy policy. Compared with the classical DRL baseline, QRLQ achieves comparable scheduling performance while using 72% fewer trainable parameters. This work explores the feasibility of using QRL for task orchestration in quantum cloud environments and demonstrates its potential for cost-delay-aware quantum resource management.

</details>
