<a id="paper-2609.27103"></a>
### [Quantum-Enhanced Sampling of Schrödinger Bridges](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27103v1)

**Authors:** Tom Lollier, Eyal Neuman  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.27103v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

<img src="2609.27103_figures/2609.27103_fig1.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>Figure 1: Roadmap of the sampling procedure. The dashed step samples eQ0,T using the row pro- cedure in Section 6.3, with additional work beyond computing the scaling vectors. The path-error bound assumes a successful scaling run and ideal endpoint sampling. The conditional sampler requires coherent row preparation for P and the bridge Gibbs kernel. After failure, the entire condi- tional procedure is repeated independently with the endpoints fixed, requiring a constant expected number of attempts.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents quantum algorithms to enhance the sampling of Schrödinger bridges on finite state spaces. It achieves significant computational speedups over classical methods by utilizing quantum walks for path sampling and quantum Newton methods for coupling potentials. This work is crucial for efficiently simulating quantum processes constrained between known initial and final states.

**Why it may be interesting.** The quantum speedups demonstrated for sampling complex path distributions are highly relevant for simulating open quantum systems or quantum channels where path integrals or optimal control problems are central.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of efficiently sampling from the Schrödinger bridge distribution on a finite state space, which is mathematically equivalent to finding an optimal path law connecting two given marginal distributions.

**Main result.** The authors develop quantum algorithms that improve the computational complexity for both conditional path sampling (from quadratic to linear dependence on time horizon $T$) and endpoint coupling calculation (from quadratic to $N^{3/2}$ dependence on state size $N$) compared to classical methods.

**Method.** The core methods involve developing a quantum Gibbs sampler based on quantum walks for path sampling, and adapting a quantum box-constrained Newton method for computing the necessary Schrödinger potentials.

**Model / system.** The model is the Schrödinger bridge problem defined on a finite state space, requiring the estimation of the optimal path law $Q^*$ that minimizes the relative entropy between the path law and the reference Markov process $P$.

**Key observables.** Total variation error ($\epsilon$), spectral gap ($\delta$), and the complexity scaling with time horizon ($T$) and state-space size ($N$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Time horizon $T$, state-space size $N$, and the required accuracy $\epsilon$ or $\epsilon_s$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the existence of positive transition probabilities and assumes coherent input access for the quantum algorithms.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides a roadmap summarizing the entire sampling procedure, detailing the components for endpoint coupling and Markov bridge sampling.

**Paper structure.** The paper is structured around decomposing the problem into endpoint coupling and conditional path sampling. It then details the quantum enhancements for each part, culminating in an analysis of error bounds when approximating the resulting distributions.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider the dynamic Schrödinger bridge problem on a finite state space and exploit its Markov structure to decompose the problem into an endpoint coupling and a collection of conditional Markov bridges. To sample from the conditional bridges, we develop a quantum Gibbs sampler based on quantum walks on the bridge path space. Under uniformly positive transition probabilities, we derive a spectral-gap bound and provide an explicit procedure for preparing the initial state. Compared with the update bounds of the corresponding classical Gibbs sampler, our quantum walk-query complexity improves the dependence on the time horizon $T$ from quadratic to linear. For the endpoint coupling, we adapt a quantum box-constrained Newton method to compute the Schrödinger potentials, improving the matrix-scaling complexity, at fixed accuracy, from quadratic to $N^{3/2}$ in the state-space size $N$. Finally, we show that exponential reweighting extends both the Schrödinger bridge formulation and the Gibbs spectral-gap estimate to models with additive running costs.

</details>

### [Refuting the QAOA fixed-angle conjecture](http://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27703v1)

**Authors:** Lennart Binkowski  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.27703v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<details open><summary>📷 Fig 1</summary>

Edges and vertices are shaded by their graph distance to the centre edge, from saturated blue at distance 1, i.e., T 1 d , to faded grey at distance 3; the latter lie outside T 2 d , and the dashed stubs indicate that G extends even further. Shown are d = 3 on the left, and d = 4 on the right.</sub>

</details>

<img src="2609.27703_figures/2609.27703_fig2.jpg" width="500"><br>
<sub>FIG. 2. The complete bipartite graphs Kd,d for d = 1, 2, 3, . . . , 9; every vertex in one part is joined to every vertex in the other part, so each edge lies in (d −1)2 four-cycles. K9,9 serves as a counterexample to the fixed-angle conjecture for p = 2 on 9-regular graphs.</sub>

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes the fixed-angle conjecture for QAOA applied to MaxCut, a key problem in quantum optimization. It proves the conjecture holds for shallow depths or low-degree graphs, but crucially refutes it by providing a counterexample on the 9-regular graph $K_{9,9}$ at depth $p=2$. This refutation refines the theoretical understanding of when and how QAOA performance scales with graph structure.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly impacts the theoretical understanding of quantum algorithms for combinatorial optimization, providing concrete counterexamples that challenge existing assumptions about parameter optimization in QAOA.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates and refutes the fixed-angle conjecture concerning the performance of the Quantum Approximate Optimization Algorithm (QAOA) for solving the MaxCut problem.

**Main result.** The fixed-angle conjecture is proven false for depth-2 QAOA on 9-regular graphs, while it is confirmed to be true for depth-1 on any regular graph and for all depths on 2-regular graphs.

**Method.** The analysis uses theoretical bounds derived from the structure of the QAOA expectation value, comparing performance on general graphs to optimized tree structures, and employs advanced techniques like symmetry-adapted bases and tensor product factorization.

**Model / system.** The model is the QAOA applied to the MaxCut problem, formulated using an Ising encoding Hamiltonian $H_f$ on $d$-regular graphs, particularly focusing on complete bipartite graphs $K_{d,d}$.

**Key observables.** The approximation ratio $C(eta, \gamma) = F(eta, \gamma) / C_{\max}$, and the difference $\delta_e$ between the tree-optimal objective value and the edge expectation value.

**Important parameters / regimes.** QAOA depth ($p$), graph regularity ($d$), and the graph structure (e.g., $K_{9,9}$ for the counterexample).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the locality property of QAOA, which restricts correlations to a distance $p$ on the graph.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the tree structure $T_{p}^d$ used for comparison. Figure 2 shows complete bipartite graphs $K_{d,d}$ up to $d=9$, highlighting $K_{9,9}$ as the counterexample for $p=2$.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the context of the conjecture, then proves its validity for specific cases ($p=1$ and $d \le 2$), and finally presents a rigorous counterexample for $p=2$ on $K_{9,9}$ by analyzing the expectation values in a symmetry-adapted basis.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The fixed-angle conjecture for the quantum approximate optimisation algorithm (QAOA) is shown to be false for depth-2 QAOA on 9-regular graphs, and proven to be correct for depth-1 on any regular graph, as well, as for any depth on all 2-regular graphs.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses Raman spectroscopy to study 2D antiferromagnetic materials, finding that high-frequency scattering is not due to simple phonon overtones. Instead, the intensity is resonantly enhanced by localized Ni 3d electronic excitations, confirming the crucial role of local orbital-phonon coupling in these correlated magnets.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong coupling between electronic excitations (Ni 3d orbitals) and lattice vibrations (phonons) provides a concrete, measurable example of strong electron-phonon coupling in a correlated magnetic system, relevant to understanding charge transfer and orbital ordering.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine the microscopic origin of anomalously strong, high-frequency Raman scattering in 2D van der Waals antiferromagnetic materials, focusing on the interplay between local orbital excitations and lattice vibrations.

**Main result.** The high-frequency scattering is attributed to resonantly enhanced multiphonon scattering mediated by localized Ni 3d8 multiplet excitations, which is distinct from standard phonon overtones.

**Method.** Temperature and thickness-dependent Raman spectroscopy, complemented by theoretical cluster calculations and Franck-Condon modeling.

**Model / system.** The study investigates 2D single crystals of antiferromagnetic (Ni_xFe_{1-x})2P2S6, comparing the charge-transfer character of Ni2P2S6 with the Mott-Hubbard insulator regime of Fe2P2S6.

**Key observables.** Anomalously high and weakly temperature-dependent intensity ratio of higher-order modes relative to low-energy first-order phonons; specific phonon clusters around 800 cm-1 and 1170 cm-1.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature (4 K to 300 K); crystal thickness (down to 10 nm); doping concentration (x in Ni_xFe_{1-x}).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The anomalous scattering behavior suggests resonance phenomena involving localized Ni 3d8 multiplets, rather than simple harmonic overtones.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate raw and fitted Raman spectra for varying flake thicknesses, showing specific peak clusters; temperature dependence plots track mode softening and anomalies near the AFM transition.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes Raman spectra across varying thicknesses and compositions, contrasting the observed high-order features in Ni-containing compounds against the background of established phonon modes and theoretical expectations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Orbital degrees of freedom play a pivotal role in shaping the physical properties of two-dimensional (2D) van der Waals magnetic systems, strongly influencing electron-phonon coupling and intermediate-state dynamics. In this study, we present a comprehensive temperature and thickness-dependent Raman investigation of the single crystals of 2D van der Waals antiferromagnetic series (NixFe1-x)2P2S6. Utilizing Raman spectroscopy, we explore the interplay between local orbital excitations and lattice vibrations, observing higher-order phonon modes extending up to the fourth order. We observe an anomalously high and weakly temperature-dependent intensity ratio of these higher-order modes relative to low-energy first-order phonons. Theoretical cluster calculations and Franck-Condon modelling reveal that these features originate from resonantly enhanced multiphonon scattering mediated by localized intermediate Ni 3d8 multiplet excitations. The local orbital occupancy of these intermediate states couples strongly to lattice coordinates, providing a selective enhancement mechanism for specific phonon channels. Notably, these higher-order features are absent in the end-member Fe2P2S6. This distinction reflects the charge-transfer character of Ni2P2S6, which places optical transitions in resonance with local multiplet states, contrasted with the Mott-Hubbard insulator regime in Fe-rich analogues. Our findings clarify the microscopic origin of high-frequency Raman scattering in transition metal thiophosphates and underscore the central role of local orbital-phonon interactions in 2D correlated magnets.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.28367v1>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper investigates the difference between two measures of entanglement robustness: standard (separable noise) and generalized (any noise). It proves that for two-qubit systems, these two measures are always equal, but demonstrates a strict inequality for the qubit-qutrit system. This work provides a comprehensive classification of when entangled noise offers a quantifiable advantage over separable noise in finite quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous, quantitative classification of when entanglement resources are equally protected against different types of noise, which is fundamental for understanding noise resilience in quantum communication protocols.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper compares two measures of entanglement robustness—standard ($R_s$) and generalized ($R_g$)—to determine if entangled noise can erase entanglement more efficiently than separable noise.

**Main result.** The equality $R_s(ho) = R_g(ho)$ holds only for two-qubit states in bipartite systems, but a strict gap is found for the qubit-qutrit system, indicating an advantage for entangled noise.

**Method.** The analysis uses semidefinite programming (SDP) formulations, optimization over noise operators, and geometric arguments involving product-vector spaces to classify the equality boundaries.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on bipartite quantum states ($\mathcal{C}^m \otimes \mathcal{C}^n$) and general finite multipartite systems, analyzing robustness relative to full separability.

**Key observables.** The two robustness measures, $R_s(ho)$ and $R_g(ho)$, and the gap $R_s(ho) - R_g(ho)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimensions of the subsystems ($m, n$) and the number of parties ($N$) in the multipartite setting.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the PPT criterion characterizes separability in both robustness settings, and the results are for single-copy robustness, not asymptotic tasks.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by first establishing the problem in bipartite systems, proving equality for $2 \otimes 2$, then constructing a counterexample for $2 \otimes 3$, and finally generalizing the classification to arbitrary finite multipartite systems.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Can entangled noise erase entanglement more efficiently than separable noise? Standard robustness restricts the added noise to separable states; generalized robustness allows any state. We prove that the two costs coincide for every two-qubit state and give an explicit full-rank qubit--qutrit state with a strict gap. Positivity under partial transpose (PPT) characterizes separability in both dimensions, so the boundary is not caused by a failure of the PPT criterion. Instead, product-vector geometry permits a rank-one bridge between the two-qubit dual optimizations and supplies a two-dimensional completely entangled subspace for the qubit--qutrit separation. Local isometric embeddings complete the finite-dimensional bipartite classification, also for any fixed multipartite cut. Combined with known three-qubit separation, the result classifies universal equality relative to full separability in all finite multipartite systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes nonabelian vortices in a two-component condensate, showing that their topology is richer than previously assumed. By utilizing the mathematical structure of the pure braid group, the authors predict the existence of novel defects and propose a physical realization of a nonvolatile memory device that encodes information robustly in the vortex texture.

**Why it may be interesting.** The connection between topological defects in condensed matter systems (like vortices in superfluids or superconductors) and information storage mechanisms is highly relevant to quantum information science, particularly in realizing robust, non-dissipative memory elements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the topological classification and properties of nonabelian vortices arising in a two-dimensional film of a two-component condensate.

**Main result.** Nonabelian vortices possess an enriched internal structure compared to abelian vortices, allowing for the storage of exponentially large amounts of topologically robust information, which is demonstrated via a nonvolatile memory concept.

**Method.** The analysis uses homotopy group theory, classifying defects using the pure braid group $P_3$, and derives topological invariants like higher-order winding numbers ($Q^{(2)}$) from the geometry of the order parameter space.

**Model / system.** The model is a 2D film of a two-component condensate, described by an order parameter $\Psi \in \mathbb{C}^3$. The physics is governed by a Hamiltonian including kinetic, Mexican hat potential, and repulsive interaction terms.

**Key observables.** Relative winding numbers ($n_{ij}$), nonabelian vortex types ($[a], [b]$), Brunnian vortices (detected by $Q^{(2)}$), and the topological information capacity scaling with $S$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The repulsive interaction strength $U_0$, the characteristic length scale $\xi \sim \sqrt{A/U_0}$, and the number of stored strings $S$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the order parameter space $C_3(\mathbb{C})$ and assumes the system can be driven to transport vortices along an engineered cylinder geometry.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the vortex soup structure, the unit cell projections in the order parameter space, and the schematic for the nonvolatile memory storage mechanism using red and blue strings.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical system and its order parameter space, to classifying vortices using homotopy groups ($P_3$), identifying higher-order defects (Brunnian vortices), and finally applying this theory to devise a nonvolatile memory storage device based on vortex transport.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the topological classification and properties of nonabelian vortices that arise in a 2-dimensional film of a two-component condensate. For a repulsive interaction between the condensates, we realize an order parameter in the ordered configuration space of three complex numbers $\mathcal C_3(\mathbb C)$. Analyzing the homotopic properties, we find the emergence of elementary nonabelian vortex-antivortex pairs connected by energetic strings that are rooted in the repulsive interaction and carry the nonabelian information. Moreover, we predict the existence of Brunnian vortices, which are textures exhibiting zero net winding of the condensate phases and can only be detected by a higher-order winding number. Finally, to motivate technological applications rooted in nonabelian vortex transport, we devise a nonvolatile memory storage device that encodes topologically robust information.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes an all-optical method to engineer moiré-like band structures in Dirac materials by interfering multiple coherent laser beams. By utilizing the Floquet framework, the authors show that this technique can create tunable, valley-selective potentials, even achieving quasicrystalline order. This provides a powerful, non-mechanical route for dynamic band engineering and topological control in 2D materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics and quantum optics as it uses structured light fields (optical interference) to engineer synthetic gauge fields and topological properties in solid-state systems, mimicking effects usually requiring strain or magnetic fields.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to develop a purely optical method to dynamically generate moiré-like superlattices and quasicrystalline potentials in gapped Dirac materials, thereby bypassing the need for physical mechanical twisting.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that using multi-beam interference, especially with confined surface plasmon polaritons, allows for the creation of reconfigurable, all-optical platforms capable of engineering valley-dependent topology and miniband structures.

**Method.** The study employs the high-frequency Floquet framework and Magnus expansion to derive an effective time-independent Hamiltonian, which is then solved using a plane-wave basis and numerical diagonalization.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using low-energy Dirac fermions in a 2D material subjected to a time-periodic, spatially modulated effective mass potential generated by the interference of multiple coherent optical beams.

**Key observables.** Valley-selective miniband reconstruction, topological phase transitions (quantized Chern numbers), and the effective valley Landé g-factor.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of interfering beams ($N$), the optical field strength ($A_0$), the ratio of optical frequency to gap ($\hbar\omega / \Delta$), and the relative phases of the beams.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the high-frequency Floquet approximation and, for the plasmonic extension, assumes the ability to control relative phases to overcome the free-space diffraction limit.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the interference geometry for $N=3$ beams forming a triangular superlattice, and the resulting optical field intensity patterns.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the general Floquet framework for time-periodic driving, deriving the effective Hamiltonian via the Magnus expansion, applying this to multi-beam interference to generate spatially modulated potentials, and finally analyzing the resulting band structure and topological invariants.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We propose a purely optical Floquet framework to dynamically generate moiré-like superlattices and quasicrystalline potentials in a gapped Dirac material, entirely bypassing the need for physical twisting. The interference of three coherent circularly polarized beams produces a triangular, valley-dependent Floquet mass landscape that folds the Dirac spectrum into a mini-Brillouin zone, driving pronounced valley-selective miniband reconstruction. We further show that the bands near the Fermi level undergo a light-driven topological phase transition. Remarkably, interfering five or seven beams yields non-crystallographic spatial patterns, giving rise to optical quasicrystals with similar valley contrasting features. Although this purely optical approach provides a versatile route to band engineering, reaching deep subwavelength modulation requires overcoming the free-space diffraction limit. To overcome this limitation, we consider highly confined, linearly polarized surface plasmon polariton fields with controlled relative phases. Their phase-controlled interference generates a spatially alternating chiral mass with zero spatial average. Consequently, the two valleys exhibit degenerate energy spectra while hosting opposite valley-resolved miniband Chern numbers. Our findings establish a reconfigurable, all-optical platform for dynamically engineering moiré-like bands, quasicrystalline electronic states, and valley-dependent topology without mechanical twisting.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports the detection and characterization of quantum ionic magnetism in SrTiO3 using THz-pumped nonlinear optical techniques. By observing oscillatory magnetization that defies classical predictions, the authors confirm that the effect originates from quantum beating between ionic eigenstates. This work establishes a method for ultrafast control of ionic magnetism crucial for advanced spintronic and thermotronic applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a direct experimental pathway to observe and control quantum mechanical magnetic moments arising from lattice vibrations (ionic motion) using ultrafast THz pulses, which is highly relevant to understanding emergent magnetism in quantum materials.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the generation and ultrafast control of quantum ionic magnetism in the quantum paraelectric material SrTiO3.

**Main result.** The research observes oscillatory ionic magnetization that persists beyond the relaxation time of polarization oscillations, confirming a quantum mechanical origin distinct from classical electro-optic effects.

**Method.** The authors combine THz-pump-induced Second Harmonic Generation Circular Dichroism (SHG-CD) measurements with theoretical modeling using density matrix formalism.

**Model / system.** The system is quantum paraelectric SrTiO3, whose dynamics are modeled using the density matrix formalism, considering ionic displacements in a double-well or four-well potential. The THz pulse drives the system, probing coherence between ionic eigenstates.

**Key observables.** THz-helicity-dependent $\Delta	ext{ICD}$ and $\Delta	ext{ISHG}$ signals, fast ($\sim 1 	ext{ THz}$) and slow ($\sim 0.2 	ext{ THz}$) oscillatory components, and the time-domain decay of ionic magnetization.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures (10 K), the quantum paraelectric regime, and the temperature dependence of the slow oscillation frequency ($\sim (1-T/T^*)^{0.5}$ with $T^* \approx 60 	ext{ K}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The fast oscillations are attributed to the quantum ionic effect rather than purely non-magnetic contributions, and the slow oscillation's temperature dependence is linked to polar nanodomain evolution.

**Figures summary.** Figures show time-domain THz-helicity-dependent $\Delta	ext{ICD}$ signals, frequency analysis (FFT) confirming fast and slow oscillations, and temperature dependence plots illustrating the decay of these oscillations.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem, presents THz-CD measurements showing helicity-dependent oscillations, develops a quantum model using the density matrix, and finally compares simulation results (showing slow oscillations persisting after polarization decay) with the experimental observations.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetic moments carried by rotating ionic motion in crystals are becoming recognized as an important contribution to magnetism, angular momentum transport, and optical activity. However, efficient approaches to their dynamical control are lacking. Here, we report THz-driven generation and optical detection of quantum ionic magnetism in quantum paraelectric SrTiO3. We observe an oscillatory ionic magnetization without a corresponding oscillatory polarization, contradicting from the classical relation M~PXdP/dt while its suppression above the quantum paraelectric regime points to a quantum ionic origin. Analysis shows that this effect results from the beating between quantum ionic eigenstates whose degeneracy is lifted due to the directional symmetry breaking by the THz pulse. The presented approach provides a pathway for the ultrafast control of ionic magnetization in quantum materials for spintronic and thermotronic applications.

</details>

**Authors:** Markus Weißenhofer, Ritwik Mondal, M. S. Mrudul, Peter M. Oppeneer  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2609.27487v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **3/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **2/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **2/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **2/5** · `Tavis-Cummings & cavity-many-emitter` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **1/5** · `quantum measurements` **1/5**

**Summary.** This theoretical work analyzes spin dynamics, comparing the full non-Markovian description with the simplified inertial Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert (iLLG) equation. The authors demonstrate that the iLLG is merely a low-frequency truncation of the true dynamics. Consequently, the high-frequency nutation mode predicted by the iLLG is identified as a spurious artifact, cautioning against interpreting such spectral matches as definitive physical evidence.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to AMO physics and open quantum systems as it provides a rigorous warning about the pitfalls of approximating memory effects in open quantum dynamics, particularly when dealing with high-frequency spectral features.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the physical validity of high-frequency 'spin nutation modes' predicted by the inertial Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert (iLLG) equation, questioning if they are genuine physical excitations or mathematical artifacts.

**Main result.** The iLLG equation is shown to be a low-frequency, second-order truncation of the true, non-Markovian dynamics, leading to the prediction of spurious, high-frequency roots that do not exist in the exact spectrum.

**Method.** The authors compare the exact dynamics derived from a non-Markovian memory kernel (nM-LLG) against the simplified iLLG approximation by analyzing the frequency spectrum in the context of a minimal spin-cavity model.

**Model / system.** The system is a localized atomic spin moment coupled to additional dynamical variables (e.g., bosonic cavity modes), leading to a non-Markovian spin dynamics described by a memory kernel.

**Key observables.** Frequency spectra ($\omega$) of the coupled system, specifically comparing the iLLG nutation frequency ($\omega_{nut}^{iLLG}$) against the exact spectrum ($\omega_{exact}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling strength ($g_c$), the cavity resonance frequency ($\omega_c$), and the memory kernel parameters ($\alpha, \eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption being tested is the validity of low-frequency, second-order truncations of memory kernels; the iLLG itself is based on such a truncation.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the exact and iLLG spectra, showing the divergence of the iLLG nutation mode and the failure of the iLLG to capture physical phenomena like avoided crossings near resonance.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the full non-Markovian dynamics, derives the iLLG as a low-frequency approximation, and then uses a minimal model to explicitly calculate and compare the resulting frequency spectra, demonstrating the artifactual nature of the iLLG root.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spin dynamics is conventionally treated as inertia-free. The inertial Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation (iLLG) extends this with a second-order time derivative, predicting a high-frequency nutational mode that recent experiments have claimed to observe and interpreted as direct evidence for spin inertia. We show that when spin inertia is generated by eliminating additional dynamical degrees of freedom, the resulting exact spin-only description is generally non-Markovian. The iLLG is recovered as the low-frequency, second-order truncation of the underlying memory kernel. Using a minimal spin--cavity model, we show that the exact eigenfrequencies and those obtained via the iLLG differ and that the spin nutation mode predicted by the latter is a truncation-induced high-frequency artifact. This raises a broader question of the applicability of the iLLG obtained from analogous low-frequency truncations, which may likewise generate spurious high-frequency roots. Consequently, agreement of a high-frequency spectral feature with the iLLG nutation frequency does not, by itself, establish a physical nutation mode.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates how to achieve ultrastrong coupling between magnetoplasmon modes in patterned semiconductor nanostructures, eliminating the need for external resonant cavities. By controlling the geometry and symmetry of the electron gas, researchers can engineer strong, tunable interactions between topologically protected edge and bulk plasmon modes. This opens a new parameter space for quantum light-matter interaction based on material topology.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a novel, purely material-based method to engineer strong light-matter coupling, which is a central theme in cavity QED and quantum optics, by leveraging topological and symmetry properties of plasmons.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to achieve ultrastrong light-matter coupling between magnetoplasmon excitations without relying on conventional dedicated electromagnetic cavities.

**Main result.** By patterning the semiconductor material geometry (e.g., square or rectangular patches), the coupling strength can be engineered up to 0.1, leading to observable energy gaps and chiral-mode hybridization.

**Method.** The study combines experimental magnetospectroscopy on patterned AlGaN/GaN quantum wells with theoretical modeling using quantum Hamiltonians and Finite-Element Method (FEM) simulations.

**Model / system.** The system is a two-dimensional electron gas (2DEG) in patterned AlGaN/GaN quantum wells subjected to a perpendicular magnetic field. The physics is described by a quantum Hamiltonian incorporating magnetoplasmon modes and coupling terms.

**Key observables.** Magnetoplasmon-Polariton (MPP) doublet splitting, energy gaps at zero magnetic field, and the magnetic field dependence of the plasmon resonance frequency.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ultrastrong coupling regime ($\Omega_R/\omega_0 \approx 0.1$), magnetic field strength (up to 16 T), and the geometric symmetry (disk $	o$ square $	o$ rectangle).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to tailor interactions solely through the spatial symmetry of the confined medium, bypassing cavity-related field distortions.

**Figures summary.** Figures show SEM images of patterned patches (disk, square, rectangle) and corresponding magnetospectroscopy results illustrating the anti-crossing and mode hybridization.

**Paper structure.** The work progresses by first establishing the physical platform and the need to bypass cavities, then developing the quantum model and Hamiltonian, and finally demonstrating the control over coupling and mode hybridization by systematically changing the geometry's symmetry.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

In the ultrastrong light-matter coupling regime, the ability to confine the electric field to subwavelength scales and define an electromagnetic mode is essential. This task is typically achieved through dedicated cavities, such as THz metal resonators. Here, we instead introduce ultrastrong coupling directly between distinct magnetoplasmon excitations, leveraging solely the spatial symmetry of a confined electronic medium in the presence of a magnetic field. By patterning an AlGaN/GaN single quantum well hosting a two-dimensional electron gas into micrometer-scale patches, we tailor the mutual interactions between topologically protected edge and bulk magnetoplasmons, achieving coupling strengths of up to 0.1. The progressive breaking of rotational symmetry determines the coupling rules and leads to the emergence of energy gaps and unique chiral-mode hybridization. Our results highlight a path towards exploiting the symmetry class and topology of the magnetoplasmon wavefunction as a new parameter space for ultrastrong light-matter interaction without a dedicated cavity.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically models how chiral Andreev edge states behave when probed by a scanning gate microscope at a quantum Hall-superconductor interface. By analyzing the system using the Bogoliubov-de Gennes formalism, the authors predict and demonstrate that the local tip potential induces Aharonov-Bohm oscillations in the conductance. This technique offers a robust method to characterize superconducting edge states, even when disorder is present.

**Why it may be interesting.** The ability to use a local probe (SGM) to map out fundamental quantum interference phenomena (AB effect) in superconducting edge states provides a powerful conceptual link between topological superconductivity and mesoscopic transport measurements.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper theoretically investigates how chiral Andreev edge states (CAESs) at a quantum Hall-superconductor interface respond to local perturbations from a scanning gate microscope (SGM).

**Main result.** The SGM tip forces the electron and hole components of the CAESs to accumulate a relative magnetic phase, leading to observable Aharonov-Bohm-type conductance oscillations, which persist even in the presence of disorder.

**Method.** The analysis uses the Bogoliubov-de Gennes formalism and the Landauer-Büttiker approach to calculate non-local conductance ($G$) as a function of the tip position.

**Model / system.** The system is a Quantum Hall-Superconductor (QH-SC) hybrid structure. The physics is governed by the Bogoliubov-de Gennes Hamiltonian, incorporating the local potential of the SGM tip and the magnetic field via the vector potential.

**Key observables.** Aharonov-Bohm-type conductance oscillations ($\Delta G$), probability current maps, and the dependence of oscillation amplitude on the filling factor ($
u$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Filling factors ($
u=2, 
u=4$), magnetic field ($B$), disorder strength ($U_d$), and the tip position ($x_{	ext{tip}}, y_{	ext{tip}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes negligible Zeeman interaction and spin degeneracy; the analysis often relies on approximating the tip potential and assuming adiabatic evolution of the edge states.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate conductance maps ($\Delta G$) showing characteristic oscillations as the tip scans across the edge, and probability current maps demonstrating the decoupling of electron and hole paths.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical framework (Bogoliubov-de Gennes model) to deriving the AB oscillation formula based on mode decoupling, demonstrating this effect for $
u=2$ and $
u=4$, and finally extending the analysis to show the robustness of the signature against disorder.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We theoretically investigate the spatial response of chiral Andreev edge states at a quantum Hall-superconductor interface probed by scanning gate microscopy. Using the Bogoliubov-de Gennes formalism, we demonstrate that the localized potential of the scanning gate tip decouples the chiral Andreev edge states from the superconductor into independent electron and hole paths. In the $ν=2$ regime, this local separation forces the quasiparticles to accumulate a relative magnetic phase in the normal region, resulting in Aharonov-Bohm-type conductance oscillations that are captured by a geometric analytical model. At higher filling factors (e.g., $ν=4$), the tip progressively interacts with spatially distinct edge channels, dynamically altering the Andreev-induced mode mixing and modifying the transport resonances. Furthermore, we show that these highly localized interferometric signatures survive in the presence of disorder, even when conventional non-local transport oscillations are masked.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper reports on magneto-optical measurements on Py/NiO bilayers, observing a large, field-dependent spectral shift. The findings confirm that this shift arises from the control of antiferromagnetic magnon energies within the NiO layer. This establishes the bilayer as a promising platform for optically probing and manipulating antiferromagnetic spin dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** The direct experimental control of magnon energies using optical probes and magnetic fields is highly relevant to understanding and manipulating quantum excitations in magnetic materials, which connects to open quantum systems and quantum optics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to detect and characterize a large magneto-optical shift in Py/NiO bilayers, demonstrating strong magneto-optical coupling in an antiferromagnetic system.

**Main result.** The research provides direct experimental evidence for magnetic-field control of antiferromagnetic magnon energies via magneto-optical interactions in the NiO layer.

**Method.** Magneto-optical magnetometry measurements were performed on the bilayers, analyzing spectral shifts using a laser source and spectrometer.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a Py/NiO bilayer, where NiO acts as the antiferromagnetic layer and Py is the ferromagnetic underlayer. The effect is attributed to magnons intrinsic to the NiO layer.

**Key observables.** Pronounced, systematic, and monotonic spectral shift (wavelength shift) up to ~400 nm, and associated magneto-optical energy variation on the order of 10^7 eV.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The bilayer thickness is Py (20 nm)/NiO (200 nm). The shift saturates at higher applied magnetic fields.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The observed effect originates from magnons intrinsic to the AF NiO layer, and the contribution from the Py underlayer is negligible due to the large NiO thickness.

**Figures summary.** Figure 3 shows the wavelength shift induced by the magnetic field in reflectance spectra. Figure 4 compares Raman spectra, showing the shift only in the two-magnon (2M) mode.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical system and measurement setup, presents the observation of the field-dependent spectral shift, analyzes the shift magnitude and origin (confirming magnon control), and concludes by establishing the platform's potential.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Here, magneto-optical magnetometry measurements on Py/NiO bilayers reveal a pronounced magnetic-field-induced wavelength shift, demonstrating strong magneto-optical coupling in this antiferromagnetic system. A systematic and monotonic spectral shift of up to ~ 400 nm is observed as the applied magnetic field increases, saturating at higher fields. Quantitative analysis shows that the associated magneto-optical energy variation is on the order of 107 eV, comparable to the magnon energy scale in NiO. Owing to the large NiO thickness, the observed effect originates from magnons intrinsic to the antiferromagnetic NiO layer rather than from spin currents injected by the Py underlayer. These results provide direct experimental evidence for magnetic-field control of antiferromagnetic magnon energies via magneto-optical interactions, establishing Py/NiO bilayers as a promising platform for optically probing and manipulating antiferromagnetic spin dynamics.

</details>

**Authors:** Hou-Yin Li, Hua-Shan Lai, Jian-Lan Xie, Xiao-Chen Sun, Yan-Feng Chen, Cheng He

**Summary.** This paper reports the realization of a novel topological regime in an acoustic semimetal where bulk and boundary properties are simultaneously protected by distinct $\mathbb{Z}$ and $\mathbb{Z}_2$ invariants. By engineering the lattice symmetries, the authors demonstrate 'helical anomaly bulk states' (HABSs), establishing a new paradigm for understanding topological transport beyond conventional single-class theories.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work extends topological physics beyond single-invariant descriptions, providing a concrete, experimentally verifiable platform (acoustic metamaterials) to study complex, multi-faceted topological invariants.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates topological phases characterized by the concurrent presence of both $\mathbb{Z}$ (integer) and $\mathbb{Z}_2$ invariants, a regime that is theoretically challenging to realize and study.

**Main result.** The authors realize and characterize 'helical anomaly bulk states' (HABSs) in a 2D acoustic semimetal, demonstrating that bulk and boundary degeneracies can be jointly governed by distinct topological invariants.

**Method.** The research combines theoretical modeling (Hamiltonian construction, symmetry analysis) with advanced experimental techniques involving 3D printing and point-by-point sound pressure field measurements.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a two-dimensional acoustic semimetal realized in a staggered lattice structure. The model utilizes engineered degrees of freedom (layer, sublattice, gauge-staggered) to control symmetries like time-reversal and particle-hole symmetry.

**Key observables.** Bulk degeneracies protected by $\mathbb{Z}$ invariants (Weyl points), boundary degeneracies protected by $\mathbb{Z}_2$ invariants (twisted boundary arcs), and the resulting pseudospin-selective HABSs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The system's behavior is controlled by engineered symmetries and the interplay between the $\mathbb{Z}$ and $\mathbb{Z}_2$ topological invariants.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system can be effectively classified within the DIII symmetry class, and that the acoustic system can mimic electronic topological properties.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the band structure showing the coexistence of $\mathbb{Z}$ and $\mathbb{Z}_2$ topology, visualizing HESs/anti-HESs, and confirming the existence of HABSs through measured and simulated dispersions.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the theoretical framework of concurrent $\mathbb{Z}$ and $\mathbb{Z}_2$ topology to detailing the physical realization in an acoustic semimetal, culminating in the experimental measurement and characterization of the novel HABS states.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Topological classification constitutes a cornerstone of modern condensed-matter physics. Historically, topological systems have been predominantly characterized by invariants confined to a single topological class, such as topological insulators with a $\mathbb{Z}_2$ invariant and Weyl semimetals with an integer ($\mathbb{Z}$) topological charge. While such classification frameworks in principle permit gapless phases in which bulk and boundary degeneracies are protected by distinct invariants, this regime has received limited focused attention, as coordinating the requisite symmetries within a single band structure is highly nontrivial. Here, we realize such concurrent $\mathbb{Z}$ and $\mathbb{Z}_2$ topology in a two-dimensional acoustic semimetal, revealing pseudospin-selective topological bulk modes arising from the interplay between these distinct invariants, termed helical anomaly bulk states (HABSs). Our lattice design exploits layer, sublattice, and gauge-staggered degrees of freedom, enabling modular control of effective time-reversal, chiral, and particle-hole symmetries. The resulting system exhibits bulk degeneracies protected by an integer $\mathbb{Z}$ invariant and Kramers-like boundary degeneracies protected by a $\mathbb{Z}_2$ invariant, forming twisted boundary arcs. Crucially, finite-size systems manifest helical or anti-helical edge states coexisting with HABSs, a distinctive signature absent in single-invariant topologies. Our results establish HABSs as a characteristic signature of concurrent topology and demonstrate a new regime where bulk and boundary invariants jointly govern topological transport beyond conventional single-class frameworks.

</details>

**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper develops a general, high-order mathematical framework to analyze Turing bifurcations in complex $n$-component reaction-diffusion systems. It derives the amplitude equation and calculates coefficients up to the fifth order, allowing researchers to precisely map out where the nature of the instability changes (codimension-two points). The method is highly computational, providing robust tools for analyzing pattern formation in nonlinear physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical reaction-diffusion systems, the mathematical machinery of deriving amplitude equations and analyzing codimension-two points is highly relevant to the stability analysis of non-equilibrium quantum systems or pattern formation in quantum field theories.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper derives general expressions for the amplitude equation (normal form) valid at a Turing bifurcation for $n$-component reaction-diffusion systems in one spatial dimension.

**Main result.** General expressions for the normal form coefficients up to the fifth order are derived, enabling the systematic detection and analysis of codimension-two points where the bifurcation criticality changes.

**Method.** The method involves an order-by-order expansion (Taylor series) of the system's nonlinear terms, leading to an amplitude equation whose coefficients determine the bifurcation type and higher-order stability.

**Model / system.** The study concerns $n$-component reaction-diffusion systems governed by $\partial u/\partial t = f(u, \epsilon) + D(\epsilon) \partial^2 u/\partial x^2$. Examples include the Schnakenberg model and four-component systems.

**Key observables.** The normal form coefficients ($C_{1,1}, C_3, C_5$) determine the bifurcation criticality; the sign of $C_3$ distinguishes between subcritical and supercritical bifurcations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The bifurcation parameter $\epsilon$ (or $\epsilon^*$), the wavenumber $\hat{k}$, and the order of the expansion (up to fifth order).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the bifurcation occurs at $\epsilon=0$ and is valid for periodic or homogeneous Neumann boundary conditions; the derivation relies on solvability conditions from multiple scales analysis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the bifurcation diagram for models like the Schnakenberg system, showing Turing bifurcation curves colored by the sign of $C_3$, and marking codimension-two points.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives the normal form coefficients ($C_3, C_5$) using solvability conditions, presents the resulting amplitude equation, and validates the method computationally using Python and Mathematica to trace bifurcation curves and critical points.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

General expressions are derived for the amplitude equation valid at a Turing bifurcation of a system of reaction-diffusion equations in one spatial dimension, with an arbitrary number of components. The normal form is computed up to fifth order, which enables the detection and analysis of codimension-two points where the criticality of the bifurcation changes. The expressions are implemented within a Python package, in which the user needs to specify only expressions for the reaction kinetics and the values of diffusion constants. The code is augmented with a Mathematica routine to compute curves of Turing bifurcations in a parameter plane and automatically detect codimension-two points. The software is illustrated with examples that show the versatility of the method including a case with cross-diffusion, a higher-order scalar equation, and a four-component system.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper develops comprehensive mathematical tools to analyze wave bifurcations in complex reaction-diffusion systems. It derives the weakly nonlinear normal form equations up to the fifth order, providing explicit formulas for the coefficients that determine whether the resulting waves are supercritical or subcritical. This work significantly advances the mathematical understanding of pattern formation in non-equilibrium physical models.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical reaction-diffusion systems, the rigorous mathematical machinery of deriving high-order normal forms and analyzing pattern formation stability is highly relevant to understanding the dynamics of open quantum systems or complex many-body dynamics where pattern formation or phase transitions occur.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to derive general amplitude equations for wave bifurcations in reaction-diffusion systems, which are counterparts to Turing instabilities.

**Main result.** Complete formulae for the weakly nonlinear normal form coefficients up to order five are derived for both standing and rotating wave solutions in arbitrary n-component systems.

**Method.** The analysis employs a multi-scale asymptotic methodology, extending normal-form analysis from Hopf and Turing bifurcations to derive amplitude equations for the system's modes.

**Model / system.** The study concerns general n-component reaction-diffusion systems ($\partial_t u = f(u, \mu) + D(\mu) \partial_{xx} u$) exhibiting wave bifurcations, which require at least three components.

**Key observables.** The coefficients of the weakly nonlinear normal form (e.g., $C[\cdot]$) and the resulting bifurcation diagrams characterizing the stability and type (super/subcritical) of standing and travelling waves.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of components ($n \geq 3$), the bifurcation parameter ($\mu$), and the spatial/temporal wave number ($k^*$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the existence of a stable, isolated homogeneous steady state and relies on non-degeneracy conditions to perform the asymptotic expansions.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the difference between dispersion relations for Turing (real eigenvalue) and wave (pure imaginary pair) bifurcations. Figure 2 summarizes the parameter space region showing the bifurcation type (SW/TW) based on cubic coefficients.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the general framework for wave bifurcations, then deriving the normal form equations up to fifth order, and finally analyzing the coefficients to determine the precise nature (supercritical vs. subcritical) of the resulting wave patterns.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

A wave bifurcation is the counterpart to a Turing instability in reaction-diffusion systems, but where the critical wavenumber corresponds to a pure imaginary pair rather than a zero temporal eigenvalue. Such bifurcations require at least three components and give rise to patterns that are periodic in both space and time. Depending on boundary conditions, these patterns can comprise either rotating or standing waves. Restricting to systems in one spatial dimension, complete formulae are derived for the evaluation of the coefficients of the weakly nonlinear normal form of the bifurcation up to order five, including those that determine the criticality of both rotating and standing waves. The formulae apply to arbitrary $n$-component systems ($n\geq 3$) and their evaluation is implemented in software which is made available as supplementary material. The theory is illustrated on two different versions of three-component reaction-diffusion models of excitable media that were previously shown to feature super- and subcritical wave instabilities and on a five-component model of two-layer chemical reaction. In each case, two-parameter bifurcation diagrams are produced to illustrate the connection between complex dispersion relations and different types of Hopf, Turing, and wave bifurcations, including the existence of several codimension-two bifurcations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details a shift-register synchronization mechanism for dual-wavelength pulse trains in nonlinear optical resonators. It shows that parametric energy exchange can stabilize this synchronization even when group-velocity mismatch is significant. This mechanism offers a pathway toward advanced photonic applications like frequency combs and optical memories.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges nonlinear optics with concepts of discrete state transitions (like shift registers), providing a robust mechanism for controlling complex wave patterns in optical cavities, which has implications for ultrafast photonics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper introduces and demonstrates a novel shift-register synchronization mechanism for dual-wavelength pulse trains propagating in resonators with quadratic nonlinearity.

**Main result.** The synchronization is achieved not through conventional velocity locking, but via continuous parametric energy exchange that modifies the effective walk-off, allowing for deterministic discrete pulse shifts after each cavity round trip.

**Method.** The analysis combines coupled-mode equations with the Ikeda-map formalism and employs variational methods to derive reduced dynamical systems for the pulse envelopes.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a doubly resonant degenerate $\chi^{(2)}$ Optical Parametric Oscillator (OPO) in a resonator, governed by coupled nonlinear wave equations.

**Key observables.** Intracavity signal and pump powers, number of pulses ($N$), pulse spacing ($T$), and the effective walk-off ($u_{eff}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Group-velocity mismatch ($u$), nonlinear coupling ($\kappa$), and the ratio of round-trip times to pulse periods ($M/N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis often assumes the neglect of chromatic-dispersion terms and treats the pulse trains as periodic with well-separated pulses for variational reduction.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the concept of synchronization via temporal displacement, show synchronization domains in parameter space, and depict representative stationary shift-register states for varying pulse numbers.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical system and the synchronization concept, deriving the necessary coupled equations and conservation laws, and finally applying variational and numerical techniques to confirm the nonlinear modification of the walk-off condition.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Self-organization of nonlinear waves underpins phenomena ranging from hydrodynamic pattern formation to optical frequency-comb generation. We introduce and numerically demonstrate a shift-register synchronization mechanism for pulse trains in quadratic nonlinear resonators. The synchronized state persists despite substantial group-velocity mismatch through continuous parametric energy exchange, resulting in deterministic discrete pulse shifts after each cavity round trip. Nonlinear parametric interaction modifies the effective walk-off, allowing the pulse trains to satisfy the shift-register synchronization conditions. The proposed mechanism reveals a previously unexplored synchronization regime in quadratic resonators and provides a route toward multicolour frequency combs, optical memories, and ultrafast photonic information processing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper provides a comprehensive mathematical theory for determining when stable chemical or biological steady states in reaction-diffusion systems become unstable due to diffusion. It rigorously derives necessary and sufficient conditions for both Turing (stationary pattern) and wave (traveling wave) instabilities. The work is significant for modeling pattern formation in fields ranging from biology to chemical kinetics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While the subject matter is far from quantum physics, the rigorous mathematical framework of stability analysis, bifurcation theory, and pattern formation in non-linear PDEs is foundational to many areas of theoretical physics, including quantum field theory and non-equilibrium statistical mechanics.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide general necessary and sufficient mathematical conditions for the onset of diffusion-driven instabilities, specifically Turing or wave instabilities, in reaction-diffusion systems.

**Main result.** The authors establish general criteria for both Turing (real eigenvalue) and wave (imaginary eigenvalue) bifurcations, providing a constructive method for selecting diffusion constants that induce instability.

**Method.** The analysis relies on linear stability theory, examining the dispersion relation $\lambda(k)$ derived from the Jacobian matrix and the diffusion operator. Key techniques involve analyzing limits of the wavenumber $k$ and using matrix determinant properties.

**Model / system.** The core model is the reaction-diffusion system $u_t = f(u) + D 
abla^2 u$, where $u$ is the state vector, $f$ is the reaction kinetics, and $D$ is a diagonal diffusion matrix. Examples include malaria dispersion models and coupled Brusselator systems.

**Key observables.** The real part of the eigenvalue $	ext{Re}(\lambda(k))$ as a function of the wavenumber $k$, which determines stability ($	ext{Re}(\lambda) > 0$ indicates instability).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Diffusion coefficients ($D_i$), system parameters (e.g., $a, b, c, \eta$), and the wavenumber $k$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis primarily assumes diagonal diffusion matrices and is based on linear stability analysis around a homogeneous steady state. Extensions to cross-diffusion or non-sinusoidal modes are noted as future work.

**Figures summary.** Figures sketch the dispersion relation $	ext{Re}(\lambda)$ vs. $k^2$, illustrating regimes of stability, the precise point of bifurcation onset, and the range of unstable wavenumbers ($k_1 < k < k_2$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops theory by first establishing general conditions using matrix properties (Lemmas 1-5) for Turing bifurcations, and then applies this framework to specific physical models (like the FitzHugh-Nagumo or Brusselator) to illustrate both Turing and wave instability mechanisms.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

Necessary and sufficient conditions are provided for a diffusion-driven instability of a stable equilibrium of a reaction-diffusion system with $n$ components and a diagonal diffusion matrix. These can be either Turing or wave instabilities. Known necessary and sufficient conditions are reproduced for there to exist diffusion rates that cause a Turing bifurcation of a stable homogeneous state in the absence of diffusion. The method of proof here though, which is based on a study of dispersion relations in the contrasting limits in which the wavenumber tends to zero and to $\infty$, gives a constructive method for choosing diffusion constants. The results are illustrated on a model for the dispersion of malaria, a 3-component FitzHugh-Nagumo-like model proposed to study excitable wavetrains, and for two different coupled Brusselator systems with 4 components

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a high-precision computational framework to analyze the spectral stability of solitons in the discrete nonlinear Schrödinger equation. By employing multiprecision arithmetic, the authors accurately resolve exponentially small eigenvalues, determining that onsite bright solitons are stable while other configurations are unstable. This highlights the computational challenges in studying nonlinear wave dynamics near continuum limits.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous computational techniques developed for handling exponentially small eigenvalues in nonlinear lattice systems are highly relevant for analyzing stability in quantum optics and many-body systems where continuum limits are approached.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the spectral stability of bright and dark solitons governed by the discrete nonlinear Schrödinger (DNLS) equation.

**Main result.** Onsite bright solitons are found to be spectrally stable, while intersite bright and all dark solitons are unstable. The study demonstrates the necessity of multiprecision arithmetic to resolve exponentially small eigenvalues.

**Method.** A computational framework combining multiprecision arithmetic, an exact Jacobian for the stationary problem, and a squared-operator formulation is developed for spectral analysis.

**Model / system.** The primary model is the one-dimensional DNLS equation, which describes nonlinear wave phenomena in discrete lattices. The continuous limit ($h 	o 0$) corresponds to the standard NLS equation.

**Key observables.** Spectral eigenvalues ($\lambda$) determining stability, and the profiles of onsite/intersite bright and dark solitons.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lattice spacing ($h$), nonlinearity parameter ($\sigma$), and the required working precision (MP).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that standard double precision fails to resolve the stability eigenvalues, necessitating high-precision computation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the profiles and spectra for bright solitons (onsite/intersite) and dark solitons, confirming asymptotic predictions for bright solitons.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the DNLS model and the standing wave ansatz. It then details the numerical methods, emphasizing the use of multiprecision arithmetic to solve the nonlinear system and compute the spectrum via the squared-operator formulation.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the spectral stability of bright and dark solitons in the discrete nonlinear Schrödinger (DNLS) equation using multiprecision arithmetic. The eigenvalues governing stability are exponentially small in the lattice spacing and cannot be resolved with standard double precision. To address this, we develop a computational framework combining multiprecision arithmetic, an exact Jacobian for the stationary problem, and a squared-operator formulation for spectral analysis. This enables accurate resolution of exponentially small eigenvalues and direct comparison with exponential-asymptotic predictions. Our results show that onsite bright solitons are spectrally stable, whereas intersite bright solitons and both onsite and intersite dark solitons are unstable. Bright solitons require only a few eigenvalues and allow efficient large-scale computations, while dark solitons demand higher precision due to their proximity to the continuous spectrum. Simulations up to \(N=65{,}250\) grid points (31.7 GB RAM) highlight the necessity of multiprecision arithmetic for capturing beyond-all-orders spectral effects.

</details>

**Summary.** This work provides direct spectroscopic evidence that monolayer SnTe on graphene adopts a puckered electronic structure, contradicting simpler square-flat models. By combining ARPES with DFT, the authors quantify the substrate's influence, showing graphene renormalizes the material's structural buckling amplitude. This establishes a crucial understanding of interface-stabilized 2D electronic properties.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state materials, the detailed understanding of orbital hybridization, spin-orbit coupling effects, and the emergence of quantum confinement/interface effects in 2D materials is highly relevant to designing novel quantum electronic components.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to directly observe and determine the electronic structure of monolayer SnTe films grown on graphene, specifically resolving whether the structure is puckered or idealized as square-flat.

**Main result.** The electronic structure is confirmed to be consistent with a puckered even-layer biatomic-layer building block, and graphene actively modifies this structure by renormalizing the buckling amplitude.

**Method.** The research combines Angle-Resolved Photoemission Spectroscopy (ARPES) measurements with Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations, supplemented by LEED and momentum microscopy.

**Model / system.** The system involves SnTe films (monolayer to multilayer) grown epitaxially on graphene/SiC substrates. The theoretical modeling compares puckered vs. non-puckered structural models (1UC/2UC) to reproduce experimental electronic features.

**Key observables.** Electronic band dispersion, polarization dependence, valence-band width, and the substrate-renormalized buckling amplitude ($\Delta z$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The monolayer limit is characterized by a specific, reduced buckling amplitude ($\Delta z = 0.13$ Å), which is crucial for matching the observed valence-band width.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the assumption that the observed electronic structure is best described by the puckered model, and that the DFT calculations accurately capture the substrate-induced renormalization effects.

**Figures summary.** Figures show LEED patterns confirming domain structure, momentum-space maps illustrating hole pockets, and ARPES spectra demonstrating band dispersion that matches DFT calculations for the puckered structure.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the necessity of the puckered model over the flat model, then using ARPES/momentum microscopy to characterize the monolayer state, and finally comparing this to multilayer films to track thickness evolution toward a bulk-like state.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report a direct observation of the electronic structure of monolayer SnTe on graphene by angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy. By combining low-energy electron diffraction, momentum microscopy, core-level spectroscopy, and first-principles calculations, we show that the observed monolayer-limit electronic structure is consistent with a puckered even-layer biatomic-layer building block rather than a nonpuckered square-flat structure. The observed band dispersion and its polarization dependence are reproduced by a puckered structure with a substrate-renormalized buckling amplitude. We further show that graphene actively interacts with ultrathin SnTe by selecting its in-plane orientational texture, renormalizing the buckling amplitude, and modifying the local interfacial electrostatic environment in the monolayer limit. In comparison, multilayer SnTe exhibits a more ringlike momentum-space distribution, interlayer-split valence bands, and a stronger p-type character, revealing the onset of thickness evolution from an interface-stabilized puckered ultrathin limit toward a more bulklike state.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes noncompact gauge theories on a lattice to study the effective lattice spacing ratio, R. Using perturbative methods, the authors find that R is bounded, reaching a maximum of sqrt(2) for pure gauge theories. The inclusion of matter fields modifies this ratio, leading to constraints on the allowed difference between scalar and spinor degrees of freedom.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on lattice gauge theory, the rigorous derivation of bounds on field content and the study of effective parameters in a lattice regularization scheme are fundamental theoretical tools applicable to many-body physics models.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the effective lattice spacing ratio (R) in noncompact gauge theories, determining how this ratio behaves when considering the inclusion of matter fields and establishing bounds on the number of matter fields.

**Main result.** For pure SU(2) and Abelian theories, the ratio R is found to increase up to a maximum value of sqrt(2). Including matter fields causes R to either increase or decrease, depending on the difference between scalar and spinor degrees of freedom, subject to specific bounds.

**Method.** The analysis employs perturbative evaluation of the effective lattice spacing using an expansion in the Planck constant, alongside functional methods to determine the effective potential.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on noncompact gauge theories formulated on a lattice, specifically examining pure SU(2) and Abelian gauge theories, and extending the analysis to include scalar ($\phi$) and spinor ($\psi$) matter fields.

**Key observables.** The ratio R of effective non-compact to compact lattice spacing, and the effective potential V.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gauge coupling constant (g), Planck constant (hbar), number of scalar ($
u_s$) and spinor ($
u_f$) degrees of freedom, and the parameter $\sigma$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is perturbative and restricted to $N_c=2$ for SU(2), with limitations noted regarding comparison to the Veneziano limit or asymptotic safety.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from analyzing the pure gauge theory in the continuum limit, deriving the effective potential, and then incorporating matter fields to derive bounds on the degrees of freedom and the behavior of the ratio R in different dimensions (4D vs 3D).

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

The work was motivated by the numerical result that in a pure SU(2) gauge theory the ratio R of the effective non-compact and compact lattice spacing is larger than 1 and increasing with decreasing gauge coupling, as well as the expectation that it should further increase extending the parameter space. This means that with a noncompact regularization, at given number of lattice sites and comparable scaling, one can obtain a larger physical volume, whose importance for the control of size effects has long been known. We confirm qualitatively results and expectation by a perturbative evaluation of the effective lattice spacing in an expansion in the Plank constant of non-compact pure SU(2) and Abelian gauge theories, but we find in addition that R reaches the maximum value of sqrt(2). Including matter fields we find that R increases ( still up to sqrt(2) ) or decreases depending on the difference between the number of scalar and spinor degrees of freedom, and there are bounds on such a difference.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a comprehensive theory for understanding how excitons align and orient themselves in semiconductor nanoplatelets. By modeling the coupling between bright and dark exciton states using a pseudospin formalism, the authors predict polarization signatures observable in photoluminescence. This work is crucial for controlling and characterizing spin-related quantum phenomena in solid-state nanostructures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant to quantum optics and open quantum systems because it models spin dynamics and polarization transfer in solid-state quantum emitters (excitons) under external fields, which is a core topic in quantum light sources.

<details><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper develops a theoretical framework to describe the optical alignment and orientation effects of excitons within single and ensemble semiconductor nanoplatelets (NPLs). It specifically models the interplay between bright and dark exciton states.

**Main result.** The theory predicts distinct symmetries for the optical response ($C_2$ for a single NPL and $C_{\infty}$ for the ensemble) and shows how bright-to-dark exciton coupling allows for the observation of dark exciton optical alignment.

**Method.** The authors use a density matrix formalism applied to two interacting pseudospins (bright and dark excitons) to calculate time-dependent and steady-state polarized photoluminescence.

**Model / system.** The system is semiconductor nanoplatelets (e.g., CdSe/CdS core/shell) whose excitons are modeled using a pseudospin formalism. The Hamiltonian accounts for exchange interactions and Zeeman splitting, while the polarization is analyzed using Stokes parameters.

**Key observables.** Polarized photoluminescence (PL), Stokes parameters ($P_l, P_{l'}, P_c$), and the degree of exciton optical alignment/orientation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperature regime, Faraday magnetic field, and the strength of the electron-hole exchange interaction.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes an isotropic distribution of in-plane NPL edge orientations in the ensemble and treats the system in the linear optical response regime.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the geometry of NPLs (single and ensemble), the axes used for polarization analysis, and the energy splitting/pseudospin evolution in a magnetic field.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the physical system and the need for the theory, developing the pseudospin formalism for bright/dark excitons, deriving the polarization response equations, and finally applying these to analyze specific effects like alignment and ensemble averaging.

</details>

<details><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present the theory of exciton optical alignment and optical orientation effects in a single semiconductor nanoplatelet (NPL) and in an ensemble of NPLs. The theoretical model is developed to take into account the emission from both bright and dark exciton states in the case of strong electron-hole exchange interaction and an isotropic distribution of in-plane orientations of the NPLs' edges with respect to the linear polarization axis. The conditions for the linear optical response regime on the polarized resonant excitation of the bright exciton are considered, and the steady-state and time-dependent expressions for polarized photoluminescence are presented for the low temperature case in the Faraday magnetic field at normal incidence. The symmetry of the obtained optical response corresponds to the $C_{2}$ point group for the single NPL and the $C_{\infty}$ point group for the NPL ensemble. The bright-to-dark exciton coupling mechanism allowing one to observe the optical alignment of the dark exciton is considered. The example calculations of the effects are presented for the parameters close to those of CdSe/CdS core/shell NPLs. The effect of the fluctuations in the exciton parameters in the ensemble on the polarization dephasing in the transverse effective magnetic field is demonstrated.

</details>
